The Mystery of the Human Bookcase CHAPTER I THE AFFAIR AT THE ABERCORN ARMS 

It was a raw March evening, and a damp wind, the marrow-searching variety that Robert Louis Stevenson calls "snell", met Cameron and Bland as they turned the corner into Twentieth Street. For the 

Sagamore is in the neighbourhood of Gramercy Park. 

If not the richest, it is certainly one of the oldest and most exclusive clubs, its membership roll reading like a page from the Almanach de Gotha. "B-r-r!" said Cameron as he turned up his overcoat collar. "Not exactly the mistral, Jerry. Would that my bones were covered like yours and that I looked like a motor-tyre ad." "I'd rather look like a motor-tyre than a wish-bone," retorted Bland. "France has made your blood thin, and your wit too. I wish you'd quit harping on my figure, I'm not really fat, only properly nourished."

These two were privileged to say impolite things to each other, for they had been boyhood friends and then classmates before, following a long separation, they met again in Centre Street as the paid protectors of society. Both were still young, perhaps 

II

thirty-five or so, as youth goes in the positions they occupied; and both were experiments, so to speak, of the new municipal administration. 

Certainly Kirker Cameron was something novel in the way of a New York Commissioner of Police. From a Franco-American mother he inherited a Gallic temperament that clashed with the equally temperamental blood of his father's Scottish ancestors; and, intended originally for one of the professions, he elected to go abroad and study the science of modern criminology. Speaking French like a native, and having an uncle who was an influential member of the Chamber of Deputies, possessing the keenest of minds and a histrionic talent that only fell short of genius, he became identified with that historic building on the Quai d'Horloge, which is known familiarly as the Sûreté. 

There he remained for over a dozen years learning the A.B.C. of crime detection and police organization from the world's acknowledged masters. He climbed high, co-operating with Scotland Yard and the Continental police in many an international case, although his connection with the Sûreté Generale was known to but few. 

Meanwhile Jerome Bland, since leaving the Columbia 

University law school, had done some climbing in New

York, and when the new administration came in he was appointed District Attorney. Cameron's friendship with Bland removed the ancient friction existing between the District Attorney's office and the police; instead of hampering each other, they co-operated with the fullest sympathy and understanding, which is not to say that a certain good-natured rivalry no longer existed, or that Cameron was given to telling 

Bland all he might think or know about a case. For

THE AFFAIR AT THE ABERCORN ARMS 

13 both were men of decided opinions which often were diametrically opposed. "The Abercorn Arms-where and what is it?" said the District Attorney, as his piano-like legs sought to keep step with the Police Commissioner's long lean ones. "How do you know we're heading right?

The 'next street' may mean a mile off. It generally does." "I'm grieved at your lack of observation, Jerry.

It's that apartment house on the next corner and you've seen it every time you visited the Sagamore, which is · about every day." "I never come up that way-well, not often. And whoever sees the names of buildings?" "Practically nobody but letter-carriers," said Cameron, "because very few people observe anything much above eye-level. If the name Abercorn Arms were cut in the sidewalk, instead of over the door, you'd have noticed it the first time you passed." "I admit I haven't your eyesight, if I don't need glasses like you," chuckled Bland, knowing the bogus quality of that supposed need. "Oh, it's not a case of eyesight, but training. I've been taught to look where others don't. But I can take no credit in this instance, for I happen to know this particular building rather well. It houses 'Ye Olde Antique Shoppe', and you know one of my many failings.' 

They received the first authentic details, little enough, of the mysterious affair, from a patrolman who was posted outside the ornate entrance on the side street, though most of the building gave on the avenue where the ground floor was occupied by several high-class stores like the one mentioned by Cameron.

"Man by the name of Devore, sir," explained the officer who, at first, had failed to recognize the couple. "Ground floor west," thumbing at two windows behind the iron railing. "When and how did you hear of it?" asked Cameron. "About fifteen or twenty minutes ago, sir. The hall-boy called me in. There's a doctor from up the street and a precinct man inside. I got orders to stay here and let nobody out." "Carry on," said Cameron, "till we see if it's a Headquarters case."

In the entrance hall they were met by a coloured youth in a grey braided uniform, while a small mixed group stood at the far end near the elevator. They were talking in appropriately hushed tones; no doubt tenants who had heard the news and wished to hear more.

A sallow, sleek-haired man detached himself from this group and approached Cameron and Bland, who were at verbal grips with the sceptical, good-looking mulatto. Evidently the hall-boy had his own ideas as to what authority should look like, especially a 

Commissioner of Police. Cameron, with his hornrimmed spectacles and "student's stoop", was not impressive. "I'm the superintendent," said the newcomer, as he waved the hall-boy aside, and he looked properly impressed on learning the visitors' identity. His name, it developed, was Donald Macford and he seemed a pleasant and efficient individual, though now looking worried and upset. "I'm very glad you gentlemen have come, and yet sorry," he said. "I was hoping it wouldn't turn out

THE AFFAIR AT THE ABERCORN ARMS 

15 your sort of case. Of course it's awfully bad for the house." "Oh, we aren't here officially-yet," reassured 

Cameron. "It mayn't need our attention. You've formed no conclusion yourself?" 

Macford shrugged and spread his hands. "I'd say it was natural causes, heart failure or something, only for the way he looks and if I hadn't been talking with him shortly before it happened. He seemed then as right as rain. But maybe they've found out something by this time," he added. "They asked me to see that nobody left the premises; why, I don't know.” "Hardly a useful precaution, I think, considering the many exits in a place like this," commented 

Cameron. "Was Mr. Devore married?" "No, not to my knowledge," replied the superintendent, as he led the way to a door on the left. "He lived alone. A bachelor apartment. We rent them furnished." "I understand he was found dead," said Cameron. "By whom ?" "The janitor." "And who was the last to see him alive?" queried the District Attorney. "I was, so far as I know," replied Macford, as he turned the handle and opened the door of the apartment.

It was a small flat. There were only four rooms, including a tiny kitchenette and a still smaller bathroom. These were en suite. There was not even a foyer hall, the entrance door opening into the livingroom, which contained the two windows facing the street. Behind this room, which could be shut off by sliding doors and portieres, was a bedroom with a single

window giving on the at shaft. Then came the kitchenette and bathroom, each with its muffed window facing the same court.

The large room on which Macford opened the door was luxuriously furnished, but, as might be expected, in a conventional manner. In the centre there stood a large, flat-topped black desk, probably imitation Jacobean, and a commonplace high-backed chair. The carved lion's head topping the leather back was crude and conventional, the leonine snarl or yawn looking more like a fatuous grin. The room was brilliantly illuminated, a green-shaded reading-lamp on the desk being switched on as well as the three incandescents hanging over the desk.

No one was visible until, glancing to the right, Cameron saw the adjoining room. This also was brilliantly lighted, the sliding doors and portieres being half open, and they entered to meet two puzzled and rather frightened-looking men.

Keppel, the precinct detective, was a large and shapeless person who reminded Cameron of Fafner in the Rhinegold. Capable enough, no doubt, in precinct routine work, but patently no genius. Golding, “the doctor from up the street", looked clever, but not too experienced. He was young, fashionably dressed, handsome.

Both these men had stamped on their faces, otherwise so different, the same expression, tense and wary, as though half expecting a ghostly hand to be laid on them, or some hidden menace to appear suddenly.

The young doctor was quite pale and he sat on the end of the bed with his accurately creased legs drawn up. 

Keppel, who still wore overcoat and derby-the rule is that an officer must always have his hands free-

THE AFFAIR AT THE ABER CORN ARMS 

17 looked as though he had been groelling on the floor. 

Most incongruous of all for a New York detective, he carried a light Malacca cane with an ornamental handle. 

Cameron was intrigued. Fond of the bizarre and outré, he had doubted very much if this case, despite its alleged mysteriousness, should be able to furnish either. But decidedly it now held a promise.

CHAPTER II  

"WATCH out!” cried Keppel. "There's a snake loose around here. A killer. I guess mebbe it's in them porteers." 

At the words, Dr. Golding jerked his neat legs farther up on the bed, if possible, while Macford gave a gasp, whirled about and began to strike viciously at nothing. "It's only the cord," said Cameron, who had seen what his two companions had not, proof of his recent contention that few people observe anything much above eye level. A decorative cord hung half hidden in a fold of the portieres, and its tassel had brushed against the superintendent's ear. 

Macford laughed without mirth and looked foolish. "I'm scared of snakes," he said. "So am I-dead scared," nodded the dapper man on the bed, with a look of brotherhood. "Even the most harmless give me the willies only to look at.” "No doubt it was the cord you saw, if you saw anything," remarked Cameron, looking without favour at the precinct detective. "Or have you been drinking? 

What are you doing with that absurd cane?'' 

Keppel, having now recognized the "Comish", furtively dusted the knees of his trousers and drew himself erect, looking like a badly-stuffed bolster. "It's better than a gun, sir, for rep-tiles," he said 18

with a sheepish look. "I thought it might be in them porteers, because I been lookin' every place else. It's just killed the party on the bed." "Did you see it?" "No, sir, we didn't see it, but it done it." to

Obviously the party on the bed did not refer to 

Dr. Golding, who now hastened to introduce himself; but to the very late Mr. Devore, of whose presence Cameron was fully aware. 

Macford leaned over the foot of the bed. "And he was such a good-looking man," he said softly. 

Decidedly Henry Devore was no longer good to look upon. His face was mottled, swollen, stamped with all the insignia of a horrible and painful death. 

He was a tall man, slender, but well made. He had a clever brow, a determined chin, and his age might be somewhere in the forties. Immaculately and rather foppishly dressed. "The poor one!" Cameron, when his emotions were stirred, or he was taken with what Bland termed his Coquelin mood, often slipped into the speech and gestures of his mother's people. "Now, doctor, I invite your opinion. So it is snakebite ?" "I don't see what else it can be," said Golding. "Of course I'm no authority, but it must be a bite of something. Look here." And, after a furtive glance under the bed and at the portieres, he proceeded with all the mechanical callousness of his calling to turn Mr. Devore over on his face.

The neck was greatly swollen, so that it bulged in a hard discoloured, repulsive roll over the wing collar, which had been unfastened, and the young doctor pointed out two marks in the nape of the neck. These were opposite each other and perhaps an inch apart.

"That certainly looks like snakebite," said the

District Attorney, and, for the first time, began to glance uneasily about the room. "The question is how did it get in here? Mr. Devore wasn't given to keeping such pets?" he added, turning to the superintendent. "What was his business ?" "I don't know," replied Macford. "I don't know anything about him. But as for keeping things like that, it wouldn't be allowed here, of course. And 

I don't know anybody who'd want to keep them, except maybe Professor Vornoff." "Vornoff, yes," nodded Golding. "No, he wouldn't keep any, but he knows everything about the slimy brutes. That's why I asked you to fetch him. Isn't he home yet?" "No," said Macford. "Well, in the meantime I notified the Chief Medical 

Examiner," added Golding. "Of course it's an accident, but there'll have to be an inquest." "And I 'phoned to the house, sir," said Keppel to 

Cameron. "They've notified Headquarters.' 

"Very good," approved the Police Commissioner, and looked at the precinct detective as a teacher might look at an unexpectedly bright pupil. "So you don't think it's an accident?" "Well, sir, somebody might have put the rep-tile in here to do the job. It wouldn't be the first time a trick like that was worked." "Quite so," nodded Cameron. But no longer did he regard the bright pupil with the same interest. "Crime, like history, has a way of repeating itself, and there's nothing new under the sun-especially that last observation. An improvement on old methods, yes, but that is all. The criminal mind is singularly

unoriginal, and Cleopatra, with the mythical smuggled asp, has supplied the inspiration to many a murderer." "Not in New York," said Bland drily, glancing at his friend with some suspicion. Rounded periods meant generally that Cameron had something up his sleeve which he might, or might not, choose to disclose. "Deadly snakes aren't easily come by." "Quite so," nodded Cameron again. "And so we arrive at the important question: Was it a snake ? 

As I say, there's nothing new, and surely you remember the Carhart murder case? Yes, it was before my time and yours, but it still stands out as one of the great causes célèbres.” 

By "before my time", Cameron meant his connection with Centre Street. The Carhart affair was easily within the memory of the youngest man present, this being Dr. Golding. And in that sensational crime, whose smallest detail had been given to the world, actual snake venom had been used. "By George, I never thought of that!" exclaimed 

Golding, plucking at his inadequate moustache. "But, look here, wasn't there only one puncture in the 

Carhart case? I can't remember, but it seems to me there was." "No, there were two, just like this," spoke up Macford, pointing to the figure on the bed. "They thought it snakebite at first. I remember that part of it." "Doctor Golding's memory is better than yours," remarked Cameron. "There was only one puncture, and so the snake theory was out. But, as I say, there are new adaptations if not new methods." "By George" ejaculated Golding again, and paused.

"Yes?" encouraged the Police Commissioner. "Well, you know Vornoff-I mean he experiments with serum made from snake venom.' 

"So I'm aware," said Cameron blandly. "I remember reading something about a Professor Vornoff, and then, when Mr. Macford mentioned him, I thought it probably the same. Not a common name. 

Do you know him personally?" "Fairly well," nodded Golding. "You see, he lives here and we're neighbours, apart from being members of the same profession, though Vornoff doesn't practise now. He's a very fine man, and of course I didn't mean to suggest for a moment- "I quite understand," said Cameron, beaming through his glasses. And again Bland gave him that look of suspicion. Cameron, naively cordial or innocent, was a Cameron for a guilty conscience to watch. "It was the mention of Professor Vornoff's name, in conjunction with this poor one," said the Police 

Commissioner, waving a hand of pity at the bed, "that made me think of the Carhart case. An inevitable association of ideas, you see. Probably no more than that. But you haven't seen any snake and, while we wait for the Chief Medical Examiner, I think our first duty is to try and find it." And, turning to the portieres, he shook them vigorously, but without result. "You say you've searched everywhere else," he added, addressing Keppel; "so we may conclude that it has gone, if it was ever here. I see that window is open. Perhaps you posted the patrolman at the steps in order to arrest the rep-tile if it left that way?" 

The precinct detective coughed and grew more

bolsterlike. "I didn't know at the time it was a rep-tile, sir. But I knew it was a murder case “Knew?" And again Cameron's eyes held that look of favourable expectancy. "Well, sir, I thought it likely, and that's why I wanted Headquarters on the job. And I thought it just as well if nobody left the house. There's something in the next room I'd like you to see, sir.”

Now Cameron, although he had merely passed through that room with the brilliant lights and had made no studied scrutiny, was satisfied that he had seen everything visible that there was to be seen. He could have even told Keppel from what corner he had lifted the malacca cane. This eye like the lens of a camera, this memory like a sensitive film, was no God-given miraculous gift, but the logical result of prolonged and intelligent training.

The precinct detective, with an air of restored selfesteem, led the way into the adjoining room and to the Jacobean desk. Two ornamental silver candlesticks flanked a large blotter, on which lay a writingpad, and there was a brass tray containing an impressive collection of pens and pencils. A telephone, an evening paper, a calendar, a few current magazines, a chess-board, and a small stand of books practically completed the desk's equipment.

Keppel opened the writing-pad and brought out a loose sheet of paper on which there was written in pencil the following:

Warning. Red Rum Myers Ides of March. 

"So I thought it looked like a Headquarters' case, sir," said Keppel as Cameron and Bland studied the

strange message. "And when the superintendent here told me there was a tenant by the name of Myer "You never said why you wanted the information," broke in Macford with a look of astonishment and resentment. "And he doesn't spell his name like that.

It's M-e-i-e-r. Mr. Meier can have nothing to do with this business." "I don't tell all I know except to my superior officers," said Keppel with a sniff. "You and the doc didn't see this paper, but I did." "Of course I saw it," retorted the superintendent. "It was there for anybody to see if they made it a business to read private correspondence. "Yes, I noticed it lying there," nodded Golding, "though I didn't stop to examine it." "Lying where?" asked the District Attorney, and both the doctor and superintendent pointed out where the paper had been. They should have thought it some scribble meant for the waste basket if they had given the matter any thought at all. Keppel then said he had placed it in the writing-pad, not wishing to. handle it unnecessarily, and he lost some of his complacency when Cameron remarked that he should have left it where it was.

Golding now put on his overcoat and picked up his oblong bag, saying that he must be going. Of course when his evidence was needed. . . "A moment," said Bland in his District Attorney manner. "Your evidence is necessary now. There are a good many things we don't know yet." "You see, "In fact everything," said Cameron. doctor, we aren't like writers of detective fiction, who know all about their characters from the start. We come to a case knowing absolutely nothing about anybody."

"If you mean" began Golding resentfully, then brought a card from his wallet. "There's my address, and my name's on the Medical Register. You'll have no trouble finding out whatever you want to know about me." "Oh, not about you," said Cameron, raising a deprecating hand. "It's Mr. Devore. The superintendent says he knows nothing about him, but perhaps you do ?" "Why? I wasn't his doctor. They only came for me to-night because I was the nearest one." "Were you personally acquainted with the deceased ?" demanded Bland. And, despite his abundant fat, he could look alarmingly official when so minded. "That's what we want to know." "Well, I was, in a sense," replied Golding. "We're neighbours, you see, and I'd met him more than once. 

But I never visited him.' 

"Not like you visited Professor Vornoff ?" suggested 

Cameron encouragingly. "I haven't much time to visit anybody socially," retorted Golding. "I'm on visiting terms with Vornoff, if that's what you mean, but I never was with Mr. 

Devore. We knew each other, and that's all." 

He glanced at his wrist-watch and said again that they must excuse him. "I've a very important call to make and I can't keep my patient waiting." "Sorry to have detained you," said Cameron. “Just one more question : You arrived here before the precinct detective ?" "Yes." "Was Mr. Devore lying there on the bed?" "Yes, though I understand he wasn't found there, It was in the kitchenette."

"The janitor found him lying on the floor of the kitchenette," explained Macford. "Then, after calling me, we carried Mr. Devore here." "I see," nodded Cameron. "And how long did it take you to get here, doctor?" "Oh, about five minutes, I think. I'm only a few doors away, you know, and I was getting ready to leave on my evening round." "I called him up," explained the superintendent, "while the hall-boy went for a cop. I knew Doctor 

Golding's hours. I don't think more than ten minutes passed from the time the janitor called me until the doctor arrived." "We've been here fifteen minutes," said Bland, glancing at his watch, "and it's now a quarter to eight. That would make the time twenty past seven when the affair was discovered. And when did you last see Mr. Devore alive, Mr. Macford?" 

The superintendent replied that he could not be certain, but it was shortly before seven o'clock, perhaps five minutes or so. "That means," said Cameron, "he died inside half an hour. There aren't many snakes that can do the trick in that time." "Of course a great deal would depend on the physique and constitution of the victim," said Golding. "Mr.

Devore seemed robust and, so far as I can see without an autopsy, he was in good health."

The young doctor now left and Macford was beginning to explain how, presumably, he had been the last person to see Devore alive, when the Chief Medical Examiner arrived, followed by representatives of the 

District Attorney's office and Police Headquarters.

CHAPTER III  

ARTHUR VARCOE, Bland's assistant, who had been on duty when the call came in from Police Headquarters, was accompanied by Detective Maddox, attached to the District Attorney's office; while the Homicide 

Bureau was represented by John J. O'Reilly and 

Detective Joseph Delaven. Not knowing as yet positively if it was a murder case, more especially one that might demand the attention of all their experts, the official photographer and others had not been sent. Yet these men, O'Reilly and Delaven, were two of the most capable cogs in the machine. Needless to say they, like Varcoe and Maddox, were astonished to find the District Attorney and the Commissioner of Police already on the scene.

Dr. Brockelbank, the Chief Medical Examiner, was another important cog who owed his position to merit and not politics. He looked like a college professor, which, indeed, he had been, as he also had been Medical 

Officer for the Port of New York. He knew both 

Cameron and Bland socially as well as officially. "Well, Doctor, here we are again-the little family party of vultures," said Cameron. "Bland and I were just finishing dinner at the club when he heard that there had been a mysterious death here. We dropped in unofficially, but I shouldn't be surprised 27

if our interest lasted some considerable time. A curious case so far. You remember the Carhart one ?" "Not officially," said Brockelbank, as he peeled off his gloves and brought out a pair of nose-glasses. “I hadn't given up my chair of toxicology then. But 

I remember it, certainly. A unique case. Very." "Well, it may have supplied the model for this one," said Cameron, and in a few words put his new audience in possession of the few facts thus far known. "More than masterpieces of music and literature have been plagiarized."

He turned to O'Reilly, a Jesuitical-looking man, small and nervous as Delaven was large and placid, and added: "Go through that desk and see if there's anything bearing on Devore's 

Devore's identity. Nobody seems to know anything about him, and we haven't had time to find out. You'll be careful about fingerprints, of course." "And, Varcoe, you'll see about the janitor," said the District Attorney, as he prepared to follow Brockelbank into the bedroom. "We'll need his evidence. 

And there may be a snake around somewhere. Let

Maddox have a look in that court, and through the house, but don't alarm the tenants unnecessarily." "Let any of them leave if they want to," said 

Cameron. "Keppel, you'd better give Detective 

Maddox a hand." "I can too," volunteered Macford. "No, you're more useful here," said Cameron. "I want you to show me where you found Mr. Devore. 

Come along, Delaven."

This trio then passed through the bedroom into the kitchenette. A camouflaged sink and ice-box (there was no frigid air in the building), an electric stove, a

little built-in sideboard, a small round table, and two chairs this was virtually all that the room contained. 

The only natural illumination came from the muffed window on the court, though now the centre drop-light made everything clearer than day. Keppel, in his hunt for the snake, had turned on every light in the apartment. "He was lying there, face down," declared the superintendent, and pointed to a spot between the end of the table and the open bathroom door. The window was to the left, about three feet distant, and, like the bedroom one, was raised a few inches at the bottom. "Feigel, the janitor, said he found him that way." "Take a look under that bathtub in there, Delaven," said Cameron. "I'm sure Keppel did, but there's no harm having another. I needn't remind you to look carefully overhead-the hotwater-pipes and cistern. 

And remember that the krait, one of the quickest killers of all, is only a foot long or less."

The Commissioner of Police went to the window and examined it closely, as he had done with the one in the bedroom, while Macford, looking as though he wished he were elsewhere, peered futilely under the table. Then Cameron began to make a systematic examination of the room. "What's this?" he asked, pausing before what looked like the door of a small cupboard set midway in the wall near the ice-box. "That's the dumbwaiter, or service lift," explained the superintendent. "It isn't fastened," pointed out Cameron, and opened the door without pulling back the spring catch. "Was it that way when you were here with the janitor ?'''

The superintendent said he had not noticed it, and 

Cameron, after looking into the shaft, asked if Devore had done his own cooking and had all his meals at home. "I don't think he did any cooking except make coffee," replied Macford. "The milkman always left a bottle on the front windowsill-you can reach the nearest window from the steps-the newsboy would stick the morning paper behind it, and then the breadserver would leave a bag of rolls. Mr. Devore wasn't an early riser; he'd bring in the things about nine o'clock and leave about ten. He must have lunched and dined outside." "So that this service lift was practically never used ?'' "Very little, anyway. I'm sure there were no groceries or anything like that delivered. Of course there'd be the garbage-you see it's a pretty old building, and we haven't the latest labour-saving devices-but there couldn't have been much of that. 

And then, I suppose, there'd be the ice-man.”

Delaven now reappeared, having discovered nothing of importance, and Cameron opened the ice-box. 

The upper compartment contained little but some milk, butter, and a small cake of ice; while the lower held nothing but several bottles of Chambertin and 

St. Julien. "Who was his bootlegger?" asked Cameron. "Did he have any visitor called Myer ?" "Not to my knowledge," replied Macford. "The only visitor I knew was a Mr. Garwinn. He came pretty often. If I knew where Mr. Devore got this stuff, I'd be only too glad to tell you." "Look here, sir," said Delaven as he came forward with a box that had been lying hidden from Cameron

on the other side of the radiator. It was perhaps three feet long, made of thin wood, and half full of shavings. Delaven then produced the lid, which bore no name or inscription, and the paper in which it had been wrapped. On the latter was printed in block letters Devore's name and the Abercorn Arms address. "I don't see any stamps or express company label,” said Cameron. "Yet why should it have an address, if Devore brought it himself?" "He didn't bring it," said the superintendent. "It came this evening, and I gave it to Mr. Devore myself. That's what I was going to tell you about, how I happened to be the last one to see him alive. I know it must be the same box because I remember the address being printed like that." "And when did it come? Who delivered it ?" "I don't know. The hall-boy gave it to me about half-past six. He said he'd found it in his place."

This "place", the superintendent explained, was an alcove in the entrance hall about midway between the door and the elevator. It contained the telephone switchboard, a small table, and two chairs, where visitors might wait while their names were sent up. "How many hall-boys are there?" asked Cameron. 

Macford said there was only one during the day or at night, and the night one left at about two o'clock, when the elevator, which was not the automatic kind, stopped running. "So the hall would be vacant if the boy was on the elevator?" asked Cameron. "Yes, unless the janitor or I happened to be about.

But there's never much elevator service except in the morning and evening.” "Is it usual for parcels to be left there ?"

"Yes, if they don't require any receipt they're left on the table in the alcove, if the tenant's out and the hall-boy isn't there. At least I've seen it done more than once." "You live here, I suppose ?" "Oh, yes. My flat's just across the hall from this one." "Does anyone live with you?” "No, I'm not married and I've no relatives." "Had you any idea what might be in this box? 

How heavy do you think it was?" 

The superintendent pondered, but could give no decided answer. Perhaps a few pounds. Certainly heavier than drygoods. "It might have been some of them, sir," suggested 

Delaven, designating the collection of bottles. "You know they're always packed in straw, if not shavings. 

They'd take extra care with the packing to keep them from clinking." "Take charge of the box and the wrapper," said Cameron.

They now returned to the bedroom, where they found Dr. Brockelbank, his examination finished, talking to the District Attorney. There was a look of doubt and perplexity on his clever old face. "This is rather beyond me, Cameron, as I've been telling Bland," he said. "Snakebite, yes, to all intents, but what kind? I don't know. For that matter, although science knows what snake venom is in a chemical sense, and even what it tastes like, it doesn't understand properly its primary action." "Have you had any experience with cases of snakebite ?" "Well, I must confess that my practical knowledge

goes no farther than two or three cases of people bitten by copperheads-you can still find copperheads over in New Jersey-but though highly poisonous, they're nothing to some. Of course the most venomous we've got is the diamond-back rattler, and then there's the cotton-mouth moccasin. But, unless the circumstances are exceptional, they can't kill in so short a time." "I think you could count on the fingers of one hand the snakes that can," said Cameron. "A good deal of nonsense has been written about the deadliness of most snakes. Permanganate of potash, if not whisky or a good antitoxin, will fix most cases if taken in time." 

Brockelbank agreed, nor did he show any surprise at the Police Commissioner's knowledge. He knew there were few subjects on which Cameron could not speak with more or less authority. Then he added, like Dr. Golding, that naturally much would depend on the victim's physical condition, while some people were constitutionally more susceptible to poison than others. The venom of all snakes was not alike, nor did even identical venom always act precisely the same on any two people. And in this case, the wound being so near the great arterial trunks that carry blood to the brain, it would have its effect quickly on those centres. There would be paralysis and then convulsions. "That's what I couldn't understand," said the 

District Attorney. "I mean you'd think anybody being bitten would raise the roof. He'd shout or run out into the hall or something. And there's a telephone on the desk. 

Do you mean he was too paralysed to do anything?"

"It's

The Chief Medical Examiner shrugged. possible. I've never seen anyone die from snakebite, nor even seen a person bitten. And you must remember that one can be paralysed with sheer fright." "But what do you think of the wound itself? asked Cameron, "Yes, I want you to examine those punctures closely," said Brockelbank. "You know, of course, the mechanism of a snake's fangs. They lie horizontally along the upper jaw, then become vertical as the reptile strikes. They are curved, yes, and, contrary to general belief, grooved instead of hollow." "But aren't they actually hollow in a few of the more deadly species?" suggested the Police Commissioner. "That is so," smiled the doctor. "But, at all events, the vast majority are grooved, and the venom, compressed automatically from the poison sacs at their base, flows down the groove into the punctured wound. 

That's Nature's weapon, and a terrible one it is.

Simple but effective. Man, however, has borrowed it for the action's practically the same as a hypodermic syringe. Those punctures, however, couldn't have been made by any ordinary needle; they're too large and, moreover, too curved." "You mean they were made by something having the same curvature as a snake's fangs?" asked 

Cameron. "You're quite sure of that ?” 

Yes, Brockelbank was certain. "In your opinion, is it possible for them to have been made at different times?" pursued Cameron. "First one puncture, you see, and then a second." "Of course it's possible, if not probable," replied the 

Chief Medical Examiner. "But, apart from all else,

that would mean naturally that the victim was helpless." "Yes," said Bland; "we can imagine him being taken unawares at the first attempt, but hardly submitting supinely to a second. I imagine he would register a kick." "But if he was unable to kick?" suggested Cameron. "If he had been knocked out by the first injection ?" "I hardly think that probable if we consider the time factor," said the doctor. "You're suggesting, of course, a weapon such as was used in the Carhart case ?" "But curved," said Cameron. "Of course there could be no other connection between that case and this one. The murderer paid the penalty long ago. 

But, as I say, it might have furnished the inspiration and suggested improvements. If the time factor is against us, why not a double needle that could make two punctures, such as this, at the one operation ?” "But where could you get a thing like that?" asked 

Bland. "They aren't on sale.” "Neither was the one used in the Carhart case," retorted the Commissioner of Police. "What has been made once can be made again.” "No, it's not impossible," conceded Brockelbank; "but what about the venom? It would be even harder for a layman to get that than for a skilful mechanic to make the weapon." "Under ordinary circumstances, yes; but there may be some right in this building." And Cameron repeated what Dr. Golding had said. "Vornoff ?" exclaimed Brockelbank. live here ?" "Yes. Do you know him?” "Does he

"No; I've never met him, but I've heard something and read something about him." "Yes, there was an article in the papers some time ago," said Cameron. "He'd just returned from abroad, and it spoke of his knowledge of snakes-he presented some to the Bronx zoo, I believe and he used the venom medicinally. I don't know what truth there was in it, if it was the usual sort of Sunday newspaper stuff." "It's all a fake," said Bland. "I remember one of these corn doctors claiming that he could even cure insanity by snake venom. Evidently he never cured himself. It's never used in medicine." "Oh, but you're quite wrong there," said Brockelbank. "It's used medicinally in many ways for many things. There's no fake about that part of it. 

If Professor Vornoff lives here, why not have him in ?” "Because he's out," replied Cameron, and told how 

Golding had sent for the other. "The hall-boy's been told to ask him to come in here when he returns.” "He should be able to give an expert opinion, which 

I can't," said the Chief Medical Examiner. "The mere fact that there may be snake venom on the premises lends more probability to your theory, Cameron." "Oh, I haven't any. Here's a box, you see, in which a snake may have come-and that's only one way it could have been smuggled in. Or it may have been an accident. The only thing we do seem to know is that it isn't a case of suicide." "No, I can hardly imagine it being self-inflicted," said the doctor. "Although, mind you, it wouldn't have been physically impossible, granting the existence and possession of such a weapon as you suggest.

NATURE'S WEAPON OR MAN'S 

37 

But then the weapon would have been found. Yet you must remember that suicide has been committed by means of a snake before this. It's the classical method. 

So, you see, we can't be positive about that." "We'll know more when we know more about the victim," said the District Attorney, "and get on the track of a motive." "And perhaps after the autopsy," nodded Brockelbank. "I'm sorry I have to be going; I've another official call to make. I hope Professor Vornoff comes in before they remove the remains."

CHAPTER IV  

As the Chief Medical Examiner left, Maddox and Keppel returned with the information that there was no sign of a snake inside or outside the building.

They added that the janitor was waiting to answer any further questions that might be asked. "In a moment," said Bland, and turned to Varcoe and O'Reilly, who were collecting a heap of papers on the desk. "Well, have you found anything important ?" "He's 'Parker Dinsmore'," said Varcoe. "What!" exclaimed the District Attorney, and stared at the Commissioner of Police. "There's Fate in this," said Cameron. 

Dinsmore! Are you sure?" "Parker "He must be," said Varcoe. "Here are some letters from Arnold & Crosby, the publishers. They don't mention his pen-name, of course, but it's all about his last book, The Murder in the Morgue. And it mentions The Triangle Case. Everybody knows 

Dinsmore wrote them.' 

"Of course," said Bland. "Why, there's a copy of his last book in Mr. Cameron's overcoat right now! 

He and I were discussing Dinsmore during dinner to-night; not having any idea that that wasn't his real name or where he lived, and now-well, this is as strange as any of his yarns." 38

"Look here, Keppel," said Cameron, "when you and Doctor Golding 'phoned, where was it from ?" "Why, from here, sir." "Yes, of course. 

Do you think I imagined you went out to a pay station? But what 'phone did you use ?" "Why, sir, that one on the desk," replied Keppel, looking injured and puzzled. "There's a postcard from somebody signing himself Paul Garwinn," said Varcoe; "but, apart from that, there's nothing of importance."

The postcard, which bore no address, but was dated that morning, contained no more than the laconic line, "Will drop in some time this evening".

None of the other papers, as Varcoe had said, was of any importance; they were receipts, unpaid bills, and pages of typescript, evidently part of a new novel. "He can't have done any serious work here," commented the Police Commissioner. "There's not even a typewriter." "He never did any serious work anywhere," said 

Bland. "But he must have been making enough out of his 'mob success' stuff to hire a dozen typists if he wanted to. And the superintendent says he was out all day." "Yes, I hadn't any idea he was an author, especially the Parker Dinsmore whose books I've seen, if not read," said Macford. "I don't think anybody in the building, or even neighbourhood, knew it. He went out regularly, as I told you, as if he was going to business." "And so evidently he was," remarked Cameron. "A very good business too. He'd need a quiet place

somewhere. here." 

He wouldn't get any inspiration "He didn't need any," said Bland. "That sort of tripe- And then, as though recollecting the figure in the next room, he checked himself and added: "How long was he living here?"

The superintendent said that Devore had rented the flat last May. "You say it's rented furnished ?" queried Cameron. "Are they all rented that way?" "No, only the small ones-bachelor flats, we call them," explained the superintendent. "Professor

Vornoff has one, and this Mr. Meier I spoke of." "And you say he'd no visitors but this Mr. Garwinn?" said the District Attorney. "Surely he must have had some? I should think a good many." "Well, Mr. Garwinn was the only one whose name 

I knew and who came pretty regularly," said Macford. "Where does he live, and what does he look like ?" asked Cameron. "I don't know where he lives. He was middle-aged, like Mr. Devore, and he looked a bit like him too.

I mean he was tall and kind of thin and clean-shaven and dark. But they weren't relatives, so far as I know." "And he had no other visitors ?” "Well, occasionally I saw Mr. Devore with a lady. though not always the same one. I didn't know the. names and wouldn't know them again. I happened to see them coming out of here at night-yes, and once in the morning." "Oh," said Bland, and cocked an eye at Cameron. "I don't mean to say," added the superintendent quickly, "that the lady stayed over night. It was at nine in the morning I saw her leaving- 

---

"A moment," interrupted Bland. "How long ago was that?" "About a month, I should say. Mr. Devore saw me as I came out of my flat, and he found occasion to speak of the matter. He said the lady was his typist and had called on business.' 

Questioned further, Macford added he had no reason to think that Devore made a practice of receiving female visitors at night. But, the flat being situated where it was, any visitor might enter, and leave without the hall-boy or himself happening to see them. The women he had chanced to see may have been Devore's sisters, so far as he knew. "More likely someone else's sisters," commented the 

District Attorney. "I take it that they wouldn't pass for his aunts?" 

No, they were young; much younger than Devore. 

The superintendent could tell that much. And, pressed further, he said that the one Devore had called his typist was a very pretty blonde. He could not remember, apart from their youth, what the others looked like, but he had happened to see three different ones on occasion. "I noticed when we came in here," said Cameron, "that you didn't have to ring or use a duplicate key.

Of course the door has the usual gadget for fixing the lock, so that it can be opened from the outside. Had you so arranged it before leaving?"

This question seemed to come as a surprise to 

Macford, and he scratched the head that was starting to go naked on top. "I don't remember doing it," he replied. "In fact I'm sure I didn't." "That would mean then, if the door was on the latch," said Bland, "that anybody could have walked

in here. It's an important point, especially when it has been shown that neither you nor the hall-boy might see them come or go."

Keppel was then asked about it, and he said that neither Dr. Golding nor himself had touched the lock.

He had rung, and the door was opened by the janitor and superintendent. "I suppose," said the District Attorney, "Mr. Devore was in the habit of leaving it like that? I mean it would facilitate the entrance of visitors whom he might not care to have standing in the hall." "But he must have been expecting Mr. Garwinn to-night," said Cameron. "Was it Mr. Garwinn's custom to enter unannounced ?" "Yes," replied Macford. "I've happened to see him more than once just turn the handle and walk in." "So," said Cameron, "that was why you did the same ?" "What? When? 

When? I don't understand," said the superintendent. "I mean when you opened the door for the District 

Attorney and me. You didn't know it was on the latch, but you knew it had often been that way, and so your action was subconscious or automatic ?" "I suppose so," agreed Macford, scratching his head again. "I can't remember what I thought at the time. And I may have put it on the latch when 

I went to see if Professor Vornoff was in. I knew

I'd be back, of course, and it would save ringing. So 

I must have done it unconsciously. Yes, I must have." "Call the janitor," said the Police Commissioner to Delaven. 

Louis Feigel was a gangling man with high

cheek-bones and the palest of blue eyes. With his long neck, vulpine nose, and habit of looking at one without winking, he reminded Cameron of some prehistoric predatory bird. He seemed more curious than concerned about the strange tragedy. "The boss," he began, thumbing at the superintendent, "sent word about fixin' the bathtub—” "A moment," broke in Bland. "What tub, Mr. 

Macford ?" "The one here," said the superintendent. “Mr.

Devore complained about the water running. He said the faucet needed a new washer and that he'd meant to speak about it before. It stopped him sleeping and he wanted it fixed right away. Yes, he spoke about it when he came in this evening." "The water's still running," commented Cameron, though he had not entered the bathroom. "So Mr.

Devore spoke of the matter at, say, five minutes to seven, but no attempt was made to fix it until about twenty past. Is that right?" 

Yes, although neither the janitor nor superintendent could say at what precise time Feigel had been informed. "I told the hall-boy to go and tell him," explained Macford. "Why, hasn't he a house 'phone?" asked Cameron. "Yes, but he wasn't there. He was up on the fourth landing putting in a new bulb that had burned out." "The hall-boy came up and gave me the boss's message," said Feigel. "I went down and got a spanner and some washers- "Went down where ?" broke in the District Attorney, and Feigel pointed to the floor. "My rooms are under here," he said. "Are you married?" asked Cameron.

The janitor shook his head and said that he lived alone. Then he went on phlegmatically with his statement. He had not been told Mr. Devore was in, and, receiving no answer to his repeated ringing, had tried the handle and found the door on the latch. 

There was no question about that. Nobody had charge of the master-key to all the apartments but the superintendent. "That's right," said Macford, "and it's never out of my charge. This apartment, and the other bachelor ones, are cleaned regularly by a woman, and I always open the doors myself." "When I come in," continued the janitor, "there was no light on in this room or the bedroom. I thought 

Mr. Devore hadn't come home yet, until I seen the light burning in the kitchenette. I called and then rapped on this desk here. There was no answer, so 

I walked through and found him laying there between the table and the window. Then I ran out and got the boss, who was in his office." "Would it have been possible," asked Cameron, "for someone to have been in this room or the bedroom and to have slipped out while you were in the kitchenette ?" 

Yes, quite possible. Feigel had been able to see nothing practically but the light in the kitchenette. 

Macford had been in his own apartment and, if the hall-boy was in the alcove, anyone could have slipped out the front door unseen. "Tell the hall-boy to come in here, Delaven,” said the Police Commissioner.

The good-looking mulatto had already engaged Cameron's attention. He did not talk like an American, nor would his accent have been out of place in a 

London drawing-room.

“AS STRANGE as any of HIS YARNS" 

45 "What is your name?" asked Cameron. "Hamilton Jones, sir." "I see. You're from Bermuda, aren't you?" "Yes, sir," said Jones, looking astonished. "I was born and brought up there."

To Cameron it had been a simple matter of deduction, however surprising it seemed to the hall-boy. He knew that this anomaly of the accent is found in 

British colonies like Bermuda, where the coloured population has learned for generations the accent of English residents and the military regiments stationed there. In all probability Jones' Christian name had been taken from the chief town, just as his surname might have belonged originally to an English colonist or soldier. They took their names precisely as the 

Southern slaves, or Scotch ghillies, took the names of their masters. 

The evidence of this good-looking "indoor aviator" lent additional support to the theory that anyone might have entered and left the Devore flat without being seen. Jones had been on the elevator, his rush hour, most of the time from six o'clock until seven.

He had been in the alcove reading the evening paper when told by Feigel of the tragedy.

Questioned about the box, he repeated what the superintendent had said. Pressed further, he added: "It must have been delivered between the time I took Miss Camblin down and Mr. Gloz came in." "I see," said Cameron. "And that was when ?"

Jones could not say exactly, but again he corroborated the superintendent by stating that he thought it must have been about half-past six. "Are this Miss Camblin and Mr. Gloz tenants?" asked Bland.

"Yes, sir. Mr. Gloz is a lawyer." "So the box wasn't there when you left to answer 

Miss Camblin's ring? And then, when you came down, you took Mr. Gloz up?" "Yes, sir. And then, when I came down again and went into the alcove, the box was there." "Why did you give it to the superintendent ?” asked Cameron. "Wasn't it your duty, instead of his, to deliver it to Mr. Devore ?"

The hall-boy then explained in detail what had happened. Shortly after his discovery of the box and the departure of Miss Camblin, the superintendent came into the hall and Jones spoke of the package. 

While they were talking another tenant entered, followed immediately by Devore. As Jones had to take this other tenant up in the elevator, Macford went and got the box. "I see," said Cameron amiably. "All this may seem a bit tedious and unnecessary, but the only way the law can find out things is by asking questions. 

And sometimes a great deal may depend on what may seem a trivial happening. I know you three are anxious to help us all you can." 

Again Bland gave him a glance. "Now, Mr. Macford," added the Police Commissioner in the same confidential manner, "did you give the box to Mr. Devore in the hall ?" "Yes, he stopped to chat with me and then spoke about the bathtub. Then I said a parcel had come for him and I went and got it. He opened the door with his key and went in with the box." "Did you see him turn on the light?" "What light?" "The light in this room."

"No, I didn't. I didn't see anything. He closed the door before I'd time to see what he did." "No, I wasn't in my office. I'd only returned to it a short time when Feigel rang. I was in the shop." "What shop?" "Ye Olde Antique Shoppe on the corner." "But what on earth took you out there?" demanded the District Attorney. "Well, you see, I'm the owner," replied Macford with a slight smile. "It's my sideline, you might say. Of course I don't do any of the selling, but I run in whenever I've time. I don't have to go out; there's a connecting door leading from my flat. The place doesn't close until nine, and I was in there with my manager and the salesgirl from shortly before seven until about a quarter past." “Oh," said Bland, and looked as though a theory he had been constructing had received a rude blow. "I'm one of your customers, though neither you nor I knew it," said Cameron with a smile. "I've bought several things there." Then he asked Macford if he was familiar with Devore's handwriting and could say if he had written the message they had found. 

The District Attorney had taken careful possession of the paper found on the desk and he now produced it. Macford said he was familiar with Devore's signature, having seen it often enough on cheques for the rent, but he could not say if this writing was his. It looked like it, and yet it didn't.

"But why should he send a warning message to himself?" he added. "And what connection can there be between a person called 'Red Rum' Myer and the 

Ides of March?" "Perhaps a very logical one," replied Cameron, pointing to the calendar on the desk. "This is the 

Ides of March." "By George, you're right !" exclaimed Bland. "The fifteenth. I hadn't thought of that."

All this, from their looks, was beyond the precinct detective, the janitor, and hall-boy, whose classical education had evidently been neglected. "Yes, the fifteenth of March, May, July, October, and the thirteenth of the other months," said Cameron musingly, a sightless look in his spectacled eyes. "Those were the Ides of the Roman month as the 

Kalends were the first of the month. Kalendscalendar. Now there is that one on the desk with its classical bromide from the first act and second scene of Julius Cæsar. 'Beware the Ides of March!' Imperial 

Cæsar ignored it and was stabbed to death. Did 

Devore ignore it too? That is the problem, or one of the many." "I s'pose I can go now, sir ?" said Feigel, who seemed to have no use for such information. "Oh, yes," nodded the Police Commissioner. "Macford too, and Jones-that is, when you've left your fingerprints with us. O'Reilly!"

to protest. They had no right to fingerprint anybody.

He hadn't done anything and he wasn't a convict. 

He wouldn't stand for it. "It's a mere formality, but a necessary one," said

If it's a case of murder and there's actually such a weapon as the Police Commissioner suggested- "And do you think we should be likely to find it ?”’ broke in Cameron with a smile. However, we'll do as you suggest. It's only fair to you all."

The search revealed nothing, and the trio had hardly gone, leaving their fingerprints with O'Reilly, when the hall-boy returned and announced Professor Vornoff.

CHAPTER V PROFESSOR VORNOFF 

CAMERON'S first impression was that seldom had he seen anyone who looked more unlike a professor of anything. Vornoff, short and fat, was stained with the sort of sunburn that can come only from a long sojourn in the tropics, and his large, round, hairless face was not unlike a mellow pumpkin. He was a sort of international picture, for he had a Hapsburg lip, Napoleonic brow, and a nose like Socrates. His eyes were small, greenish, and deeply set. 

When, following an exchange of introductions, he entered the next room and saw what the bed contained, he gave an exclamation and recoiled with a look of fascinated fear. "Ah, the work of a pit viper !" he exclaimed. "You will excuse me, gentlemen, but it recalls memories 

I would so much rather forget. Yes." He unbuttoned his heavy ulster and, producing a large, lurid handkerchief, wiped his face with trembling hands. "I have seen more than one man like that-yes, and women too. Terrible!" "What do you mean by a pit viper?" asked the 

District Attorney. "Of course I know what pit dogs are 

"You think they fight in a pit? Well, yes, and sometimes so they do. For a few sous you see in 50

PROFESSOR VORNOFF 

51 

Saint Pierre the mongoose fight the terrible fer-de-lance.

So, so, like brown moving shadows too quick for the eye to follow." And Vornoff gestured with a pair of large and remarkably hairy hands. "But that is not why they are so called," he continued. "You must know that there are two kinds of snakes, as there are two kinds of everything-good and bad. Nature has set the seal of warning on the bad as Cain, the killer, was marked to warn his fellowmen. You see a snake smooth and even between the eyes; that is a good snake, no matter how evil he may look. But you see one with a depression or pit between the eyes-what you call a 'stop' in a bulldogand that is a bad snake, no matter how small and beautiful it may look. That is a pit viper, a killer, a fellow to beware of. It is Nature's infallible warning. Yes."

Little of this was news to Cameron, if to the others, but he listened with the closest attention and interest, studying the man rather than the words. Undoubtedly

Vornoff was a personality, and one forgot his almost grotesque appearance when he began to talk. He spoke with the greatest animation, and there was something that fascinated even in his gestures. One forgot the ugly hairy hands when they also began to talk so expressively. Moreover the subject seemed to fascinate Vornoff himself, attract even while it repelled. "But I want you to examine the wound closely, if you'll be so good," said the Commissioner of Police, as the Professor still maintained his respectful distance from the bed. "Assuredly. But first-you have searched everywhere most carefully? These wicked ones are full of

guile and cunning. Incredibly so. 

Not for nothing 

Coiled up did the Devil himself take their likeness. in a slipper, waiting for the tired unsuspecting foot; under a cushion in one's favourite chair; in the clothes basket, the wardrobe-ah, the wicked ones! 

And in a warm, steam-heated flat, where might they not conceal themselves?" "We've searched the place thoroughly," said 

Cameron. "But doesn't it strike you as singular that a snake should have been here at all?" 

Vornoff shrugged and spread his hands. "I have lived so long where the creeping death is a commonplace. And in New York one finds everything, eh? 

Yes, sooner or later."

Very warily he approached the bed, his little greenish eyes on the alert; nor did he examine Devore until first he had examined the pillows and bedding. He seemed fascinated by the horribly swollen and discoloured face and neck. "I would not know him," he said. Then he rubbed his hands like a child listening to a ghost story. "A big fellow!" he breathed, referring evidently to the reptile and not its victim. "Oh, a big one, I say you. 

Perceive the width of the bite." "Too big to fit in this?" asked Cameron, and suddenly plumped down the box on the bed. 

Vornoff shrugged. "By big I do not mean of necessity size of body, but of fang. A puff adder, par exemple-that wicked one who always strikes backward-could be accommodated in a box half that size or less. The hamadryad or king cobra, the diamond-back, the mamba-all could make such a bite and accommodate themselves to that box. If I knew how long it took Mr. Devore to die, then I have

PROFESSOR VORNOFF 

53 a better idea. Yes. You know approximately perhaps?" "Yes," said Bland, "half an hour at the most." "So," nodded Vornoff, pulling at his pendulous lip. "If that is the case, then I think it the work of the black mamba. I think so when I first see him. I have seen many just like that. The king cobra, he kill quick; but the black mamba, he kill quicker.

Sometimes it may take two, three hours, and other times I see a big, strong man die so quick as that!" 

He snapped his thick fingers. "Yes, I say you, before we can apply the tourniquet." "How big is it?" asked the District Attorney. "Is it found in, say, Bermuda ?” "There aren't snakes of any kind in Bermuda,” said Cameron. "That is so," nodded Vornoff. "Like Ireland, that is one of the few places where they are not. Farther south in Trinidad, Barbados, Martinique-yes. We have the fer-de-lance, that evil one that strikes at the face. Ah, the unfortunates who were thus killed when they fled from the lava of Mont Pelee! To scramble up the hillside and get it in the neck, as we say. Death lurking in safety!" And again he rubbed his hands. "But the mamba, green or black, that is African," he continued, as though lecturing to a class. "Ten feet of fury and wickedness. The hamadryad, it will pursue if angry, but the black mamba it attacks ever.

Some maintain that the former is as deadly, but now 

I say you this: From one cobra we get one grain of venom on an average, but it takes seventy mambas to supply one grain. So little it needs, so deadly is its poison. The lancehead of Brazil, whose venom tipped

the arrows of the ancient Aztecs-and a scratch was fatal, they say was not more deadly." "But where would you be likely to find a snake like that in this country?" asked Cameron. "Are there any even in Bronx Park?" "Well, I myself presented two. No, they are not things one would care to keep as pets, and yet who knows? People keep strange things, even alligators.

The mamba is easily come by; in Natal they are as common as common. I know a man who kills hundreds every year, just as they kill thousands and thousands of people. In India one million people die every year from snakebite, and Africa cannot be far behind, if we got statistics from the interior. You are cognisant for a certainty that Mr. Devore did not keep such a pet ?" "Writers are queer folk and liable to keep anything," said Bland; "but I hardly think his taste ran to that." "Was he a writer?" asked Vornoff, and looked astonished when Bland explained. "So," he said, "I have seen the name Parker Dinsmore on many books on the stalls, books with wonderful covers. 

I have read them, no." "He'd need a cage or something to keep the snake in," said Varcoe, "and there isn't one in evidence.

A woman came in to clean and he couldn't have a thing like that lying round loose." "Do you think," said Bland, "it could have come in that box?" And he explained about it. "Ah, so that is why you show it to me?" nodded 

Vornoff. "I see." Then he examined the box, turning over the shavings, and shook his great round head. "No doubt it would not have been in here long enough. 

Tell me, where precisely was this box found? I mean here by you?"

PROFESSOR VORNOFF 

55 

Cameron explained, adding: "But I hardly see how such an aggressive brute could have been put into the box in the first place. And surely the superintendent, or Devore, or the hall-boy, would have felt some movement when they lifted it?" "Not so," said Vornoff. "Firstly you take ten feet as thick around-so !-and what room for movement is there? Secondly the wicked one is torpid. Say he has been put in an ice-box or out in the cold. He is half frozen, his wickedness at zero. He is like a string of sausage, and, in skilful hands, harmless. 

Maybe he is gorged with food, too, or drugged. "The box is delivered here not more than half an hour before Mr. Devore opens it. The wicked one has not been in his bed long. He is getting warmerand then he is placed right beside the radiator! You see, gentlemen? That awakens him, the good heat that he loves. He finds himself a cramped prisoner and tries to get out. "Mr. Devore hears a noise. He has put the box down. He thinks maybe it contains something he is expecting. He has not been curious to open it. But now he is. Why should there be a noise, or did he only imagine it? Why should the box move a little perhaps? It is strange. So he bends over, cuts the string, removes the wrapper, takes off the lid-Ah !

In a panic he starts back, tries to arise. He trips and falls on his face. The wicked one is upon him! It strikes once, and only once. There is no need to strike more."

There was a hush. Vornoff had described in speech and pantomime the tragedy so graphically, that everyone present felt as though they had been actual witnesses of it. And it was all so logical, including

the attitude in which Devore had been found. And now the District Attorney added another logical touch. "Those wine bottles!" he exclaimed. "If Devore knew they were to be delivered that way, if he thought there was wine in that box, he'd put it by the radiator to get warm. Not a doubt of it. That brings us back to Myer." "Meier?" echoed Vornoff. "You mean the gentleman who has the flat overhead? 

You think he may know something, eh? But you must not make too much of his quarrel with Mr. Devore." "Oh, no," said Bland quickly, "we know what to discount. But perhaps you can give us the true version of it ?”

Vornoff shook his head. "I only know there is not such good feeling. Some trifle or other, no doubt.

Perhaps Mr. Meier he makes too much noise overhead for a writer. He is a noisy fellow. And now that 

I know that Mr. Devore was a writer, I understand. 

Mr. Meier, he likes canned music, and I suffer from it too. Tenants are always treading on one another's corns, you see; one wants to sleep while another craves the loud speaker. And so on. They get on one another's nerves. It is inevitable in a human bookcase like this, where no two books are alike. 

The life is not natural. No, I should not make too much of that quarrel, gentlemen, whatever it may have been about. Little things are magnified in a place like this." 

Bland mentioned what Dr. Brockelbank had said about a victim of snakebite being too paralysed to do anything. "That is so," nodded Vornoff. "There is no more

PROFESSOR VORNOFF 

57 fearless man than the Zulu yet, when bitten by a black mamba, he does nothing but die. I have seen it, not once but often. He is paralysed morally and physically. No one heard Mr. Devore fall or cry out ?" "There was no one to hear him," said Bland. "That's just the point. The janitor and hall-boy were upstairs, the superintendent in his office, Mr. Meier out. Those are the four who'd have been most likely to hear him." "I understand, Professor, that snake venom is used medicinally," said the Police Commissioner, "and that you've experimented with it ?" "But yes," nodded Vornoff, once more animated. "It is strange, is it not, that death should be the means of life? These wicked ones, thanks to man's knowledge, are made to serve him. There is a man in 

Natal-I spoke of him before-whose sole business is the collecting of such venom. Up to a thousand snakes a year he kills, and the venom is sent all over the world. Yes. Me, I act as his American agent. 

So we arranged when I was last in Natal." "I see," said Cameron. "Now do you remember the 

Carhart murder case? It happened about ten years or so ago, here in New York." "The name has no meaning for me," said Vornoff, shaking his head. "I was abroad ten years ago, and crime cases I never read. No, neither fiction nor fact.

There is too much sorrow in the world that one must pay heed to without paying good money to read about it. But there is something pertinent behind your question, yes? You think that case has something to do with this one?" "Only in the sense that it might have suggested it,"

said Cameron, and told of the method employed in the famous case. "Diabolical!" exclaimed Vornoff, after expressing his astonishment and interest. "Diabolical, yes, but not new," said Cameron. "I remember a case in France, and there may have been others. There's nothing new in crime. That's a proved maxim like cherchez la femme. The Carhart idea was borrowed, and so it may be here." "Well, I have learned that very little is not possible in this world," said Vornoff, pulling at his pendulous lip. "Yet if that wound was not made by a snake's fangs then I have never seen one. No. That there should be such a weapon as you suggest, that it is a crime like the Carhart one you speak of-no, that seems too improbable for me." "It doesn't seem as improbable to me," replied

Cameron, "as the idea of a black mamba being in 

New York-outside the zoo." "Well"-Vornoff spread his hairy hands-"I do not insist on the black mamba. I only say that assuredly it looks like that wicked one's work which I have so often seen. But any pit viper may do. The Texas diamond-back is very deadly, and some people they die more quickly than others. But a snake, that is certain. There is that box and how it came here. 

There is everything. What have you to place on the other side of the argument? You have something to support your theory, yes?" "I'm looking for you to support it, Professor. Where do you keep this snake venom you get from abroad?” "In my office, my laboratory. That is downtown. 

I see what is in your mind. You think it falls into unlawful hands perhaps? But no, I take good care

PROFESSOR VORNOFF 

59 of that. I don't advertise that I have it and no layman could get it. But specialists, they know where to come.' "You haven't any here in your flat?" "No, no. It comes to my office always." "You never had any here? You're sure of that?” "Yes. I do not keep it here. But wait!" cried

Vornoff, and struck a hand against the dome of his forehead, while a look of doubt and something else dawned in his queer eyes. "I have told you the lie. 

You say have you any here and I say no. And so I think. I forget, but then I remember. I bring some home-Oh, a good many weeks ago. Yes, I bring some home to experiment on my new serum. 

I do that-and it is black mamba !" "You mean the venom, not the snake itself?" 

Yes, 'Assuredly the venom. That is the name in the trade. The wicked ones I would not keep for a million dollars. I give them to the zoo."

Vornoff buttoned up his ulster and reached for the flat soft hat that made him look more grotesque than ever. "You think perhaps, because of your theory, that some unlawful one has purloined it ?" he asked. "No, no, impossible. But I go see now for your reassurement. I show you it is not so." "I'll come along," said the Police Commissioner, and 

Bland accompanied them. 

Professor Vornoff's flat was on the third floor, over that of the one occupied by Meier. Like the latter and Devore and the superintendent, he lived alone. 

All the way up the professor kept repeating his assurance that the poison could not possibly have been touched by anyone but himself, until he unlocked

a large medicine chest in a room that was a combination study and laboratory. Then, after searching among a bewildering array of vials and retorts, he started back and threw up his hands dramatically. "Gone! I have been robbed!" 

Cameron received the information with superlative calm. "At what time are you absolutely sure it was there?" he asked at length. "That I can't say," replied Vornoff, still gesturing excitedly. "That serum I finish with and take to the office. What is left of the black mamba I put here. 

That is weeks ago. See, I put it there in the corner, behind this retort. I forget about it until you speak. 

And I label it clearly 'snake venom', as I label everything, so that there can be no mistake." "What was it like ?" asked Bland. "A liquid ?" "No, no, powder. It is dried and shipped so. A liquid you can make from it again, of course. pale amber in colour." "You couldn't have misplaced it?" 

It is 

The professor looked injured. "Impossible. I am not so careless with poison. And why should it not be safe here? I lock the cabinet when I go out, in case the lady who cleans should be tempted to investigate to her discomforture. The cabinet she is always locked, as you perceived, when I am absent." "Nine "It wouldn't be hard to find a key to open that lock," dismissed the Police Commissioner. wardrobe keys out of ten would do it." "But I ask you what robber would do it?" said 

Vornoff. "See, I have missed nothing else. What robber would want such a thing? What robber would know I had it ?"

PROFESSOR VORNOFF 

61 "Everybody that reads the paper knew you had it," said Cameron shortly. "That's the advantage of having a free Press. They knew you had such stuff, if they didn't actually know it was here. But they might naturally infer it was. And then, when they make a search, you have it all nicely labelled, so that all they have to do is pocket it." "Is it that you censure me, Mr. Police Commissioner ? 

You censure me for taking the precautions?" "No, no, I didn't mean that, Professor." "It is not my fault. I take the precautions. I lock the cabinet, I lock the door. I do not ask robbers to come. 

I tell no one about it." "Did you tell Doctor Golding?" asked the District Attorney. "Ah, but that is different. He is of the profession, a clever young man. He is progressive, interested in new ideas; but a general practice does not give much time for research. On occasion he drops in of an evening and I tell him about my new serum. I show him the black mamba, yes, but I do not give him any." "What it comes to," said Cameron, "is that somebody has had this poison for weeks. And anybody could have stolen it, not merely a tenant, but an outsider. They could have found a key for the door as well as for that cabinet; and, if they couldn't, there's the fire escape and the dumbwaiter. You ask why anyone should steal it. Well, perhaps the obvious answer is downstairs. At least you'll admit that my theory doesn't look so improbable now." "I am distressed!" exclaimed Vornoff. "But am I in any way to blame? No, I take the precautions.

And do not make too much of what may be no more than a coincidence. I am robbed, Mr. Devore he dies, but is there a connection? I say you again, gentlemen, that if a snake's fangs did not make that wound, then I have never seen snakebite."

CHAPTER VI  

It was eleven o'clock when at length the Commissioner of Police and the District Attorney left the building in which they had thought to spend but a few minutes. 

Devore's remains had been removed and the Official 

Photographer, in answer to a summons, had arrived, together with two more detectives from the Homicide Bureau. "Well, this looks like a Grade A case," said Bland. "Mysterious death was right, though we didn't think it would amount to anything. I suggest we go back to the club and have something to eat. Henry can get us a snack in the grill. The rest is routine, and we can't get hold of a motive until the machine starts excavating the past of Devore and the others." "You're an optimist, Jerry, but I believe all great eaters are." Cameron turned and stared moodily at the bulk of the Abercorn Arms, which, with its tiers of lighted windows, suggested some monstrous glowworm rearing itself erect into the stardust. "Have you ever considered the inner life of a place like that? 

I've often wondered why more murders aren't committed in human bookcases, as Vornoff calls them." "Yes?" "Yes, my fat friend. The Professor said a true thing when speaking of tenants getting on one another's 63

nerves. A big family, and no one can hate like members of a family. A rich family, people at least who are under no great necessity of keeping the wolf from the door and therefore have time to consider one another's doors. I fancy there'll be no trouble in digging up motives; that's just the difficulty." "I mean the real motive. There's this quarrel between Devore and Meier to start with. Of course there's got to be a real, well-defined motive among all the false ones." "Has there?" said Cameron. "If people always had a real, well-defined motive for even doing murder, your job and mine would be easier. You know that, or should. One of the most mysterious cases we ever had in Paris, was the murder of an old lady in a place like the Abercorn Arms. Apparently there was no rhyme or reason for it, and it baffled the Sûreté for a long time. Well, the murderer turned out to be a most respectable gentleman who lived in the flat below. And what was the motive? Well, the old lady had a habit of dragging about a trunk every morning, Heaven only knows why, and her neighbour, having stood it for just so long, goes up one fine morning and brains her. So much for human bookcases, nerves, and motives." "The French are an excitable, impulsive race, but this is New York, not Paris," said Bland. "Isn't it strange that the victim should turn out to be Parker 

Dinsmore? Like one of his own coincidences. I never thought of that being a pen-name. Of course his publishers were careful to keep his real one secret, because he's supposed to be an ex-sleuth. It's all part of the ballyhoo. But imagine him living so near the club and our not knowing it."

"Apparently even the other members of that large family didn't know it either," said Cameron. "Such is fame. Rather an interesting family, such members as we've met. Now Donald Macford is certainly a name that belongs north of the Tweed, but did you ever see a Scotchman with smoky black eyes ?” "Yes, he looks more like a wop." "And isn't it rather remarkable how often the little word 'happened' cropped up in his conversation? He happened to see this, he happened to do that, he happened to be here or there." "Of course," nodded Bland, "he seemed the obvious solution, obvious as wet water, even if there was no apparent motive. And for that reason I knew we could cross him out." "Tut, tut, Jerry. Surely you're not a victim of that popular fallacy? Merely because a certain solution may seem obvious, it doesn't follow necessarily that it must be wrong. Indeed, it's very often right. But that's a mistake clever people fall into; they are always looking for something abstruse and complex. 

In the case I was speaking of, the obvious answer was the gentleman in the flat below. But, no, that was too obvious; of course he was the most likely one to have done it, physically speaking, but then his character was impeccable and there was absolutely no motive. And so some of the best brains in the Sûreté exercised themselves quite unnecessarily in trying to find a complex answer to a simple affair of nerves and incipient insanity." "I suppose that was one of your triumphs, eh?

Well, but that alibi, if it's watertight, lets Macford out," said Bland. "If he can prove he was in the shop from before seven until a few minutes before

Feigel called him, then obviously he couldn't have been in Devore's flat." "Most obviously. I understand that nobody can be in two places at the same time. But he could have put the box where the hall-boy found it.” "So could Vornoff or this man Gloz or Meier, the hall-boy himself, the janitor-anybody. don't believe an actual snake was used? 

But you 

You think it's an improved copy of the Carhart case ?" "Do I?" said the Police Commissioner with a shrug. "I hope I've never been fool enough to try and make facts fit a preconceived theory. I can't see through a blank wall any farther than the next man. I think it's useless to try until a chink's been opened by the machine. But I've an idea that in death Devore may have given us as ingenious a problem to solve as any he gave in life." "In that case," said the District Attorney, as they entered the Sagamore Club, "it will soon be solved. 

And wasn't I right about him? Take those women who visited him." "I wasn't hazarding an opinion of his morals, merely his mentality," retorted Cameron.
