The original discussion about Devore had started on Bland discovering that Cameron had bought the other's latest thriller and liked it. The District 

Attorney called Parker Dinsmore's books claptrap, faked-up stuff that purported to be actual Centre 

Street cases. Dinsmore told them in the first person, as a putative ex-Headquarters' man, and most of his readers thought them true. His amateur sleuth, argued Bland, was a pedantic ass, and, like every other amateur sleuth in fiction, never had any foundation in fact. These dilletante gentlemen who, in their

casual moments, solved problems that confounded trained men who earned their daily bread at the business how could anybody with any sense believe it? It was a fitting commentary on the reading taste of the "herd", plus the power of clever advertising.

The books offended the District Attorney's legal mind, his critical faculty and respect for the verities. And then to discover that the fastidious Cameron, of all people, read the stuff!

Cameron had replied that Bland was taking them too seriously, but that there was no mystery why they were popular. Granted that they contained all the faults and mistakes charged against them, yet they should be judged solely on their entertainment value. 

Man was so made that he must torment himself with mysteries and enigmas and thus the detective story always had been popular. It was the most ancient form of self-torment, and, as Bland might remember, the first three ever written could be found in the Eneid, Herodotus, and the Jewish Apocrypha. For himself, if there was no real crime puzzle to engage his imagination, he turned to the fictional variety. The ones set by Dinsmore were ingenious. He did not believe that the author had grubbed up everything out of books, even if his work did smell a bit of the lamp. He did not think, as Bland charged, that he was like a monkey who had learned to do a certain trick, and that he was dull as ditchwater in private life. 

He thought he must be a genuinely clever and wellinformed man with a good mathematical brain. 

Writers might have no morals to speak of, as Bland also remarked from hearsay, but that had got nothing to do with their work.

Thus they had argued about a man whom neither

of them knew or thought it likely they ever should know. He was merely a name, and even his pseudonym had only begun to appear in the literary "puffs". He had not yet been called "The great American novelist", but undoubtedly that protean title was on the way. 

And now, when success was coming to him openhanded, he had died as mysteriously as any character in his stories. "Of course," said Bland, as the ambassadorial Henry departed after leaving the impressive cold collation which the District Attorney called a snack, "this man 

Garwinn may be able to throw some light on the darkness when we dig him up. But why did he write instead of 'phoning?" "Because he doesn't live in town-and a one-cent stamp is cheaper than a 'phone call, leaving out the question that he mightn't have known where Devore would be." "How do you know he doesn't live in town?" "Why, surely you noted the East Orange postmark ?"

Bland looked sheepish as he admitted that he had not. Although he did not wear glasses all the time, he could not, unlike Cameron, read without them. "I took it for granted the card was a local one," he said. "But if that's Garwinn's address, then he should be easily found." "If it is, yes. Also if he has no objection to being found. The real question is, not why did he write, but why didn't he appear after writing? Or did he ?" "Yes, he's another probable," nodded the District 

Attorney. "He says he'll drop in, and he doesn't drop.

By 'some time this evening' he must have meant some time after seven, for he'd know when Devore usually

got home. But, as you suggest, he could have come between seven and twenty past without anybody knowing." "Also somebody might have been in the flat waiting for Devore," mused Cameron aloud as, ignoring the food, he took out pipe and pouch. "Aside from the windows, there's the dumbwaiter. Did Devore leave the door of it unfastened, or did someone else? You know, if they can escape the janitor, it's a favourite trick of sneak thieves. In old houses like the Abercorn 

Arms, they can open the door by pressing back the catch with a knife; but they can't close it fast behind them if they leave the same way. There's nothing, you see, to get a grip on and they daren't try to slam it." "Do you think it likely the dumbwaiter could be used that way without the janitor knowing, considering the situation of his rooms? Of course if he was upstairs fixing the light . . . But you saw, of course, how he objected to having his fingerprints taken.” "Yes, but he may be one of these hyphenated citizens that knows more about his rights than the Constitution." "Well, it's quite a pretty problem, taking it all round," said Bland. "Now if Devore had written it, his amateur sleuth with the God-given prescience would have noticed some clue that you and the rest of us have overlooked." "At least he'd have noticed the postmark on that card." "Score one to you," said the District Attorney amiably. "I left myself wide open for that. But I mean clues refuse to turn up with us the way they

do in his books. He has them well trained. His hero always lights on some pivotal point that solves the whole thing." "And quite right too," said Cameron. "There's a pivotal point in every case; something on which the whole thing turns, though one mayn't recognize it as such at the time. We seldom do." "Well, what's the pivotal one here?" "It may be that warning message." "Yes, of course, if we can find somebody known as 'Red Rum' Myer-and he may be the Meier upstairs.

He may be a bootlegger, and Devore himself may have been making money that way before his books began to sell as they're doing. Maybe that's why they quarrelled. When we find who supplied Devore with that booze- "A trifling matter. There are only thirty thousand speakeasies in town. And I can imagine how anxious a precinct inspector will be to tell me that he's been taking the graft. The bootlegger must have a love for the classics-a rare blend. Why the Ides of 

March instead of March fifteenth ?" "Anything you like," said Bland. "But Devore may have written the thing himself, and, like the flowers that bloom in the spring, it may have nothing to do with the case. I mean, supposing it's a note or reference about a yarn he was writing?" "Yes, there were those sheets of typescript he must have brought home to correct.” "Well, then he jots down on the pad some idea he means to weave into the plot; something to do with a hijacker called Red Rum Myer, the villain of the piece, whom the hero has been warned about." "Yes, we mustn't lose sight of that possibility,"

nodded the Police Commissioner. "Devore may have written it himself." "It's more probable, the more I think of it, Kirk.

Writers will jot down an idea anywhere at any time, on their cuffs or collars if there's no paper handy. But that pad was handy. He sits down at the desk—" "When? That's the point." "Why, to-night, when he came home." "Not according to the evidence, Jerry. Feigel says the light in the front room was out. Are we to imagine that Devore comes in, is suddenly assailed with this idea for his story, and writes it down before going into the kitchenette? Yes, it might have happened, but, if so, why bother to turn out the light?" "But if he didn't do any writing, then the light wouldn't have been lit." "Tut, tut," said Cameron. "Are we to imagine that he groped his way in the dark to the kitchenette, impeded with a cumbersome box? Why should he? 

What does anybody do first thing on entering a dark room they're familiar with? The light switch is beside the door, and you may be sure Devore's first action was to reach for it. You may be sure he'd been doing it so often that the action was automatic.” "Well, but what was to stop him switching it off again ?" "Why such economy?" countered Cameron. "A habit left over from his impecunious days-if he had any? Perhaps. But I'm intrigued by this question of the light, Jerry. That's why I asked Macford if

Devore had closed the door immediately. That there should be a light in the kitchenette and nowhere elseno, it doesn't strike me as reasonable. And I'm

suspicious of things that aren't reasonable. We've no right to imagine some abnormal action. "Consider," he went on, "that only twenty minutes passed, perhaps half that time, before he met his death. Hardly more time, one would think, than to take off hat and coat, place that box by the radiator and open it, or to sit down at the desk and write that message. Yet in either case I think he'd have left the light burning in the front room. He must have meant to return there at once; the evening paper was on the desk, and he did no cooking at night. 

He dined outside." "Yes, there may be something in your point," said 

Bland thoughtfully. "It would suggest then, of course, that someone turned the front room light out. But why? If anybody was hiding in the flat" "Yes, if he escaped by the front door, after the murder, he wouldn't want the light as a background." "Well, it's a problem. Now there's Golding, another possible. Of course he could have stolen the stuff from 

Vornoff, no one easier. And there's the Professor himself who 'takes the precautions'. Undoubtedly a clever man." "Undoubtedly, Jerry. I wonder if he told us inadvertently about the quarrel between Devore and 

Meier. You think he thought we knew about it, eh? But did he ?" "If he's the answer, I don't see why he should be so positive about it being black mamba venom and then admit he brought some of the stuff home. We could never have proved it, even if we had suspected." "Tut, tut," said Cameron for the third time that evening. "You're out of form, Jerry. Too much food.

It Vornoff keeps it only in his office, no one's likely to steal it or not the person he may have in mind.” "You mean he may want to throw the guilt on somebody else, someone he may hate as much as he may have hated Devore ?" "That's better," approved the Police Commissioner. "But obviously if he puts the venom where it can be stolen, he divides the suspicion that otherwise should be directed solely at himself. But was it actually stolen? Who knows but Professor Vornoff." "Hang it !" exclaimed the District Attorney, “it's just like one of Devore's yarns-a bunch of suspects, and take your choice. But his are red herrings drawn more or less clumsily across the trail of the real criminal. 

Any seasoned reader knows that all the people who seem guilty have got nothing to do with it, and that the answer is some smug lad or lady in the corner." "No, that's not fair to Devore," said Cameron. "That's the conventional kind of detective yarn, but Devore knew that it's the thing under our noses that we're the most likely to miss. Yes, the too obvious. 

You remember the school game played with maps?

The boys who thought they were clever picked out names one could hardly see, but the really clever lads picked a name that spread all over the State." "And, of course, you were one of the clever lads ?" "Of course. 

And I'm sure Devore was another.

I think you're so hard on his stuff because it fooled you, if only you'd the grace to admit it." "Nonsense," laughed Bland.

CHAPTER VII  

It was not until toward noon of the following day that the Commissioner of Police had the opportunity of picking up the threads of the mysterious case and seeing the sort of pattern Centre Street had been weaving overnight. For naturally he was not at liberty to give his undivided interest to it, nor did many cases enlist more than a passing attention. 

His administrative duties were onerous, and a pressing matter of internal discipline had engaged his attention most of the morning. But at length he was at liberty, and, instead of calling for the reports on the case, he strolled in characteristically to see Corrigan and O'Reilly. 

Daniel Corrigan, who had long borne the playful nickname of "Biff", was another innovation as head of the Homicide Bureau, for he was not an ex-banker or ex-something else, but a man who had dealt professionally with crime and criminals since the old 

Mulberry Street days. Like O'Reilly and Delaven, he owed this belated recognition of his ability to Cameron. 

True, he possessed what the latter called a one-track mind, and he had a weakness for the old Big Stick methods, which the Police Commissioner deprecated; yet his honesty of thought and action-in other regimes it had militated against his promotion-was 74

unquestioned, while none knew better how to direct the arduous spadework of a case that goes by the name of routine.

Cameron now learned the following facts about Devore, most of which had been given by his secretary and publishers. He had come originally from the 

Middle West, where he had served his apprenticeship to journalism, and in New York had devoted himself to free-lancing for papers and magazines. For years he had lived a hand-to-mouth existence, turning out pot-boilers for the "pulps" under various pen-names, while trying to break into the glazed paper group under his right one. But this more ambitious work never paid; perhaps it was not ambitious enough, or he lacked the trick of the conventional "cultured" short story. Nor had he any influence. He did not fraternize with his fellow literary mechanics, was not a "joiner" in any sense, nor did he try to cultivate the acquaintance of editors and agents. He played a lone, happy-go-lucky, devil-may-care hand. He seemed to have few, if any, close friends, and nobody appeared to know much about him. To the end he remained much of a mystery and stranger even to his publishers, Messrs. Arnold & Crosby, for whom he was making a fortune.

A mystery-detective novel written under one of his pen-names, and which a publisher took a chance on, suddenly and unaccountably turned out to be a big best-seller. Unaccountably, because Devore had written better ones which book publishers had declined to publish. But this one, without any advertising or beating of the drum, sold to the tune of fifty thousand copies in a few weeks. Immediately there began a scramble for Parker Dinsmore's work, and he moved

from a Bronx rooming-house to the Abercorn Arms. 

He also rented a large, quiet, top-floor office, where he dictated his stories and a secretary did everything else. 

All the work he had to do was to polish up all the novels hitherto rejected, for anything bearing his name was now accepted.

Money was now pouring in, but it seemed to make little difference in his mode of private life, whatever that may have been. At all events, he did not blossom out in society or fraternize any more than formerly with those of his own profession. Remarkably few people outside his secretary and publishers knew that he was Parker Dinsmore. And both gave out much alleged information concerning him, such as his personal knowledge of criminals and the police, which had no foundation in fact. It was all a sort of spoof game on the public, but we have been told that the 

American nation likes to be fooled.

There was no evidence that he had ever made a hobby of keeping deadly reptiles as pets, nor did he appear to have a relative in the world. Of course, now that his name was being featured in the papers, a great many relatives might turn up, as he had left a respectable fortune. "What about this Mr. Garwinn who seems to have been a close friend?" asked Cameron. "At least he 

Nobody seems "Disappeared," replied Corrigan. hasn't been home since last night. to know where he went. He left his boarding-house in East Orange about four o'clock, and we've traced him across the river, but he seems to have gone up in smoke." "H'm," said Cameron, and scratched his chin. "You'd think, if anything had prevented his calling

on Devore last night, that he'd communicate first thing to-day, especially when he saw in the papers what had happened. He hasn't visited Devore's office or rung up the apartment ?" "No, I was up there myself for a look round. The superintendent's to 'phone us if he does." "Did you check up on that alibi of Macford's ?" "Yes. The manager and shopgirl verify it. I've no reason to think they're lying." "Did you manage a search of his apartment ?”’ 

Corrigan smiled and nodded his grizzled red head. "I got him into Devore's flat for a talk, while one of the boys he hadn't seen went through his. Nothing doing. If a weapon was used, like you suggest, he's got it hid where it'll take more than a search like that to find it. He's a bit queer-looking to have a Scotch name, isn't he? He'll bear looking up like the rest. 

There's a dame there-yes, Miss Camblin. A demure little thing, but what Hollywood would call a supersex model, I guess." "Did she know Devore ?" "Yes, and she's all bust up about it, of course. 

Says she only knew him since she became a tenant. 

Didn't know he was Parker Dinsmore, or what his business was." "Do we know her?" 

Corrigan shook his head. "She lives with an aunt,

Mrs. Royal, who may be her aunt. Golding's the family doctor, but he wasn't the only visitor. Sometimes she gave little parties-mostly stag, it seemsand Devore and Meier and Vornoff and Gloz were there. I'm going mostly by what we pried out of the hall-boy. The janitor wouldn't talk. Macford, when

I asked for a quiet line on her, said she'd brought the

best references, and he got very upstage about it. 

Of course no superintendent would want to admit a thing like that-you know what I mean-and you can't keep even the best houses free of it. It's a problem these days." "What business is this man Meier in ?" "Wholesale fruit. He has a place down in Washington Market. I guess maybe he specializes in peaches.' 

And Corrigan closed a hard turquoise eye. "He took 

Miss Camblin to the Winter Garden and then Pierre's last night. That's her style." "None but the fair deserve the best," parodied the 

Police Commissioner. "What about the other tenants ? 

Who are the ones that live on the west side? I'm more interested in them at present." "Well, Meier lives above Devore, and Professor 

What-you-may-call-him on top of Meier. Then comes

Mrs. Royal and her niece, this lawyer called Gloz, and a party by the name of Hamilton. That's the six floors." "What are the Hamiltons like ?" "Stockbroker, wife, and old maid daughter." "Nothing suggestive on the east side ?" "Nothing we've found yet. A lot of the tenants seem to be bachelors, or at least men living alone. It's a sort of bachelor hall in a way." "I see. Any results on Red Rum Myer?" "Not so far. Steincamp" (this was the name of the 

Police Inspector in whose precinct the Abercorn Arms was situated) "says he doesn't know who was Devore's bootlegger. Of course there may be a lot of Myers in the game-it's a common name-but there's none in Steincamp's precinct that he knows of. I guess that's right too. He wouldn't hold up information in

a case that's breaking like this one. Looks like it's going to be a teaser. There's a man called Belshaw that he thinks may know something if he's properly handled." "What does he think he knows?" "Where Devore got his booze." "I see. Well, what about those fingerprints?" 

Corrigan shook his head again. "All accounted for.

If there was somebody in the flat we don't know, he was slick enough not to leave any. O'Reilly's going through the records for duplicates. It's a slim chance, but you never can tell." "You've started an inquiry among the makers of medical instruments? That's a slim chance too, of course; the person clever enough to plan and execute such a crime wouldn't have the weapon made in New 

York. Still, as you say, one never can tell. If clever criminals never made mistakes, where should we be?" "That's so. And the best of 'em make them." "But I think, if such a weapon actually exists, it's only reasonable to infer that it was home-made, as in the Carhart case. You've personal knowledge of that case ?" "Yes, sir, I was on it," replied Corrigan with some pride. "You'll find the weapon up in the museum.

It's a simpler affair than the one you suggest, a crude enough job. But if any regular instrument-maker was asked to make the other kind-well, if he agreed, it would make a lot of talk. I mean it's a thing he'd remember, and then when he heard of this case" "Exactly. Therefore it's more likely to be homemade than even foreign-made. A doctor might be able to order one to certain specifications without arousing too much curiosity, and to be home-made it

postulates a high degree of amateur mechanical skill. 

So there you are." "I guess a regular sure-enough snake, shipped in that box, did the trick," said Corrigan. "At the same time Macford's mighty handy with his hands." "So must Feigel be, for that matter." "Yes, he's got to be a mechanic, but the superintendent hasn't. Yet Macford makes a hobby of fretsaw work and carpentry. He's got a lathe and a lot of stuff in one of his rooms." "Indeed? Well, perhaps it's more than a hobby.

The making of antiques is a profitable art." "You mean he makes fakes to sell in that store ?" 

Cameron laughed. "Don't be so crude, Dan. 

Antiques are never faked, merely restored. For instance, you get two or three pieces of genuine 'period' furniture and make one whole piece out of it. 

You strip them, as they call it-remove the outer layer and cover plebian wood with it. On the surface the article is genuine this or that, but underneath . . . " 

He laughed again. "The houses of our best people are full of the stuff, Dan. It's an art, and they can make wormholes that would fool even a worm. But I don't mean that 

Ye Olde Antique Shoppe makes a practice of that sort of business. I've bought more than one thing there, quite genuine. If Macford happens to be a good cabinet-maker, he could save the wages of a trade unionist. They must get a lot of stuff that needs fixing up. And he would have the time. The superintendent's duties in a place like the Abercorn Arms can't keep him very busy. I wonder his employers allow him to have a sideline like that." "Well, you see, he's one of the employers," said

Corrigan. "Yes, he has an interest in the house. He's a partner or something.' "That explains it then. I think that at least he's 

Scotch enough as regards money. That sideline must be profitable." "Yes, lots of folk pay a fortune for that sort of truck-meaning no offence to you," concluded Corrigan hastily. "We must trace this man Garwinn, Dan. What does his landlady know about him-his business and all that ?" "Nothing. He didn't seem to have any regular business. Went out at all hours. Didn't have any visitors either. Sometimes he'd be gone for days." "When he left yesterday evening, did he take anything with him?" "Yes, the landlady says he had a parcel. He seems to be a secretive duck like Devore. Never told anything about himself." "Is there anything to suggest that he'd no intention of returning ?" "No, his things weren't touched; not that there's much to remove. He has only a hall bedroom, and he left nothing to show what his business is, or where he came from originally." "He couldn't be Devore himself?"

Corrigan straightened up in his chair and stared at the Commissioner of Police. What had put that staggering idea into his head? "I was merely thinking," said Cameron, the old sightless look in his spectacled eyes, "that the description we have of Garwinn would fit Devore--and many another man. But Macford said they looked something alike. You see, is it Devore who's dead? Yes,

he went into the flat, but might he not also have come out of it?" "But he's been identified !" "And how?" said Cameron blandly. "Yes, his clothes are Devore's, but his face is so much swollen and discoloured that his own mother mightn't know him. Vornoff said he couldn't recognize him. And who has identified him but those who didn't know him at all intimately?" 

Corrigan rubbed his grizzled red head and then his equally red face. He was not an imaginative man. "This case is going to be hard enough without thinking up any new turns to it," he remonstrated. "There are so many already, Dan, that one more doesn't matter-and one good turn deserves another," said Cameron as he arose and knocked out his pipe. "I'm only suggesting that, if we admit the escape of a criminal, we may have to admit also the possibility of that criminal being Devore. There may be nothing in the idea, but it might be as well not to lose sight of it. The District Attorney doesn't think Parker 

Dinsmore clever, but I do. If you haven't got trace of Garwinn by to-night, send out a general alarm."

CHAPTER VIII  

It was the daily custom of Cameron and Bland, when their respective duties permitted, to lunch together, the one who was first at liberty calling on the other. 

The District Attorney having failed to appear, Cameron now went up to the other's office, on the fourth floor of the old Criminal Courts Building, where he discovered the cause of the delay. There was a woman in conversation with Bland, and she turned out to be 

Miss Henrietta Gower, Devore's secretary. 

The Police Commissioner saw at a glance that this was not the blonde young beauty of which Macford had spoken as coming with an early morning message from the office. Miss Gower, who wore large hornrimmed glasses like Cameron, but evidently needed them, was of uncertain vintage and singularly without claim to physical charm. She wore sensible lisle stockings and a sensible high-necked blouse, sensible heels and a sensible hat. Good sense radiated from her as from a book on diet or uplift. She sat with an uncompromisingly stiff back, hands in lap tightly clutching a large shiny bag. Everything about her shone-shoes, glasses, teeth, and even her impressive nose. Cameron had the feeling that if one looked at her long enough one might become mesmerized. 

Evidently the District Attorney was suffering from 83

the same feeling, or from her manifold virtues, for he welcomed Cameron's intrusion with relief. "Miss 

Gower," he said, "has come to see me because she thinks Devore was murdered." "Pardon. I didn't say I thought it; I said I knew it." Miss Gower clicked off her words like a typewriter. "I said it was a case for the District Attorney's office, because the police never discover anything. 

They are quite incompetent to handle a case of this kind." "This is Mr. Cameron, the Commissioner of Police," said the District Attorney, bland as his name. "That doesn't change my opinion," said Miss Gower, with a brief nod in Cameron's direction. "The police who've interviewed me say they don't know whether it's murder, suicide, or accident, and I'm quite sure they don't. It's just like them. But I know." "Yes, but as far as I'm able to gather, you've nothing to support that belief," exclaimed Bland. "The law of evidence requires something more than woman's intuition, however valuable that may be. 

What we need is fact." "It's a fact that he was foully and horribly murdered," stated Miss Gower. "He wasn't the kind that commits suicide. He had everything to live for. It couldn't have been an accident either. He had a horror of reptiles. He has been murdered just when he was beginning to enjoy life and receive the reward for which he worked so long and hard. Are these not facts? He has no relatives, no real friends, to demand an investigation and see that justice is done. 

But I will do it,"

Bland, evidently exasperated by the monotonous staccato voice that spoke so much and yet said so

little, was about to make a sharp rejoinder when the 

Police Commissioner interposed. He had seen what the District Attorney had not, that though Miss Gower might act like a robot and talk like a phonograph, her hands betrayed her. Hands betray us all, in one way or another, and Cameron had made a special study of them. The Sûreté sets great store by the human carcass. Ears, the first members to show signs of degeneracy, can tell much, but they cannot reveal emotion. "Personally I'm quite satisfied that it's a case of murder, Miss Gower," he said. "The only problem is why, and by whom, it was done. We shall appreciate your co-operation, of course, and so perhaps you'll be good enough to answer some questions... No, if you please, allow me to ask them. Now, how long have you known the late Mr. Devore?" "Since he became world-famous." 

Bland raised his brows and smiled faintly. "You mean," pursued the Police Commissioner, "since The Triangle Case made such a deserved success? I see. And you don't know anything about his life previous to that time ?" 

That simple little word "deserved" seemed to have an immediate effect on Miss Gower, for her grip on the handbag relaxed somewhat and her back became less uncompromising. "No," she replied, "but I knew about it, all he had to go through. The usual penalty of genius. There are people even now who try to belittle that genius, for one petty reason or another," turning her glasses on Bland. "But Mr. Devore had more brains and education than most of these highbrows who presume to criticize his work."

"Now, do you know," said Cameron in his confidential manner, "I've always thought so. I admire his work very much. A very clever man.” 

A little spot of red appeared in each of Miss Gower's white and shining cheeks, as though she had been paid a personal compliment. She relaxed still more. "He could have written a book on higher mathematics-anything, if it had paid him," she declared with a challenging glance at the still faintly smiling 

District Attorney. "But an author can only sell what the public wants, like anyone else.” "Exactly," said Cameron. "If there's to be any criticism, it's not his taste that should be criticized, but their's," she added. "He was ashamed in a way of his world fame. I never could see why, but he belittled it and said he was going to retire and write 'worthwhile stuff' the minute he could afford it. He never thought it worthy of his real genius, but it paid and the other kind of work didn't." "I quite understand," nodded the Police Commissioner. "Few of us are wholly satisfied at the way we earn our living. Most of us want to be at something else. Now, in the last novel Mr. Devore was engaged on, is there any character called Red Rum 

Myer ?" "She says no," put in Bland. "I've shown her that message and she says what Macford said—that it's like Devore's writing, and it isn't."

Asked by Cameron what exactly she meant by that, Miss Gower replied that it had a bogus stilted look, quite different from Devore's facile, flowing style. "As if it had been traced from a copy by a child," she said.

"I see," nodded Cameron meditatively, as he strolled to the window and gazed sightlessly at the grey bulk of the neighbouring Tombs. "That's very well put. 

By the way, did he always write in pencil ?" "No, never. I mean the first draft of his stories was always in ink. He said he couldn't think into a pencil like he could a pen. And he always had to have very black ink, the blackest he could get. Every author has his own little peculiarities." "He didn't dictate his work ?" "Yes, but only from the first rough draft.” "And how did he write-at a desk?" "No, on his knee. He used a drawing-board and an easy chair." "I see. So apart from the writing in this message being somewhat different from his, there's the fact of it being written in pencil. You've no idea what the message is about, or why it should be the Ides of 

March instead of March fifteenth ?" "I think it's obvious that the one explains the other," said Miss Gower. "I mean it's a message of warning, and naturally the warning was about his murder. Mr. Devore was a great Shakespearian scholar and could have written a book on the plays." "But why should he write a warning to himself? 

And, if he didn't, why should anyone try to copy his handwriting ?" 

Miss Gower could not say. "But if he wrote it," she added, "he may have done so some time ago and in the nature of a reminder. has no connection with his 

I know." 

All I can say is that it literary work, so far as "Had he any visitors at his office ?" asked Bland. "No, it was a workshop. That's what he always

called it. He wouldn't tolerate any interruption, once he got started, and for that reason he wouldn't have a telephone. I don't think many people even knew he had an office. He never worked very long at a time, but he worked hard." "Had he any regular hours of work?" asked the 

Police Commissioner. "No, very irregular. Genius can't be harnessed.

Sometimes he didn't appear until noon or later, and sometimes he didn't turn up at all. If he was in the mood he could do a day's work in a few hours. At other times he might spend a whole morning getting me to rewrite one page to his satisfaction—yet his critics say he turned it out like a sausage-machine !

He generally took the first typed draft home to correct." "Were you in the habit of visiting him at his flat?" asked Bland.

Miss Gower stiffened again perceptibly. "My relations with Mr. Devore were purely of a business nature, sir. Purely so." "Then you wouldn't know if he was acquainted with anyone called Myer?" said Cameron. "I don't mean the Mr. Meier at the Abercorn Arms." "No, I never heard of any other one." "Did you ever hear of a Paul Garwinn ?" "No. He never discussed his friends or private life with me. I was a secretary, that's all." "You wouldn't know then," said Bland, "anything about these various ladies who visited him ?" "On the contrary, I know a great deal," retorted 

Miss Gower with sudden violence, as she gripped her handbag as though it were something she wished to strangle. "He was pestered to death by women. He

was a genius; he was rich, handsome, kind, and considerate, and so he was their natural prey." "Really?" said Bland, sitting back in his swivelchair. "They hunted him, taking advantage of their sex and his courtesy," declared Miss Gower, her dark eyes flashing. "That's another reason he wouldn't have a 'phone in the office. He could be absolutely free from them there. It was a sanctuary. He knew that I wouldn't try to make love to him like those shameless creatures, these modern Bright Young Things. He knew he could rely on my sense and decency, on the fact that I'm a business girl, not a fool or adventuress." "If he had neither telephone nor visitors at his office, and you never went to his flat, how do you know he was pestered in such a way?" pursued the 

District Attorney. "Is it woman's intuition again, or did he tell you?" 

Miss Gower looked at him with acidulous contempt. "I believe I've said we weren't on that footing, nor was Mr. Devore that kind of man. But I know. Any intelligent person, who knew him as I did, could see what he had to put up with." "Yes, but how?" asked Bland. "Let us have facts." "If there's no 'phone, there's the mail," snapped 

Miss Gower. "Letters came constantly, nasty little pink and blue things reeking with scent and written in all kinds of scrawls. They came even from women he'd never seen, forwarded through his publishers. 

They wouldn't let him alone."

She turned to Cameron as though conscious of his sympathy or better understanding. "I dare say you'll hear some talk about Mr. Devore's love affairs, if you

haven't already. I heard about them long ago. It's all inspired by ignorance or jealousy or both. Once a man becomes famous he makes enemies, and they put the worst construction on everything he does. 

I knew Mr. Devore better than anyone, and I've the right to defend his memory even if I was only his secretary. All he ever wanted was to be let alone. He was always the perfect gentleman with me. Always!

Women are born huntresses and it's they who really pursue. He was too kind, too good, and he hated to hurt anyone's feelings, especially a woman's. They forced themselves on him, and then, when finally he had to defend himself, they called him names. I know." "Have you any particular case in mind?" asked 

Cameron. "Have you ever seen him with any woman ?" "Yes, I've happened to see him with several on various occasions. The only one I know is the one who lives at the Abercorn Arms." "Miss Camblin ?" "Yes, that sleek cat!" exclaimed Miss Gower, gripping her handbag. "She gave him no peace. She marked him down from the day she moved there. 

Perhaps that's why she moved-or the police made her. I know her kind. Aunt? Bah !" "She isn't known to the police," said Cameron. "All the more dangerous then. No, she's too sly, but she can't fool me. I shouldn't be at all surprised if she was at the bottom of this national tragedy." "You mustn't make reckless charges," said Bland sharply. "What it all comes to is that you haven't anything to offer in the nature of evidence. This office isn't concerned with hearsay or suspicion, but facts, and I must remind you that its time is limited. 

You must excuse me."

"I've given you any number of facts," declared Miss

Gower, arising as though she were made all of one piece. "If you choose to ignore them it's no more than I expected. This case demands something more than the routine red-tape mind. I shall have to engage a private investigator, for the person responsible for this foul and cowardly crime I shall and will bring to justice, or my name's not Henrietta Gower!"

Her peroration, however theatrical in phrase, was delivered in her old mechanical staccato manner, as though she were saying it was a fine day. 

The District Attorney, at the end of his patience, got red in the face, but Cameron laughed good humouredly. "Come, Miss Gower, give us a chance," he said. "The case isn't a day old yet, remember. By the way, you haven't identified the remains ?" "I don't wish to see him," she exclaimed. "I want to remember him as I last saw him." "But how do you know what he looks like now?” asked Bland.

She ignored this question and turned her implacable back on the District Attorney. "Of course you can imagine his appearance, even if the papers didn't dilate on it," said Cameron. "I'm afraid you must see him, Miss Gower. It's unpleasant, I know, but necessary." 

She recoiled, a hand at her throat. "No, no, I couldn't bear it! I couldn't really. Don't ask me to." "I'm sorry, but I must. It has to be done, for, as it turns out, you're the only one now available who knew Mr. Devore intimately. You saw him every day, as others didn't, and you're not liable to make a mistake."

A strange look crept into her small, dark eyes, very bright eyes set rather too close together and arched with heavy black brows that met on the bridge of a large, thick nose. "Why," she said slowly, "is there any question of a mistake? Is there any doubt of it being Mr. Devore?" "A possible one," replied Cameron. "No one else has thought of it, and it's no more than a remote possibility. But, of course, we must eliminate anything in the nature of a doubt. Now, apart from the usual means of identification, is there any way you'd know positively? I mean had he a birthmark or scar that others mayn't have noticed ?"

Miss Gower drew herself more inexorably erect, if possible. "I think Miss Camblin might be better able to answer that. Such intimate observation didn't come within the scope of my secretarial duties. But I'd know

Mr. Devore, no matter how disfigured he might be. 

I'd know his hands among a million. If there's a possibility of a mistake, if he should be still alive-I'll go at once. . . . Yes, I know where to go. I need no assistance, thank you." And, nodding curtly to the two important officials as though they were office boys, she stalked like a robot through the swing-door.

CHAPTER IX  

"AND they wonder why you and I don't get married," murmured the District Attorney, as he began to struggle into his overcoat. "If that lady had remained much longer, I'm afraid I should have been obliged to strangle her with my own fair hands. She's made us half an hour late, and all for nothing." "A heinous crime to come between you and your victuals, Jerry. But for nothing? No, I don't think 

SO. She's an interesting study. You're familiar, of course, with the Marquis de Sade's novels ?" "Of course. In my prurient youth I tried to purloin them, but they lost their lure when I discovered that a member of my profession is supposed to know them." "Yes, his writings are a treasure-house of true observations in the domain of sexual psychology, as 

Havelock Ellis observes. But if you haven't read them in the original- 

"Hang de Sade! Come along. I'm starving." "Well, it will have to be Savoni's for me to-day," said Cameron, as they went out to the elevator. "I've got to get back early for a session on the traffic problem."

Bland grumbled a little. But Savoni's was his own discovery, a chop-house within a few minutes' walk of the Criminal Courts Building where, if unable to 93

go to the Sagamore, one might expect an excellent menu without alleged music. Not much of a place to look at, and, for that reason, thus far unspoiled. 

Bland remained monosyllabic until half a dozen oysters and a pint of mock turtle had allayed the worst pangs of starvation, and then over a succulent entrée he asked Cameron what he had meant by the reference to de Sade. "Well, if you remember Rodin's sister in Justine, you have Miss Gower. No one knew better than de

Sade that the passionate woman, and all she suggests in the way of complexes or fixations, is the thin, dark type. People who have thick hair and heavy eyelids like that, big noses and bright eyes—' 

"This ragout is excellent," said Bland. "The woman has a look of frustrated motherhood about her, if that's what you mean." "A compulsory virgin," nodded the Police Commissioner. "One of the many. It's a great tragedy in its way, Jerry. Too poor ever to get married, too ugly ever to have a romance. And romance is burning in them. They're made for love and nobody wants them." "Doomed to bloom alone, eh? Well, vinegar plants should. To my mind the real tragedy would be if anyone had to marry her. You know that ordinarily 

I'm graciousness and sweetness personified to the ladies, but that one-why, she'd sour the milk of human kindness in a she-angel!" "Because you belittled Devore's work. Surely you saw that she was in love with him? It was sticking out all over her. And all you were thinking about was being late for your nosebag." "You defame me," protested the District Attorney.

"I never opened my mouth about Devore's books. 

No, not once." "No, you merely raised an eyebrow or smiled compassionately. Oh, I know you. Miss Gower's a clever woman, and even a stupid woman in love is uncannily intuitive about anything touching the object of her affections." "You're too French," protested Bland. "How could 

I know she loved the man? And did she? That's only your idea. I've nothing against Devore, but 'genius', 'world famous', a 'national tragedy'-you know that's all piffle." "No, call it the exaggeration of devotion.” "Well, do you believe he was persecuted by women, that he was the innocent victim of their machinations -one of these fellows who has to wear a veil when he goes out because his unearthly beauty would cause a traffic jam ?" "That's exaggeration too, of course. 

I dare say we'll find that it was fifty-fifty; that Devore pursued some women just as some women pursued him. I told you I wasn't defending his morals, merely his mentality. Of course nothing could make Miss Gower believe that it wasn't entirely the women's fault, not merely because he never made love to her, but because that's female psychology. If a man is betrayed by the object of his love, he first wreaks vengeance on her. 

But a woman? No, she goes first thing for her rival. 

It's all her fault." "Well, it's just what I told you," said Bland. "The general run of writers haven't any morals to speak of.

They don't have to keep hours and they know nothing about discipline. They're their own masters, and precious bad masters most of them make. If it isn't

women, it's dope or drink or something else. It's not a question of genius being allied to insanity. If De

Maupassant died in a madhouse, it was his bad living, not genius, that put him there. And there are mighty few geniuses these days, Devore included. It's lack of discipline, that's all." "You're forgetting imagination," said Cameron. "Most people haven't the imagination that makes vice attractive. And business men aren't anchorites, for that matter. The difference is that they're crudely vicious. They buy their vice as they'd buy merchandise." "You think Devore went in for philandering?" "Yes, from the type of secretary he chose, apart from any other evidence. Your tyro would pick a pretty one and mix business with pleasure until either he would have to get rid of her or marry her. 

But your veteran knows better; work is work, and play is play. He picks a respectable old watchdog with whom his relations won't be misunderstood. She keeps off all the women he doesn't want, or of whom he has grown tired, and she furnishes the alibi for a perfect character. The mistake they make is in forgetting that even respectable old watchdogs are capable of falling in love." "That's true. So, notwithstanding your interpretation of female psychology, couldn't the old watchdog have done the trick? You noticed, of course, how she received your invitation to view the remains. That might have a more sinister explanation than the one she gave." "It's conceivable, of course," nodded the Police 

Commissioner. "And it wouldn't conflict with my interpretation, which is really that of the foremost

psychologists. In this case Devore wouldn't be her husband or lover, therefore she couldn't kill somebody for taking from her what she never possessed. Obviously she hates Miss Camblin as she hates all the other women he may have known. Obvious also that she spied on Devore and them and probably read their letters. But they weren't rivals; their removal wouldn't increase her chances. She loved Devore secretly, for otherwise he'd have got rid of her, and she knew it was hopeless. So she might kill him in order that no one else should have him. That would be the motive; it always is. "As I say, when you get women of that temperament anything may happen. Consider her position; her only chance of continuing her association with 

Devore is to pretend she doesn't love him. A barren association, but infinitely better than nothing. She must see him every day, hear his voice, watch his beautiful hands, et cetera. A snow-capped volcano.

Perhaps the strain becomes too great even for her admirable self-control and she erupts. reveals her passion and is given notice. she carries out what she has long planned in her halfmad brain. And perhaps remorse will set in, and she'll turn her attention to those who helped to make the deed inevitable." 

Bland drank his coffee in silence. 

Perhaps she 

Perhaps then "What gave you the idea of it not being Devore ?" he asked at length with a curious glance. "Well, it might be convenient for him to disappear.

There is great competition in the philandering business. 

One makes deadly enemies. Money and women—the two great causes of crime." "I'd put women first."

"Yes, there is no hatred like jealousy. Devore may have led a double life-I mean two different and distinct identities. Parker Dinsmore mayn't have been his only assumed name. He kept no regular hours, and Miss Gower says that sometimes he didn't turn up all day. Garwinn's landlady says the same of him. If Devore wrote that message of warning, it may be that he wished naturally to supply a reason for his supposed murder-a false clue, yes. Yet I think if we knew who wrote it, and why, we should know a great deal." "Miss Gower's suggestion, that he wrote it some time ago as a reminder, seems reasonable," said Bland. "On the other hand, it's even more reasonable to suppose, considering the character of the writing, that it was the murderer who supplied the false clue. If Devore isn't the victim, who is? That's the point." "One of the questions," nodded Cameron. "Why did Garwinn not show up? Or did he? Did he write that postcard because, as we've seen, Devore had no office telephone and never received visitors at his office, or because he wanted it to be known that 

Devore expected a caller on that fatal evening? Did he use a postcard because it's cheaper and handier, or because it's more public? What was in the parcel that Garwinn carried when he left home? Why did he leave at four o'clock when he was not to be at the 

Abercorn Arms until some time after seven ? Where is he now? Is Garwinn Devore? And, finally, why was the light burning in the kitchenette and not the front room? You may also tell me, if you like, what song the sirens sang, and what name Achilles assumed when he lived among women." 

"You mayn't have been so far wrong when you said

that this might turn out as great a crime puzzle as any 

Devore wrote," said the District Attorney. "If you should ask me, I'd say that already it's far greater.

But we're bound to get an answer to some of these questions before long." "The really important ones? Perhaps. Yes, a curious and interesting case so far, potentially and otherwise. I only wish I'd more time to devote to it. But, hello! here comes 'the blameless physician'. 

What news, Esculapius?"

The Chief Medical Examiner, speaking of the autopsy, said he adhered to his original opinion that a snake had caused the fatal wound. "I've had Marvin down," he said, referring to the curator of the Bronx reptile house, as he took a seat at Cameron's table. "And Professor Vornoff volunteered his opinion. A very clever and interesting man, by the way. I'm glad I met him. Both Marvin and he agree with me." "Did he tell you about the black mamba venom and the robbery at his flat?" "Yes. And, apart from that fact, Marvin and he think it highly probable that it's black mamba venom, though we can't prove it. Of course, Cameron, that robbery tends to support your suggestion, but we really can't believe in the existence of such a weapon." "Why? It existed in the Carhart case.” "I know. But still-" And Brockelbank shook his head. He then went on to explain why the other two experts supported his scepticism. Quite apart from the problem of having such a weapon made secretly, there was the question of its use. And, on the back of the menu, the doctor proceeded to illustrate his argument with diagrams.

"There was no such question as this in the Carhart case," he said. "Vornoff's idea about Devore falling prone in his effort to escape is entirely consonant with the course of the punctures. Their general direction is downward-like this, you see. Now Devore, as you saw, is tall above the average, a fraction over six feet to be exact, and it would be almost impossible to produce such a wound with an artificial weapon unless he were lying prone or sitting down." 

The Police Commissioner made no attempt to query this assertion, but Bland did, his legal mind craving proof. "I said almost impossible," repeated Brockelbank, "for we've no right to infer some unusual, action. 

Consider this hypothetical weapon, this hypodermic syringe with the curved double needles, for that's what it would be in substance. Now you've not only to insert it, but work the plunger or piston. Try reaching up a hand and doing that. You see? The most natural movement is horizontal, not vertical. A reptile would strike downward because it must, but not the human hand holding such a weapon." "Your argument mayn't be conclusive, but I think there's a great deal in it," said Cameron. "And all the evidence goes to show that Devore was on his feet almost up to the moment of being struck. Did the curator say anything about those two mambas that Vornoff presented to the zoo ?" "Yes, they're dead. Died some time ago. They don't thrive in captivity and there isn't another one, to his knowledge, in the country. It was a feat for the professor to get those two alive. The curator says that, as a rule, you have to go after them with a shotgun instead of a noosed stick. You have to kill

ΙΟΙ them before you can extract their venom, or they'll kill you. Vornoff and Marvin say that, of course, it may have been some other deadly reptile more easily come by, and it wouldn't have had to be so deadly either." "Why?" "Because," said the doctor, "Devore had a bad heart. He may have seemed organically sound, but he was far from being so. An alcoholic heart. I don't mean he was a sot, but he must have done a lot of steady drinking over a long period. Perhaps it's what he worked on. It would mean then he'd succumb to a poison that a healthy system could throw off, while a virulent one would finish him quickly." "Vornoff was telling us how even strong Zulus are paralysed when bitten by a black mamba," said 

Cameron. "It's partly psychic, of course—the knowledge that there's no hope. We've seen that, even if

Devore called for help, there was no one to hear, and 

I suppose he may have been unable to go out into the hall?" "Quite likely," nodded Brockelbank. "If he wasn't able to telephone he must have died where he fell. 

Perhaps the shock killed him, or at least produced unconsciousness, before the poison got properly to work. The condition of the heart tends to support that idea. If so, then he was saved much suffering." "Yet there's no sign of a snake," said Cameron, shaking his head. "And there's that mysterious message." "Look here," exclaimed Bland, "I've been thinking about that message and I've got an idea. Supposing it's in code ?" "The clever one !" said Cameron, patting him on

the shoulder. "I sat up until four o'clock this morning trying to decode it." "The Harry you did! You haven't got the paper." "Oh, but I know it by heart. If it's a code, then it's the first one that ever stuck me. Why should it be in code ? You think, after all, that Devore was clever enough to invent one that would defy an expert?" "You needn't rub it in," said Bland. "It was only an idea and, with your usual beastly secretive habits, you never told me you'd been working on it. Why did you think it might be one?" "Well, you never can tell, Jerry. And why should I confide in you when you don't believe Devore had the brains to make one ?" "No, and neither had he, for that matter." "Well, there you are. Anyway, it's not in code.

I'm quite satisfied of that. My opinion of Devore's cleverness doesn't go to the point of thinking him capable of devising one that won't yield to treatment. 

But it was a good idea, Jerry." "Thanks," said the District Attorney drily. "What it all comes down to then is that we know just about as much as we ever did, which is the grand sum of nothing."

But when they returned to Centre Street their knowledge suddenly increased. Miss Gower had positively identified Devore, there was some reason to believe that Paul Garwinn had been traced, and the fingerprint department had unearthed a set that were duplicates of one person involved in the mysterious case.

CHAPTER X  

WORD had come into Police Headquarters from St. Luke's Hospital that a patient answering Garwinn's description was in the accident ward, and Detective Delaven was detailed to investigate. "He was hit by an automobile or something,” explained Corrigan, "and knocked cuckoo. He can't talk, and he's got nothing on him to show he's our man, but they saw a description in the noon edition and think he may be. You'll need somebody to identify him, of course, so get Macford to go along. It'll be two birds with one stone, for the Comish wants us to make another search of his flat. Feigel's too."

If the superintendent of the Abercorn Arms had any reason to suspect an ulterior motive in Delaven's request, or that his apartment had been searched already, he gave no sign. He demurred at first, on the presumably reasonable ground of having something else to do, but when Delaven pointed out the time it would take to fetch Garwinn's landlady and that there was no one else available, he consented. "The hall-boy knew Garwinn as well as I did,” he said, "but I suppose I can be better spared. You've got a taxi? Well, all right; I'll be out in a minute."

He was talkative on the way to Cathedral Heights, expressing a natural curiosity about the case and asking 103

if there had been any developments. Delaven, soberfaced and sedate, had a gift of conversing in a confidential manner, as though making a priceless present, but on subsequent analysis one realized how little he had given away. "Well, I hope you boys clear up this business in quick time," said Macford, "for it's not doing the house any good. No tenant likes to think there may be a deadly snake hiding out somewhere on the premises. I don't fancy the idea myself. Some of them are scared even to go to the dumbwaiter. And it may be somewhere in the shaft. It could use the rope, even be mistaken for it. It's as dark as sin in there. And the dumbwaiter door was open in Devore's flat, remember." "We've had a look at the shaft," said Delaven, "but we'll go through it again with a fine-tooth comb if we have to. The trouble is we don't know what we're looking for. Between you and me, and quite confidential, how about that snakeologist on the third floor? 

Yeah, Vornoff. Had he any trouble with Devore ?" "Not that I know of. Why?" "Well, he had the stuff if he wanted to use it. And the hall-boy was saying he's friendly with this Miss Camblin-" "Yes, I know what he'd say," broke in the superintendent, his black eyes beginning to snap. "Well, the 

Homicide Bureau can lay off her; she hasn't a thing to do with this case and I hope they aren't going to try and drag her into it. If that white coon don't watch his step I'll fire him so quick it'll make his head swim.

I've an idea what's biting him, and if I knew for certain- "Kinda growing outa his pants, eh?" encouraged

Delaven as the other checked himself.

"Never mind," said Macford as he lit a cigarette and smoked quickly. 

He was silent for some time, then: "A good-looking and sociable girl, if she's got no men folks, has a lot to put up with. And if she has a bit of money of her own, all the worse in a way." "I thought it was her aunt who'd the money." "Well, it's all the same. She hasn't any men folks either. Your chief was asking me about Miss Camblin, and I know what he thinks. But he's all wrong, and you can tell him so from me.' "I guess he didn't really think it," said Delaven "He was only wanting a line on her." "Well, you'll get many a crooked one. She's the kind that's friendly with everybody. She can't help being friendly, having a smile and pleasant word for everybody, and so a lot of cats and dogs start clawing her. You know what I mean." "Yeah, it's a hard world," said Delaven sympathetically. "I tell you," went on Macford, grinding his cigarette under heel, “an apartment house is worse than an old woman's home. Only a man in my shoes knows all the stuff that goes on. And men can be as bad as women when it comes to snooping and dirty gossip. 

Yes, a lot worse. Janitors and hall-boys have a lot of time for it. I've had complaints about Miss Camblin. 

Her radio keeps some crabs awake, or she's bringing down the ceiling with dancing. Harmless little parties, but a woman can't be forgiven for having a lot of men friends." "She's made some enemies, eh?" "Of course. There's that Miss Hamilton, on the top floor, who hates her like poison. Why? Well, if you

saw Miss Hamilton, you'd understand. She's dying to get married, and she pulled a pretty good stroke with Meier and Devore and the others till Miss 

Camblin came." "Put her nose out of joint, eh?" "Yes, and such a nose! Miss Camblin hasn't any idea of getting married, but she's friendly and people like her. She don't have to run after men like Miss

Hamilton. She's good looking, full of life and fun-so there must be something wrong with her. That's the idea." "Devore used to take her out, eh? And this wholesale fruiterer too?" "I've given her a good time myself, for that matter," said Macford with a gesture of dismissal. "I daresay you'll find it out, so that's one reason I'm telling you. 

I guess the hall-boy would have told you if he'd known.

But it was before he came, and just after Miss Camblin moved in." "You'd known her before?" "No, I hadn't. She'd sense to see I'm not the marrying kind and that a man can want a woman's companionship without romance. That's why she's popular with men; they know they're safe with her. 

But most people can't understand it; they think there must be either a marriage certificate or money in it. 

They make me tired." "You don't think then that this trouble between 

Devore and Meier had anything to do with her?” "Who told you about that? Well, no matter. It may have, for all I know. They had a row about something. Maybe they were jealous of each other, but that wouldn't be Miss Camblin's fault. She certainly isn't Devore's kind, if you know what I mean, though

he may have hoped she was. She'd never go to his flat alone like those other women I saw." "Was this fellow Garwinn one of her friends ?" "Not to my knowledge. When he came here it was only to see Devore." "If this is him we're going to see," said Delaven ungrammatically, "I wonder what he was doing uptown." "I'll bet it isn't Garwinn. Reports like that will be coming in from all over the map. They always do." 

But it turned out to be Paul Garwinn, Macford identifying him at once in spite of the bandages swathing the patient's head. He was still unconscious and, although there was no fracture, it was highly problematical if he should ever recover. He had been found the previous evening, at half-past six, lying in the gutter a few blocks from the hospital. It was dark at that hour, the side street practically deserted, and no one had seen how he had come by the injury. "He may have been slugged," said the house physician. "I mean so far as his injuries go, there's really nothing to show how it happened. He may have been stood on his head by an automobile, or hit with a club. 

A speed-fiend might have sent him flying and never known what had happened or stopped to find out." "Any sign of him being robbed ?" asked Delaven. "Not so far as we can tell. His money and watch weren't touched. But he hadn't any private papers or anything to show his identity." "Had he a parcel with him?"

The house physician could not say, and further inquiry showed that the parcel was missing. 

Delaven, after 'phoning Police Headquarters, went

to look up the night patrolman who had found Garwinn, and Macford returned home. 

Meanwhile, both the superintendent's and janitor's quarters had been searched thoroughly while Louis Feigel himself was going through a bad half-hour in Devore's apartment, whither he had been summoned by Corrigan.

For Feigal was the person whose fingerprints were filed in Centre Street, which may explain his objection to having others taken. Under another name he had served a sentence for burglary with violence. "That was years ago, Chief, and I been goin' straight ever since," he now pleaded when taxed with this indelible past. "I've only been in the shade once- "You mean, you've only been caught once.'' "No, honest, Chief. I was outa work and desp'rate at the time. It was an amachoor job, and I got nothin' outa it but three years' hard. It taught me a lesson, and I never tried it again, honest, Chief." "If you did we've yet to learn about it." "I never did, Chief. I knew you'd get me if you checked up on them prints, but I been hoping for the best. If the supe learns I'm an ex-con I'll be outa a job again, and God knows it was hard to get. Gimme a chance, Chief." 

Corrigan, if given to wielding the big stick on occasion, was no persecutor of Jean Valjeans. He knew the problem of the ex-convict, the difficulty of finding and keeping an honest job when old companions in “stair”, and many a detective, found profit or pleasure in spreading the glad tidings of the past. "We won't run you out of your job if there's no call for it, and what your boss don't know won't hurt him," he said, and dismissed the other's effusive thanks. "But if there is a reason you'd better come across with

I it now. 

For instance, if you've been holding out anything on this Devore case-well, I'm giving you fair warning."

Feigel protested that he had told the whole truth, but as proof of his appreciation of the treatment received, and his anxiety to help Corrigan all he could, he would now speak of matters upon which he had thought it best to keep silent. "I don't wanta to get anybody into trouble," he prefaced, "but, if this is a murder job, my idea is that it all started with that Camblin dame. I'm not saying anything against her, understand, but we never had any real trouble here until she come. Most everybody in the house that wears pants fell for her, and that goes for the hall-boy and the supe himself." "The hall-boy?" "Yes, sir. It was just like a Tabby come into a house of 

Tom-cats, for most of the tenants are living apart from their wives if they ain't bachelors or divorced. And 

Jones, even if he ain't quite white, or because he ain't, is some Tom. He's had trouble with a black-and-tan that lives up in the Black Belt. She come here one day and tried to pull a razor on him. Yes, sir, we had our troubles, but we were a happy family until Miss 

Camblin come."

All this, and more, together with Delaven's report, was duly passed on to the Police Commissioner that evening when he was at liberty to receive it. "So, according to Macford, the lady is an injured angel, while according to the janitor" Corrigan shrugged. "Not that he could, or would, put a name to anything. And it may be that sour grapes hasn't made Feigel any sweeter, though he claims he's off women for life. But, like I told you, her looks are

IIO  

enough to stand a community of saints on its ear, and the Abercorn Arms don't seem strong on saints. That man Meier is living apart from his wife." "Oh," said Cameron, "when did that happen? Is she known at the Abercorn Arms?" "No, they separated before he went to live there." "I see. And Feigel's an ex-convict," mused Cameron. "No, we don't want to hound him, Dan, but we'll have to discover all he's been doing since he came out of the shade." "He says he's been going straight. They all do." "Yes. You found nothing in his apartment, or 

Macford's ?" "Not a thing." "Well, Mr. Garwinn seems almost as much of a mystery as ever. Even if we now know he isn't Devore, we don't know much else. What happened to that parcel ? 

What was he doing up near Cathedral Heights? Has his being in the hospital anything to do with Devore being in his coffin? Anyway, I should like to meet this Miss Camblin." 

But meeting Miss Camblin, even for a Police Commissioner, was more difficult than it sounded. That same evening after dinner Cameron went from the 

Sagamore Club to the Abercorn Arms, Bland being engaged elsewhere, and made the acquaintance of 

Mrs. Royal, but not her niece.

Mrs. Royal, who reminded him of a pampered 

Pekinese, received him with an arch look that masked a certain curiosity; for with his comparative youth, spectacles, and general air of ingenuousness, Cameron presented a new problem in police officials.

She was so sorry her niece was not at home; however, they had been questioned by the police and she hoped

III 

this dreadful affair, which they felt so acutely, wasn't going to cause them further distress or annoyance. 

Really, they knew nothing about Mr. Devore, no more than they had already told the authorities. It came as a complete surprise to discover that he was a popular novelist. It was strange how one could even receive a person in one's home as a guest and yet know nothing about him. And then Mrs. Royal, as though encouraged by Cameron's evident inexperience, became maternally confidential. "But that's just like little Madeline, making friends with everyone," she said, waving a puffy, jewelled hand. "You never saw such a girl, Mr. Cameron, for having friends. She has such a big heart, and even people like the hall-boy and janitor must tell her all their troubles. Yes, indeed. I say it's all right to be democratic, but you must draw the line somewhere. 

And people do talk so. You know the world, Mr. Cameron." "I m not so sure of that, Mrs. Royal." "Oh, I know you're cleverer than you look. But, as 

I was saying, you might as well argue with the wind. 'Auntie', says my niece, 'I can't help it. What am I to do? They insist on being friendly or confidential, and 

I can't be rude. Goodness knows, I'm not dying to hear about the elevator-boy's fiancée in Harlem, or why

Feigel never married, but they seem to think I was born for it, and I don't like to hurt their feelings. 

And if it helps them in any way to tell me, why, all right. Though sympathy doesn't cost anything, nobody seems to want to give much of it away'. "That's what my niece says, Mr. Cameron. Such a big-hearted girl. And so Professor Vornoff tells her all about his travels, and Doctor Golding about diseases

and operations, and Mr. Meier about the wholesale fruit business, and Mr. Gloz about lawsuits, and Mr. 

Devore about books and plays, and Mr. Macford about the antique furniture trade. Yes, they all come here I don't mean the janitor and hall-boy, of courseand it's better for a girl to meet gentlemen friends in her own home than outside. "Girls aren't what they were in my day, Mr. Cameron, but I don't think they're any the worse for that. I'm not one of these old mossbacks who think there's nothing like the good old days. No,, indeed. It does girls good to have men friends, what they call pals.

They don't look at it the way they did in my time. 

Even divorced people aren't looked at the same way. 

And why shouldn't they be? If a man can't get on with his wife it don't say he's bad, maybe only sensible. "Maybe I don't quite approve of all of little Madeline's friends, though I must say they've never been anything but gentlemen. I don't mean she hasn't any girl friends too, understand. Yes, dozens and bushels.

But middle-aged men seem to find her so sympathetic and understanding. Most young girls aren't like that nowadays; they're too selfish. But my niece was always different. And she likes middle-aged men because generally they're more considerate than the young ones, if you know what I mean. They aren't so vain and selfish. "She's a good, sensible girl, Mr. Cameron, and I've tried to make a proper home for her since my widowed sister died and left her alone in life's battle. She's never given me an uneasy thought, and when I go to join my dear Julius in Heaven, whenever the dear Lord wills, little Madeline will have my modest fortune to add to the little her parents left.

"Must you go, Mr. Cameron? Well, I've enjoyed our little talk so much. A good conversationalist, that's what I like best about a man. I always say he must be a good talker if we're to get on. 

I'm so sorry my niece isn't at home, but I'll tell her you called. 

I know she'll be sorry not to have met you."

But Cameron saw her if he did not meet her, when he was leaving, after a visit to Devore's apartment. As he stood for a moment in the shadow of the steps a taxi glided into the kerb, and then he caught a whiff of perfume as a light footfall sounded behind him. 

Attractive, as Corrigan had said. Demure, petite, baby-faced. Not beautiful, scarcely even pretty in a sense, but with something far more arresting than either. Sex-appeal, animal magnetism, personality, vital force, "It"-call it what you will, it was there in abundance. A passion-flower.

Suddenly conscious of the tall figure standing in the shadows, as though informed by a sixth sense, she turned for a moment and gave him an enigmatic electric glance. 

Dr. Golding, in full evening war-paint, hopped from the taxi and held the door wide for her as if ushering in royalty.

Cameron knew it was Miss Camblin, if for no other reason than that the perfume she diffused was the same as he had detected in the hall of Mrs. Royal's flat. 

Not a blatant odour, but intriguing like the girl herself. She had not recognized him, even if she knew his appearance. Quite palpably the confiding aunt had told a social fib, and they had given him every opportunity of removing his unwelcome presence from the vicinity. Evidently Dr. Golding had arranged for a night out, and was to call at a specified hour.

"And so that's little Madeline, the great-hearted one," said Cameron recounting the incident to Bland. "Her good aunt, who is waiting so patiently to join her dear Julius in heaven, reminds me of a lady I knew officially in Paris. She managed a maison de tolerance. 

But all this does not explain Red Rum Myer, my friend, or why the light was burning only in the kitchenette."

CHAPTER XI THE CENTRE STREET PROBE 

THE following morning Paul Garwinn was able to give a short statement which appeared to dissipate all mystery concerning his connection with the case. 

According to Garwinn he had been Devore's best friend. He had inherited a small income and, like 

Devore, he was a bohemian, but, unlike the other, never had the urge to make his literary talent pay a dividend. Though Devore and he no longer saw each other so often as formerly, they still kept up the old friendship, Garwinn calling when he was in town. 

They spent the evening playing chess, a game of which both were inordinately fond. Garwinn said it was now the real bond between them, for no longer did they share the same social contacts. Apart from 

Devore's rise to affluence, Garwinn's state of health had demanded an. abandonment of the city and its night life. "We were to have had a game of chess the night before last," he explained. "Devore always let me know when he'd be free, and then I'd drop a line if I could come. I knew he'd no office 'phone and that he never wanted visitors during working hours. "So there's no mystery there, and none about that box or my accident. No, nor about my being up in this neighbourhood. Why shouldn't I be? 

I've a 115

friend who lives on Morningside Avenue, and I was bringing her a little present, that's all. After I'd climbed the steps at One Hundred-and-Sixteenth

Street, meaning to get the subway down to Union Square, an automobile came whizzing out of the night and knocked me flying. I know that much, if 

I don't remember anything else." 

Questioned, he said he never had heard of anyone called Red Rum Myer, nor did he know where Devore got his liquor. Neither had the Ides of March any meaning to him but the accepted one. He had no knowledge of an enemy who would wish to take 

Devore's life.

He was not allowed to be questioned further, and it was problematical if he could have told anything more. Plainly it was a great effort for him to think and talk. There was evidence of imperfect coordination, and his speech, if he recovered, might be permanently impeded, his memory clouded. 

Subsequent investigation, aided by voluntary information from other quarters, corroborated and amplified in a measure what Garwinn had said. But the female friend who lived on Morningside Avenue did not come forward and reveal her identity, nor did the driver of the automobile. Evidently they preferred to remain anonymous and had good reason for so doing, if one read between the lines of Garwinn's story and it was true. According to the hospital authorities, there was reason to believe he was not a prohibitionist, and perhaps the little present for a lady had been in the nature of contraband. Nor did inquiry tend to show that a debilitated constitution interfered seriously with his bohemian ways.

The Centre Street machine was now grinding

THE CENTRE STREET PROBE 

117 steadily, and the salient fact that emerged about Devore was his addiction to philandering. No longer could there be any question about it, and perhaps this game, as much as chess, was the real bond between 

Paul Garwinn and him. Which is not to say that

Devore suffered from satyriasis, or that the police were able to find one woman ready and willing to give adverse evidence against him.

But naturally the case had made a tremendous sensation, not merely because of the mystery surrounding it, and Parker Dinsmore in death was getting the publicity denied Parker Dinsmore in life. People came forward in shoals who claimed to have known him at one time or another, and, apart from all the chaff, there remained the wheat of his philandering. 

Whether responsible or not, women had always played an important part in his life, though none appeared to have known him long. Word even came from his home town in Ohio that a girl was really at the bottom of his migration east.

He attracted women; he possessed what the Commissioner of Police had sensed at once in Madeline Camblin. "And so you really can't blame him in a way," said Cameron to Bland, "any more than you can blame her. You've got it, or you haven't-something that's born with you, like a wen or poor relations.

It's what makes film stars and stage stars, and it comes over in a way even in Devore's books. Most of the criticism comes from people who haven't got it, or wish they had. Now Miss Gower, for instance, has got passion, but not 'it'. If she had, she'd no more need good looks than did Madame de Staël. Sex, sex, first, last, and all the time. It's at the root of everything, including religion."

"If they aren't responsible for being born with it, they're responsible for the use they make of it," said the District Attorney. "A great deal of bunk has been written and talked about it too." "Undoubtedly, yet there's no denying its existence. 

And I'm not saying Devore didn't misuse it, merely that he'd more temptation in that line than the average man. As I suggested before, there's no doubt that some women pursued him just as he pursued others. I don't agree with your idea that he was a hell-rake and all that sort of thing. I dare say much of his philandering was quite harmless, or he meant it to be, but he always had to be having some kind of an affair with a woman. I think that was the keynote of his character, as it is of many writers, great and small. And I still think it was at the bottom of his death." "You believe Miss Gower and the janitor, and that it's Miss Camblin ?" "Quite possibly. I repeat that the obvious is very often right. But, of course, there are other women we know nothing about."

Centre Street would have liked particularly to interview the four women whom Macford had seen on various occasions visiting Devore's flat, especially the young blonde whom he had referred to erroneously as his secretary. But not one of these ladies offered to give evidence, nor, indeed, could any trace of them be found.

Those who admitted having known Devore, denied that they had ever gone to his flat, nor could the superintendent identify them. In the main they were women of a certain class, or queer, "artistic” creatures of bohemia, and there was nothing to show

THE CENTRE STREET PROBE 

119 that any of them had been seriously in love with

Devore, or stood to profit in any way by his death. 

In fact it represented to them a financial loss, if nothing more, for Devore had been generous with his new-found wealth, giving open-handedly to these denizens of an impecunious world in which once he had lived and had continued to visit on occasion.
