They spoke of him with affection and gratitude; oh yes, his philandering propensities were well known, but no one ever took his efforts in that line seriously, not even himself. In brief, whatever affairs Devore may have taken seriously, were too well covered up for 

Centre Street's taste. 

Strangely enough, the only woman who had been seen near the Abercorn Arms was Henrietta Gower. 

The patrolman on beat had noticed her more nights than one, and so also had the hall-boy. This she did not deny, when questioned, but explained that she had been out merely for a walk and happened to pass the place by chance. As she lived within reasonable radius of the Abercorn Arms, this seemed plausible, though Cameron considered a different explanation more than likely.

The Centre Street probe was at work on every tenant of the Abercorn Arms, and Professor Vornoff, Mr. 

Meier, Dr. Golding, and others were coming in for a close scrutiny. There was nothing to show, however, that they had any enmity toward Devore, or that they were rivals for the affection of Miss Camblin. 

The Professor in particular was outraged at the suggestion that he was anything more than a fellow tenant and casual friend. Mr. Meier laughed and dismissed the idea with a gesture, as he dismissed his quarrel with Devore. That had been merely an affair of

words, Devore saying he made too much noise overhead.

As for Miss Camblin and Mrs. Royal, their relationship was evidently what it purported to be, while their income, derived from no more heinous a source than Liberty Bonds, enabled them to live comfortably if not extravagantly. Presumably they were respectability itself and always had been. 

Donald Macford's smoky black eyes and Gaelic name were also explained: his father, a Scotch- 

American master mechanic, having married a Mexican,

Macford inherited all his father's skill with tools, and at one time he had worked as a machinist and carpenter, as well as at various other trades, before speculating in house property. As Cameron had suggested, he did all the repairing in the antique business, and therefore his skill, not merely with wood but steel, was such that he might easily be capable of improvising such a weapon as the Police Commissioner suggested.

Against this fact, apart from a presumably blameless, if roving past, there was the undeniable one that no incriminating evidence could be adduced against Macford. His person and apartment had been searched and no trace of either the weapon or poison found. 

For considering the amount of venom stolen, as related by Vornoff, but a relatively small amount would be necessary to make a lethal solution; therefore, if it had been used on Devore, the remainder must exist somewhere, if it had not been destroyed. 

There was also Macford's alibi. None knew better than Cameron the leaking propensities of seemingly watertight alibis, if properly tested, and he was not surprised when the superintendent's showed a flaw.

It did not concern the reliability of the witnesses,

THE CENTRE STREET PROBE 

121 the manager and saleslady of the antique store. No question that he had been actually in the shop from shortly before seven until ten or fifteen minutes past, but that gave him at least five minutes in which to enter Devore's flat. "And he could have entered it without being seen," said the Police Commissioner. "He could have been in and out before Feigel came. He's the one who has the duplicate key, and, after unlocking the door, he might have left it on the latch in order to widen the field of suspicion." "Quite ingenious," said Bland, "but here's a question you seem to have overlooked: Why should he want to enter Devore's flat? Devore was dead at that time."

Yes, there seemed to be no question of that, and so 

Macford's alibi was as good as flawless. If possible for him to have entered the flat, it was impossible for him to have committed the murder, if murder it was. 

According to the medical evidence, and even allowing for Devore's diseased heart, he must have died within five minutes of being found. Yet Cameron put the question to the Chief Medical Examiner and received a fresh negative. "When I say Devore probably died from shock as much as anything else, I don't mean he died immediately," said Brockelbank. "On the contrary, I think he was fully conscious for some time and in possession of his mental, if not physical, faculties. 

If he had been struck within five minutes of being found, life would not have been wholly extinct, not by any means. Again there's the question of the poison; it could not have circulated through the system like that. No, he must have been lying there fifteen minutes or so."

To sum up, there was nothing to show that Miss

Camblin had caused the heartburnings the janitor alleged, or that the men involved in the case, apart from the relatively closer contact between Golding and Vornoff, were anything but acquaintances who had got to know one another through propinquity and Miss Camblin. Either this was the truth, or there was a conspiracy of silence, or Miss Camblin had played her cards so well that each man thought himself the only favoured one.

And so all that the inquest could produce was the verdict that Henry Devore, alias Parker Dinsmore, had come to his death by snakebite, though whether accidental or not it was beyond their power to say. 

Thus matters stood when there occurred the second mysterious death at the Abercorn Arms.

CHAPTER XII  

"WELL, this lets Macford out," said the District 

Attorney, "and so I offer my condolences." "Why?" demanded Cameron. "Because you hoped to hang Devore's death on him. You wanted to prove to me that the obvious was right this time. It would support your argument about the cleverness of Devore's plots, because they're always obvious." "I've had more to think about than that," said the 

Commissioner of Police. "I wish I'd nothing to do but camp on a case as I did at the Sûreté. There are advantages in being only a subordinate. Your office and my Department don't seem to have distinguished themselves in this matter so far." "Nobody can do the impossible," said Bland equably. "And there may be nothing to distinguish, after all, but a willing and irresponsible snake." "Snake your grandmother!" said Cameron, suddenly irritable. "We've had the place searched from roof to cellar, or so I've ordered, more than once." "Yes, but not every apartment," retorted Bland.

The second victim was Herman Meier, the wholesale fruiterer who lived in the flat above Devore, and, like the latter, lived alone. He had come home at midnight and, almost an hour later, the switchboard 123

telephone had rung. The hall-boy went up, but could not get in. The superintendent was out, as he had been all evening, and Jones went for the janitor. The latter entered Devore's flat, now no longer locked, and went up the fire escape. He got in through the window of the kitchenette. The place was in darkness and he stumbled over something in the bedroom. It was the wholesale fruiterer, who lay on the floor in his pyjamas.

He smelled strongly of liquor and was evidently in great agony. He said something about being bitten, then lost consciousness and never regained it. 

Such were the bald facts that reached Cameron and 

Bland over the wire from Police Headquarters, as the

District Attorney was about to leave his friend's house, where they had spent the tail-end of the evening together. And, in Cameron's car, they were now on their way to the Abercorn Arms. 

Donald Macford overtook them as they entered the vestibule. Inquiring what had brought them there, and at such an hour, he seemed appalled at the answer. "Meier ? Another one !" he exclaimed incredulously. "Surely you don't mean it? When did it happen? How? Good God, this will ruin the house!"

His Scotch ancestry seemed to be asserting itself, his principal thoughts financial; yet as he was part owner of the Abercorn Arms it was a serious enough matter to him.

It was almost a repetition of the scene the night Devore died, except that there was no patrolman on guard outside, or precinct detective inside, while the whole machinery of the law was already assembled. The janitor had communicated direct with Centre Street, and Corrigan himself had turned out. Once again there were curious whispering groups in the halls, and

outside Meier's flat they came upon Mrs. Royal and her niece, both in wonderful semi-négligée. "Oh, Mr. Macford, I'm so glad you're here at last!" exclaimed Miss Camblin. "Isn't it too terrible, and what has really happened? We haven't been able to learn a thing. Is Mr. Meier really dead? They won't let us in, and he was such a dear friend. There's a perfect ogre, with red hair, in there, who snaps and snarls and slams doors in our faces. I'm sure we've the right to help all we can." "My dear, this is the Police Commissioner," said 

Mrs. Royal, "and he'll soon put that rude person in his place. Mr. Cameron, meet my little Madeline." "O-o-h," said Miss Camblin, with an upward lingering glance from under long dark lashes. Then she offered, a warm, white, dimpled little hand that seemed almost to coo like a homing dove as it nestled confidingly for a long moment in Cameron's. "I've heard ever so much about you from Auntie," she added with a pensive, childlike smile. "And I did so want to meet you the other night. I think Police Commissioners are just too wonderful for anything. And now, please, mayn't we go in and see poor Mr. Meier ?" "No, certainly not." And Miss Camblin looked like a brutally slapped child until Cameron added: "It's not a sight for women, and nobody can do anything." "There, it's just what I said!" exclaimed Mrs.

Royal, and shivered so that her tide of fat seemed to ebb and flow all over her. "He's dead, and he died like poor Mr. Devore. It's that serpent again. Ugh! 

I declare I won't live in the place a day longer if it isn't found and destroyed at once. I'll break my lease, and you may sue me or do anything you like,

Mr. Macford. I don't mind cockroaches and things, but I draw the line at deadly serpents. I'm all of a tremble when I think of it, and now I wouldn't set foot in that flat if you strung every hair of my head with diamonds. But I do wonder what poor Mr. 

Meier looks like." 

Morbid curiosity, the magnet that can draw even refined women to the most revolting and dreadful scenes that seemed to be the motive behind Mrs. 

Royal's anxiety to aid, if not Miss Camblin's. Perhaps the latter really wished to be of service, or to figure in the limelight. At all events they were not pleased at being treated precisely like the other tenants and denied admittance to the chamber of horrors, in spite of Mrs. Royal's putative intimacy with the Police Commissioner.

Meier's flat was the same as Devore's, but though leased furnished, and thus having a certain similarity, the individual tastes of the respective tenants had been catered for. Thus in the front room there was no desk and bookcase, as in Devore's, Meier preferring lounges and easychairs, the radio, pianola, and talking-machine instead of books. It was easy to understand, if it were true, why the novelist, if engaged in correcting proofs, had words with Meier over the noise the latter made.

All the old familiar faces-the vultures, as Cameron called them-now crowded the front room, so that the 

Police Commissioner felt as though re-living the past.

There was Dr. Brockelbank and Varcoe, Delaven and 

Maddox and O'Reilly, Professor Vornoff, the janitor, and the hall-boy. And now here were Macford, Bland, and himself. Dr. Golding was missing from the picture; the janitor had summoned Professor Vornoff,

though he had learned, he said, from Devore's case, that no doctor could do anything.

Professor Vornoff, half-dressed and wearing an atrocious pair of carpet slippers, now came forward, gesticulating in his voluble and graphic manner. "It is the black mamba again, gentlemen! But yes, I am sure of it. Extraordinary, inexplicable if you like, but there you are." "Where was he bitten-in the neck?" asked Cameron. "No," said Brockelbank, "the leg." "What's this we heard about liquor?" queried the 

District Attorney. "Was he drunk?" "Not when he came home," said Corrigan, "though the hall-boy says he was pretty well oiled, and not for the first time. He finished here what he'd begun outside, treated himself to a few hookers. Devore had nothing on him in the way of private stock." "There's no question that he was under the influence of alcohol," said the Chief Medical Examiner. "It served to keep him living. Although unconscious, and beyond all aid, he only passed out a few minutes ago." "I always suspected," remarked Macford, "that he drank a lot, far more than Devore. I think perhaps he was that hopeless case—a secret drinker." "Well, we all have to do it secret these days," said

Corrigan, and led the way into the adjoining room, where the unfortunate Meier now lay stretched upon the bed.

He did not present so horrible a sight as had Devore, though a less dignified one, being short and corpulent and fond of violent colours even in night apparel. But his neck and face were not discoloured and swollen, WATLA TY OF aries,

as was the case with Devore, though his expression was equally unpleasant to look upon. It seemed compounded of maudlin astonishment, fear and pain.

But the swelling appeared to be confined mainly to his right leg, where, in front of the thigh and midway between knee and groin, there were two punctures.

And these, as Brockelbank pointed out, were not only of the same measurements as in Devore's case, but had the same downward course "It must have happened in the dark," said Corrigan, "and I guess he never saw what bit him. There'd be no sense in switching off the light unless he was ready to step into bed. You see here's the wall telephone, not in the front room, as in Devore's flat. He gropes his way to it, and then collapses after calling Jones." "Yes, sir, that's where I fell over him," said the janitor, pointing to the space between the foot of the bed and the wall telephone. "And the receiver was still hanging off the hook." "What exactly did he say?" asked Cameron. "He mumbled something about fetching a doctor quick, for he'd been bit by a snake." "Are you sure he actually used the word snake ?" "Yes, sir, he didn't only say it once, but a lot of times. It seemed to be on his mind, as if he couldn't say nothin' else. When I smelled the booze I thought mebbe he had the D.T.'s, or had been thinking too much about Devore. This was in the dark, you understand, and then I turned on the light and saw he'd really been bit. He mumbled something about the window and the ice-box and the dumbwaiter-all jumbled up like and with no sense to it." "We've been through the whole place,” said Corrigan. "The dumbwaiter door's fastened tight, and the only

snakes in the ice-box are the kind that come in bottles. 

Yes, that window's open, but a real snake can't climb twenty feet up a wall." "But with the help of a drainpipe, yes," said Professor Vornoff. "And that drainpipe is very near.

I have looked and examined. The window, you will remember, was open in Devore's flat, and we have the same drainpipe, yes."

The window referred to was the one by the bed; it was open a few inches from the bottom, but not from the top. A high-backed chair stood by it on which were Meier's clothes. It was quite evident, of course, that he had been ready to retire, although, as the Chief Medical Examiner pointed out, it was impossible to say how a drunken man might go about such a proceeding. "If he hadn't been drunk," added the doctor, "he should have been quite able to explain what happened, certainly to the hall-boy, if not to the janitor." "And didn't he?" asked Cameron, looking searchingly at Hamilton Jones. "No, sir, I could hardly understand a word he said.

All I could make out was something about a doctor and help. He seemed to be talking through a blanket." "Are you sure it was Mr. Meier talking?" pursued the Police Commissioner. "Why, yes, sir," replied the hall-boy, looking astonished. "You recognized his voice, even though it sounded so queer?" "No-no, I can't say I did, sir.” "Then how did you know it was Mr. Meier speaking?" "Because he said so." "I see," nodded Cameron.

"It's quite possible," said Brockelbank, "that, as in Devore's case, he wouldn't have been able to go in search of help even if he had been sober. On the other hand, he may have been organically sound, unlike Devore, and quite able to have done a great many things if he hadn't been fuddled with drink." "But, drunk or sober, he could have done nothing to save himself," said Vornoff, with what seemed an almost morbid satisfaction. "For the black mamba there is no cure. True, he took much longer to die than Devore, but die he did. And so it is with every victim." "Has the roof ever been searched?" asked Bland. 

And Cameron turned to Corrigan, Corrigan to O'Reilly,

O'Reilly to Delaven, and Delaven to Maddox. No, it had not been searched. No one had thought of the roof itself in connection with a ground-floor apartment. "You can't tell me," said Corrigan defensively, "that a snake climbed up there after biting Devore, and then comes down to have a go at Meier, and then climbs up again. That would be some snake." "Improbable, I grant you," said Vornoff, "but not impossible. No, far from it. And I have known these wicked ones to do even the impossible. For guile and cunning man himself is not their equal." "But," said Cameron, "no snake could exist outdoors during the sort of weather we've been having. 

It's more like winter than spring. It would be torpid, if not frozen stiff. The question of food isn't vital, but warmth is." "Ah, now you touch the real heart of the objection," nodded the Professor. "Warmth indeed is vital. And so we rule out the roof, unless there is some snug nook there, perhaps near a steampipe. But there remains

the cellar, any number of places. It could enter any open window within reach of that drainpipe. "You look incredulous, Mr. Cameron? You think we have human intelligence to deal with, not reptilian instinct ? You think it is the black mamba stolen so mysteriously from me? There is nothing new even in crime, you say, and it is an improved 

Carhart case? Well, and so you may be right. But 

I ask you this: Why should Mr. Meier be struck in the leg instead of a more quickly vulnerable spot such as the neck?" "Yes, why?" said the Police Commissioner moodily. "Does one stab a man in the leg ?" pursued Vornoff. "Human intelligence and capability would not select such a place, but a reptile must strike where it can. 

Devore, he panics and falls; but here we have no box to open, and Mr. Meier, he does not panic and fall." "You didn't hear anything unusual?” "Me, I was asleep," said Vornoff. "The janitor, he awakes me. But I say you this, it happens in the dark, and Mr. Meier, he does not know his danger until too late. He is standing up, and perhaps he steps on the wicked one as he is about to enter the bed. Who knows? But the point is, he is bitten precisely where nine out of ten victims are bitten, because a snake, big or little, can only raise just so much of its length from the ground." "It's a very interesting point," nodded Cameron, who had listened with close attention. "As you say, the leg's a queer place for anyone to select-but was it selected? If it happened in the dark... You see? 

And any spot would prove vulnerable. The point is also that if a snake could enter and leave this flat, so

could a human being. There's the fire escape, if not the drainpipe." "It couldn't be used from the court, sir," said the janitor, "unless the fold-up section was let down. 

And I'd have seen anybody who tried it. My bedroom's there, and I hadn't turned in." "But you couldn't see anyone who came down from a flat above," said the Police Commissioner. "Apart from the place being in darkness, there was plenty of time for a person to have escaped that way, or they could have gone down the stairs while Jones was going for you. The door being locked signifies nothing, for it locks automatically."

They passed at length into the kitchenette, where, on the table, Corrigan pointed out unnecessarily a halfempty bottle of brandy, a siphon of soda and a glass. An evening paper and a cigar butt supported the idea that Meier had sat down and completed the convivial proceedings begun elsewhere. As evidence of the maudlin condition he had achieved, cigar ashes were piled on the table instead of in the tray, while the siphon had been squirted on the cloth. Evidently he had been fond of fruit, despite the fact that he got his living from it, for there were quantities of all kinds in evidence, including a big crate of oranges and bananas.

Varcoe, who had remained in the front room with Delaven and O'Reilly, searching anew for fingerprints or other evidence, now appeared with the triumphant announcement that he had found something of great importance.

The something which the Assistant District Attorney had found was a letter that everyone else had oyerlooked. He discovered it on a casual table in the

front room, hidden between two piles of talkingmachine records that Meier had been too lazy to replace in the cabinet. Varcoe, very keen on his job, naturally plumed himself on scoring off such old hands as 

O'Reilly and Delaven, not to mention Corrigan himself. It was seldom, in their friendly rivalry, that the 

District Attorney's office got one up on the Homicide Bureau. "Meier must have laid it there," said Varcoe, "and it slipped down and he forgot where he'd put it. I caught a glimpse of something white and thought

I'd have a look. It's well I did, for we'd never have found it." "Oh, it was bound to be found sooner or later," said 

Corrigan shortly. "Couldn't help itself being found, once we started moving things."

There was no envelope, no address, no date; simply a single sheet of cheap paper, on which the following had been printed crudely in pencil : 

Warning. The Ides of March are gone, so also Devore, but Red Rum Myer remains. "Can you beat it? Who the devil is Red Rum 

Myer?" exclaimed Bland in a tone of exasperation. "We can't get track of anybody by that name. Was he Herman Meier ?" "I'm familiar, of course, with Mr. Meier's writing," said Macford, "but I never saw his printing. And why should he write this to himself any more than Devore should? Wouldn't this go to show that Devore didn't write the other?" "It may show anything you like," rasped Corrigan, scratching his head violently. "But Devore died too

soon. He ought to have lived long enough to put all this in one of his yarns. When you get snakes climbing drainpipes and acting brainy like college professorswell, maybe the snake wrote it. I'm ready to believe anything." "And I'm ready to believe," said the Police Commissioner, "that if this case isn't solved pretty soon, you and I had better resign, Corrigan."

CHAPTER XIII DEVELOPMENTS 

CENTRE STREET redoubled its efforts under the spur of the Police Commissioner and the goad of public criticism. The Abercorn Arms, now something of a national institution, was given a raking over from roof to cellar, and this time the tenants themselves assisted, being only too glad to have the authorities search their apartments. For a state of fear, bordering on panic, had descended upon the place, and Mrs. 

Royal was not the only one who threatened to leave.

But the result of all this renewed activity was nil, no reptile presenting itself obligingly for identification. 

The message found in Meier's apartment had been compared with the address on the box delivered to Devore, but, apart from the fact that both were printed, there was no resemblance. The suggestion that it might be a code yielded no result to the experts who were still engaged in trying to read into it something that evidently was not there. The fact that no envelope could be found was susceptible of two explanations: either Meier, for some unknown reason, had written it himself, or he had received it some considerable time before his death and the envelope had been destroyed.

The Commissioner of Police seemed to have Donald Macford still at the back of his mind, for, as he pointed 135

out to Bland, the fire escape could have been reached through Devore's flat, as the janitor had demonstrated. "Macford might have gone out for the purpose of establishing this alibi," said Cameron. "He could have come back and got into Devore's flat without the hall-boy seeing him, and left when Jones went upstairs. In fact, it might have been Macford who 'phoned, Meier being too far gone even to talk. You heard the evidence about the voice." "So that's why you asked about it so particularly? 

But why 'phone at all, advertise the crime?” "To facilitate his escape, of course," retorted 

Cameron impatiently. "It would get the hall-boy out of the way. And for the tragedy to be discovered then, when it was known Macford was out . . . You see?

He could have gone up and down the fire escape without the janitor being any the wiser." "Ingenious," said Bland. "You deserve to be right, Kirk, the way you've stuck manfully to your original guns. But I don't think you are; no, and I never did. 

What I do think is that if you could put Donald 

Macford out of your mind, once and for all, you'd have a much better chance of finding the real culprit." "It doesn't seem to have worked in your case.' "I haven't the trained mind, but you have," said 

Bland. "I never pretended to be a whale at crime problems. So far as I'm personally concerned, I'm afraid these two murders will never be solved-if they are murders and not accidents." 

"Accidents? You ask too much of coincidence." "Perhaps. But, if they're murders, you're capable of solving them, if you only will. I think you've been concentrating too much on Macford to the detriment, or benefit, of others."

DEVELOPMENTS 

137 

The Police Commissioner denied this, saying he had not the opportunity of concentrating on anything but the multifarious duties of his office. A hundred and one things were demanding his attention, and he had thought the District Attorney's office and Homicide Bureau quite capable of coping with the matter. "If, to me, Macford's the logical one, and has been from the first," he finished, "it's because I haven't had time to seriously consider anyone else." "Yes, you've followed the line of least resistance. 

Why? Your multifarious duties didn't stop you taking a very personal hand in the Horder murder case. But now you've a bee in your bonnet, and you can't see anyone but Macford." "Well, that may be so, mon ami. None of us is immune from bees. But if we don't get some direct action soon, I'll have to take a personal hand in the case. 

And, at any rate, this second alibi of Donald Macford's needs as strict an investigation as the first."

Investigation proved, however, beyond all possible doubt that there was not the semblance of a flaw in the superintendent's alibi. He had attended a lodge meeting of a fraternal society to which he belonged, and, on the way home, stopped to talk with the patrolman on beat. There could be absolutely no question that he had left the Abercorn Arms long before Meier came home, and that he was still absent when the alarm was raised. It was proved, not merely by one witness, but a dozen or more, that he could have been nowhere near the apartment house that evening, nor that he could have even met Meier outside.

The next development which tended to remove the last shred of suspicion from Donald Macford concerned

the box received by Devore, the mystery attaching to it being dissipated. The putative owner, not proprietor, of a neighbouring speakeasy, perhaps feeling that silence was no longer golden, now yielded up the truth, while under a cross-examination by the District 

Attorney. This individual, the Belshaw whom Corrigan had mentioned, gave some surprising information, apart from the fact that the box had come from his place and contained nothing but wine. "Devore was a regular customer," he said, "and I guess he got all his booze through me. He had a taste for good red wine mostly, and, if he didn't step in and carry a bottle or two home, I sent it round boxed up like that. If he didn't happen to be in, it was left with the hall-boy. Devore first came to me through Meier-Yes, 'Heinie' Meier, who lived at the 

Abercorn Arms." "And did you supply him with liquor too?" 

Belshaw smiled. "No, he supplied himself. It's 

Herman Meier who was behind me, see? Sure, his money was in the game." "Oh," said Bland. "Was any of Devore's money in it ?" "I don't know. Meier may have been a syndicate, and he may have backed other joints. All I know is, he backed me and got his big percentage. It was a sideline, like it is with lots of rich, respectable guysyes, and women too-and it paid him better than fruit." "Did you ever have any trouble with him?"

Belshaw had been expecting this question, and he now explained that it was one of the reasons why he had not volunteered information before, ignoring the fact that he was not actually volunteering it now. "Yes, I'd trouble with him, off and on," he said.

DEVELOPMENTS 

139 "Nothing to speak of, you understand, but folks might try to make it out worse than it was, and tell you I was his enemy. Nothing of the kind. Meier always wanted too much for his money." "A common failing," said the District Attorney. "So you quarrelled about that? Anything else ?" "No, sir; that was the main thing. He always kept under cover, slick as a fox, and I was expected to do all the dirty work and take all the risks. If we'd been pulled it would have been me who'd have gone up, not him. I couldn't have fixed a thing on him. We'd words at times over the percentage he was dragging down, but never any real trouble. I needed his backing and pull, and he knew it." "Did you ever know him to spell his name with a 'y' ?” "No, sir, and I never knew he had the nickname of 'Red Rum' either. But then I knew darned little about him and his affairs outside our partnership. 

He was a very secretive duck, but, from what he let drop now and then, I know he lived in the tropics for some time, and that it was at Nassau, in the Bahamas, he first became interested in rum-running as a profitable sideline."

Bland passed on the result of this interview to the 

Police Commissioner. "So you see," he said, "the mystery of that box is explained, and Macford had nothing to do with it. 

It's quite possible, of course, that this man Belshaw knows more than he says, but, even if he doesn't, his information suggests an entirely new line of inquiry." "It suggests an end to the snake theory, at any rate." "Why? Even if we can't find any trace of one, the fact that Meier lived in the tropics-well, he may

have made a deadly enemy there that Belshaw would know nothing about. If his statement shows that there wasn't a snake in that box, it doesn't show that one couldn't have entered Devore's flat the way it entered Meier's." "It shows more than that," said Cameron. "What becomes now of Vornoff's theory? If Devore didn't 'panic and fall', how did he come to be struck in the neck? You eliminate the possibility of a snake being in that box, and you eliminate more than one logical explanation." 

The District Attorney pondered this argument. “Well," he said at length, "we've only Belshaw's word for it that there was wine in that box. For that matter it might have been tampered with after it left his place. But Macford couldn't have done it; he wouldn't have had time. It wasn't there when the hall-boy went up for Miss Camblin, and it was there when he came down. Of course we've only 

Jones' word for that too. Belshaw says that the man who delivered it isn't sure of the exact time." "So there you are," said Cameron. "You can't be sure of anything." "Yes, we're sure of Macford. He wouldn't have had a chance to tamper with the box, even if it was delivered earlier than the hall-boy says. It's Jones who would have had charge of it." He "I never believed the box had anything to do with it," said the Police Commissioner. "Devore opened it and laid those wine bottles where we saw them. didn't put any near the radiator because he wasn't going to drink. He was going back to the front room to read or write, and I believe he did."

DEVELOPMENTS 

141 "What! You mean you don't think he was in the kitchenette when he was struck?" "No, I don't, and never did. That would explain the inexplicable-why the light was burning in the kitchenette and nowhere else. That point, as I've said, has intrigued me more than any other. If I've learned one thing in all my years of criminal investigation, it's the futility of trying to imitate Nature." "Meaning?" "Meaning that a natural sequence of events can't be faked so as to defy detection. The cleverest hand always produces a flaw, however minute. It's humanly impossible not to. I think the flaw in this case is the absence of light in the front room." "But what would it matter where Devore died ?" "Not where he died, Jerry; where he received the fatal wound.” "Well, what's the difference? Why should anyone want him to be found in the kitchenette ?" "Ah, that's the question, my friend. If we could find an answer to that, perhaps we should find an answer to everything." "You're beyond me," said Bland, shaking his head. "Front room or back room, I can't see a blessed bit of difference. But does this idea of yours, whatever it may be, apply also to Meier? Was he killed in the wrong room, and, if not, why not?" "Forgive me for not being an oracle," sighed 

Cameron. "But I have an idea, yes. I sat up most of last night considering this case, and it came to me like a small glow-worm in the darkness. But it is so small that it's not worth discussing at present. It may grow brighter, or it may go out. I don't know." "In other words, you refuse to tell me anything

about it until you're good and ready? Well, all right. I'm used to that kind of treatment. God be praised that at least you've got the glimmering of an idea. I told you you'd hit on something once you washed your mind clean of Macford. evidence suggests something, eh?" Belshaw's "Perhaps. But if it shouldn't open up a new productive line of inquiry, there remains Garwinn. I imagine he could tell us something useful if he could or would talk intelligently. By the way, does this man Belshaw know Mrs. Meier? Did you ask him anything about her ?" "Yes, but he doesn't know where she is. He understands she's in California." "Yes, so do we, but we can't trace her. If she reads the papers she must know her husband is dead, yet she has given no sign. However, let us go up to Saint

Luke's and see if there's any improvement in Garwinn's condition. I should like a personal chat with him.”

But Paul Garwinn was dead from concussion of the brain, died almost as the two visitors entered the ward. 

The injury, which another might have survived, had proved too much for his enfeebled constitution. He had not added a single word to his first and only statement, and, following that brief rally, slipped gradually into the coma from which there is no earthly waking. "So that's that," said Cameron with compressed lips. "One avenue of inquiry definitely closed for ever. Yet perhaps he could have told us no more than we know already." "That wouldn't be much. What did you want to ask him particularly?”

Cameron did not reply; he had suddenly grown

DEVELOPMENTS 

143 tense, alert like a setter flushing a bird. They were standing in one of the long white corridors of the hospital, waiting for the elevator, and there was no one in sight but a female figure at the far end. Yet the 

Police Commissioner's eyes had begun to sparkle as though he had caught sight suddenly of an old and dear friend. "Do you know her ?" asked Bland curiously. "Who is she?" "Who is what?" asked Cameron blankly. "The lady at the end of the corridor." "Never mind who she is. Let us go to the Abercorn

Arms. My idea is growing, becoming quite bright.”

CHAPTER XIV  

WHAT had caused Cameron's idea to grow brighter as he expressed it? Who was the lady in the corridor? The District Attorney saw that the figure was that of a woman, not a man, but he had seen nothing more.

Had this woman anything to do with it, or had something else supplied the necessary stimulant to 

Cameron's imagination? Bland pondered the problem as they motored from Cathedral Heights. He knew it was no use questioning his friend; when fired with an idea this strange, temperamental man, with the brilliant and superbly trained mind, might either adopt his Coquelin mood or the manner of a sphinx, in either case totally uncommunicative. 

He was sphinx-like now, reclining on his spine, slouch hat drawn over eyes and apparently fast asleep.

The District Attorney tried to visualize that long white corridor, bare as one's hand but for themselves, the distant figure by the window and an occasional reception chair or two. No, there had been nothing else. But wait; of course there was the elevator, coming up in answer to their ring, and the long, looped cable. . . . Yes, reminiscent of a snake. Was that 144

the association of ideas? the cable-snake.. 

Elevator-dumbwaiter ; "Singular," murmured Cameron suddenly without opening.his eyes, "that Devore, a tall man, should be struck in the neck, while Meier, a short man, should be struck in the leg. Wouldn't you think it should have been rather the other way about?" "Granting a human agency, yes. And doesn't that fact tend to eliminate the human agency theory altogether?" "Agreed. But does it eliminate human agency acting in conjunction with inhuman agency?” "Yes, I see," nodded Bland. "A snake... Look here, if it had been hanging head downward 

You remember that night in Devore's?" added the 

District Attorney quickly, as he became fired with an idea he considered his own, but which might have been a telepathic borrowing from the man beside him. "You remember Macford and the cord of the portiere ?" "I haven't forgotten the incident." "Well, supposing the brute had been up there originally; been placed there with malice aforethought? Devore wouldn't need to have been lying prone when he was bitten." "I never thought he was," said Cameron, and apparently fell asleep again. 

But a few minutes later he again murmured, as though thinking aloud, "Has that comma any significance ?" "What are you talking about now?" "A comma. And a comma, my fat friend, is a period with a little curly tail. Comets have tails and so have commas, and one may cause as much trouble

in a way as the other. If the tail of a comet should hit you, what would happen?"’ 

Bland realized that the Commissioner of Police was passing into his Coquelin mood. The Gallic streak was coming to the top, generally a sign that he was making progress in the solution of some difficult problem. At such times the District Attorney was quite content to be the butt of any nonsense Cameron might care to talk. But was this nonsense or a parable? The tail of a comet . . "You, as a legal luminary," said the Police Commissioner, sitting up suddenly and clapping Bland on the knee, "should know what a big difference a little comma can make. In a legal document, say a will or contract, if one should put it down instead of a period -and the mistake is so easily made-it might cause a great deal of trouble and suffering, yes? It might give an entirely different meaning to the clause or phrase, eh?" "Yes, of course. But- "Wait!

It might even cause, for instance, our little Madeline to lose her fortune." "You mean the fortune Miss Camblin's going to inherit from her aunt ?" "No, I mean the fortune she has inherited already from Mr. Meier." "What!" exclaimed Bland, staring at the other. "Meier? You're joking, of course." "Am I? I don't think so." "But when did she inherit it ?” "Obviously when the gentleman died. All that doesn't go legally by law to the absent wife, goes to

Miss Camblin, unconditionally, for ever, until death do us part-or whatever the quaint phrasing of a will is.”

"But there wasn't any will!" protested the District 

Attorney. "Meier died intestate, like Devore. I ought to know if anyone should." "Assuredly, but you didn't. It was only discovered to-day, a new will that Meier's lawyer knew nothing about. You know Centre Street has long ears, though it's not always an ass. You were so busy with Belshaw, and it slipped my mind." "Liar," said the District Attorney. "No wonder you found an idea like a glow-worm in the dark-not last night, but to-day. This puts a different complexion on the case, even if it doesn't provide a motive for Devore's death. I dare say Varcoe's heard of this development; I wasn't at the office since Belshaw left. Yes, a different complexion on the whole case.

I understand now why you're going to the Abercorn Arms." "Do you ?" said Cameron with a provocative glance. "However, I should like to congratulate Miss Camblin on her good fortune, for I'm sure Mrs. Meier won't. 

I wonder if it was on our little Madeline's account that husband and wife separated." "They separated before Meier went to live at the 

Abercorn Arms." "I know. But mayn't he have known Miss Camblin before he went there? It's remarkable how much trouble perfectly innocent and well-meaning people with great hearts can make. And so unfortunate. 

Now I'm sure Miss Gower and Miss Hamilton aren't the only ones who dislike Miss Camblin." "Well, but what has all this got to do with a comma or a comet ?" "We shall see, I hope, in due time," said Cameron, as their automobile stopped at the Abercorn Arms.

"Patience, my friend. A comma, the light in the kitchenette, the Ides of March-I have an idea they are all connected most logically and inevitably, if only we can discover the link. Yes, and many other little things that seem incongruous, puzzling, and bizarre.

The most beautiful picture can look the same if cut up in pieces. And this is a beautiful picture, I think, if only we can piece it together. Yes, a beautiful crime picture. Parker Dinsmore himself could not have done better."

But Miss Camblin was not at home, and again her aunt deplored the unfortunate circumstance, apparently taking it for granted that this was a social call on the part of these new acquaintances. Evidently 

Mrs. Royal was accustomed to new acquaintances losing no time in calling, and, if they were desirable, she was not one to stand on ceremony. "My niece is down at Mr. Gloz's office," she explained. "The most extraordinary thing has happened-poor, dear Mr. Meier has remembered her in his will." "Very handsomely too, I believe," said Cameron. "I wished to offer my congratulations. Mr. Gloz is your lawyer?" "Oh, yes. Being a neighbour, you see. You could have knocked me down with a feather," declared Mrs.

Royal, sticking to her usual clichés and rolling her 

Pekinese eyes. "When Mr. Gloz rung us up and said that Mr. Meier's lawyer had found a will-I declare it was just like something in the fillums." "You weren't expecting it, then?" said Bland drily. "Expecting it? Certainly not, no more than I'd expect my dear dead mother to rise from her grave.

It's one of the greatest surprises of my life, and little 

Madeline's too. 'Auntie', she said, 'after this anything

can happen. Fancy poor dear Mr. Meier remembering me like that'. And then she burst out crying all over again. She's so sensitive and tender-hearted, you know." 

Bland waited for a lead from Cameron, but waited in vain. That strange man seemed to be stricken with another of his sphinx-like silences. He was looking dreamily about the room as though his thoughts were more concerned with objets d'art. Mrs. Royal's flat was luxuriously furnished, and it conveyed the idea that here was the discriminating accretion of years. "In that case," said the District Attorney, feeling that the conversational lead had been shouldered unfairly on him, "why should Mr. Meier have left the bulk of his estate to your niece?" 66 "Why indeed?" said Mrs. Royal, waving her pudgy hands. ''Auntie', said little Madeline, 'what have I ever done to deserve it? It makes me feel awful in a way, as if my friendship had to be paid for. And you know what some people will say'. 'Never mind, my dear', I told her, 'people talk anyway, even about angels white as the driven snow. You know, and I know, that never a mercenary thought ever entered your pretty head, and that's all that matters. A clean conscience is above rubies'." 

Mrs. Royal waxing confidential, it appeared then that her niece had resolutely declined to become an heiress until Mrs. Royal, as her bounden duty, pointed out what an insult this would be to a dead man. Her niece must accept the generous gift, though afterwards she could give it to charity if she liked. "The fact of the matter is," added Mrs. Royal, "Mr. Meier was a lonely man, and little Madeline cheered him up with her bright and sympathetic ways.

He was never understood by his wife-he told me so himself and he appreciated the friendship of my niece. He was old enough to be her father, of course, and he looked on her as a dear daughter. He knew she was an orphan, dependent on my little income, and so, having no relatives he cared about, he decided to leave his money to her without saying a word to us of his intentions. After all, why shouldn't he if he wanted to? That's the way it happened, I think, and so it really isn't so extraordinary at all.” "No, not when you come to understand it properly," said Bland. "But it's quite possible, of course, that

Mrs. Meier won't understand it properly. She may contest the will, charge undue influence." 

Mrs. Royal's eyes changed from those of a Pekinese to those of a terrier. No longer were they large and lambent, but narrowed, hard, and watchful. And her indolent, good-natured voice suddenly became strident. "Let her bring any charge she likes! I'm ready for her!" she exclaimed. "We'd nothing to do with it, not a thing, and she can't prove we had. Meier wasn't crazy either. Mr. Gloz says that will's as good as wheat, and it couldn't be broken with an axe. Meier's lawyer says the same. Let her try, that's all! I'll fight her for the principle of the thing. She don't deserve to get even her widow's share, the shameful way she treated poor Mr. Meier.” "Were you personally acquainted with her?" "I was not, and I wouldn't want to be."

The dialogue languished, Cameron declining to add a word. Mrs. Royal had become formidable; her eyes were still like a terrier's and her vast bosom rose and fell as she breathed. The idea that it was a

pleasant social visit had vanished, if it had ever existed, and she was ready to do battle. "If you two have come to badger me about this business, you can get out," she declared. "I know my rights, and I don't care how high and mighty you are. If you want any more information you can go to Mr. Gloz." 

They got out. "Well, you congenital clam ?" demanded the District 

Attorney indignantly. "Why didn't you say whatever's on your mind? Why let me do all the talking?” "Because you talk so well, Jerry. You can get anybody's goat without half trying. That smile and uplifted eyebrow of yours . . . And when people lose their tempers . . . Oh, you did it far better than 

I could. I'm much too amiable." "Did what?" ... "Why, revealed the real Mrs. Royal." "Is that all you came here for-to get the old lady mad?" "No, I came mainly to see a flat." "Oh, Devore's?" "I'm not interested in Devore's. Let us have a look at Meier's."

There was a plainclothes man from Police Headquarters in Meier's flat, and there had been one, night and day, since the tragedy. Cameron had made a particular point of this, and the men were chosen carefully. There were four of them and they worked in six-hour details.

After exchanging a word with the detective on duty, who said that, as per instructions, no one had been allowed into the flat but Meier's lawyer, Cameron passed into the bedroom. Apart from the fact that

the dead man's clothes had been removed and placed with his other personal belongings, the place presented the same appearance as on the night of the tragedy. 

The Police Commissioner went to the window and looked into the court. He stood there for a long time in Olympic aloofness, while Bland watched him curiously. But whatever was passing in the strange man's mind, found no outlet in words; he seemed to sink deeper and deeper into his slough of thought and silence. Finally he turned, with sightless eyes and bowed head, sat down on the bed and began to fumble for pipe and pouch. "Isn't it strange," he remarked at length, "that

Meier should have been a scientific man, while Devore wasn't?" "How do you mean scientific ?" "Well, perhaps I express it badly," said Cameron, nursing a knee and blowing meditative smoke. "I don't mean he was a man of science. No, he was a man of bananas and oranges, while Devore was a man of mathematics and books. And yet it was Meier, not Devore, who had knowledge of a certain scientific truth. But so it often is with practical men. Writers are seldom practical, else they wouldn't be writers, I suppose. It's not a surefire way of making money." "But what scientific truth did Meier know, and how do you know he knew it ?" "Observation, my friend. My eyes assure me that it is so. And my eyes, unlike those of most people, do not tell me lies." "Confound your eyes," said the District Attorney, his curiosity clamouring for satisfaction. "What is it you see? Can't you give a plain answer for once to a plain question ?"

"Not to you," said the Police Commissioner, waving his pipe. "I'm trying to instruct you in the simple but necessary art of observation. Does a pupil benefit if the teacher tells the answer? What did I say the night of Devore's death? I said if the name Abercorn

Arms had been cut in the sidewalk you'd have noticed it the first time you passed. And that is so.

People refuse to see anything much above eye level, but the first time I entered this room I saw it though I didn't draw the necessary deduction. I'm not infallible, and to observe correctly and deduce correctly aren't the same. "There! I've given you a hint. And now, mon ami, put on your good spectacles and look carefully about you. I assure you that nothing in this room has been added to, nothing has been changed, except that Meier's clothes have been removed from that chair. Even the window, as you see, is still open at the bottom, and everything is as it was on the night 

Meier died. So put on your good spectacles and observe."

The District Attorney put on his glasses obediently and began a meticulous examination of the room. 

Something above eye level, that must be it. But what? He could see nothing that he had not seen before. The pictures on the walls, the window curtains, the chair, bed, telephone, a casual table, another chair-there was practically nothing else in the room. 

Was he being made a fool of? Cameron's Coquelin mood was capable of anything. "I don't see it," he said at length. "Of course you don't." "Well, no one else could see it either. There's nothing there."

"That's precisely the point," said the Police Commissioner. "What do you mean?” "That, as you express it, there's nothing there." "Then how could you have seen it?" demanded the exasperated Bland. "I saw it, I see it, but it isn't there." And Cameron grinned suddenly like a schoolboy. "There's a riddle for you." "How could it have been there if nothing has been changed or added to but Meier's clothes? Do you mean them?” "I do not," said Cameron, "though, now that you mention them, Meier's clothes have an interest all their own. 

But were they above eye level? No, no. You're hopeless, Jerry; blind as an owl and only fit for the law. Too much food. No, I won't tell you the answer." "Because there isn't any," exclaimed Bland. “I defy you to give me any sensible one. This is all tomfoolery, and you know it," he added as Cameron remained silent, serenely smoking. "You like to mystify me. But your big idea has gone flop and you haven't discovered a thing. And it's only a waste of the taxpayers' money having a detective on guard here. Why not have one in Devore's flat, while you're at it? There'd be as much sense in it."

To his surprise and discomposure, Cameron rose to the bait. "That's the most helpful suggestion you've made for a long time, my friend," he said, knocking out his pipe. "The poor taxpayers! Yes, we must consider them. These detectives have loafed long enough; they've detected nothing, and so to-night they go

back to pounding the pavement. It's a valuable suggestion because I was going to suggest it myself.” "Then," said Bland, not trying to hide his keen disappointment, "you really haven't discovered anything? This big idea of yours hasn't grown any brighter?" "On the contrary, Jerry, it's a regular conflagration. It has become so great that, instead of calling the firemen to put it out, I send away the police." "I could quarrel with you sometimes, with your childish love for mystification," declared Bland. "In plain English, what do you mean?" "I mean that to-night, with any luck, I show you the murderer of Devore and Meier. Is that plain enough?” 

The District Attorney drew in his breath sharply.

His most optimistic expectations had never visualized this rapid termination of the case, this sudden solution of the baffling mysteries. "You really mean it?" he exclaimed. "But yes," nodded Cameron, still in his Gallic manner. "We dismiss the detectives, and publicly. 

Oh, very publicly. Let it be known to the world that no longer is there any guard in Meier's flat-but you and I will be here secretly. I should prefer only one person, for two increases the risk of being seen. 

Nobody must know, neither the janitor nor hall-boy,

Macford, Vornoff, Gloz nobody. We leave publicly and return secretly. That's the idea. Meier's flat is unguarded, and so the murderer, I hope, will pay a return call at last." "But why?" "You will see, if all goes well," replied Cameron. "You've been very patient with me, mon ami, and so have patience a little longer. I should much prefer

to be alone, but you are entitled to see with your own eyes. It is an experiment, a test of a theory of mine, and it mayn't come off. So I say nothing until we see for ourselves. No, not a word." "Tell me one thing," pleaded Bland. "Have you discovered anything about Red Rum Myer ?” "I have." "And who is it?" "The murderer."

CHAPTER XV MIDNIGHT ADVENTURE 

JEROME BLAND, dining alone, never remembered being so worked up and excited over a case, experienced though he was, and his curiosity was now at white heat. His customary phlegm was so disturbed that he could hardly eat any dinner, while his mind shuttled back and forth between several different solutions of the problem.

There were so many puzzling features, questions that demanded an answer, that finally he took pencil and paper and wrote them down as they occurred to him. They seemed to fall naturally into three divisions, and this is what the paper looked like when at length he had finished.

DEVORE'S FLAT 

Of what significance is it that the light was burning in the kitchenette and nowhere else?

Why should Cameron think that Devore wasn't in the kitchenette when he received the fatal wound? 

What difference should it make where De vore was? 

Did Devore write that mysterious message, and, if so, why?

Was the door of the du mbwaiter left unfastened by accident? 

MEIER'S FLAT 

What was it that Cameron had seen, that was there and yet not there? 

What scientific fact did Meier know?

If it is important where Devore happened to be when he 157

received the fatal wound, why is it not equally important as regards Meier? Or is it? 

Why should Meier's clothes have an interest of their own?

What was the real cause of the quarrel between Devore and 

Meier? Was there any one but the one given?

Was it actually Meier who 'phoned to the hall-boy? 

GENERAL 

Who was the woman that Cameron saw at the hospital, and what has she to do with the case? 

What did Cameron hope to find out from Paul Garwinn ? 

Was Garwinn really killed by a street accident? 

Who is "Red Rum" Myer?

These were merely the salient questions, for Bland found that he might continue to jot down others indefinitely. There was a whole untouched field of conjecture concerning Mrs. Royal and her niece, their lawyer Gloz, and even Mrs. Meier.

Meanwhile the detectives had been withdrawn from Meier's flat, and everybody in the Abercorn Arms knew that no longer was there to be one there. To every appearance it was a gesture of defeat, a tacit admission on the part of the police that they could no more solve this case than they could the Devore one. "If you think the murderer will pay a visit to 

Meier's flat," Bland had said, "what's to stop it happening between the time the detective leaves and we arrive?” "It won't happen," Cameron had replied with the utmost assurance. "If the thing happens at all it will be after midnight. I'll call for you at the Sagamore about twelve, for I'll be busy until then." "Isn't there anything I can do?" "Yes, wear rubber-heeled shoes, and have Henry put up some sandwiches."

MIDNIGHT ADVENTURE 

159 And so Bland was dining alone at the club and trying to kill time until the momentous hour arrived.

A strange position for the District Attorney of New York County, to be kept in the dark like any small subordinate. But he was not resentful, for he knew his strange friend, and it was a privilege to share on any terms one of his adventures.

This Gallic streak, this love for mystery and the spectacular, must be condoned in Cameron. Nor was it solely that; Cameron had said it was only an experiment, and he would have no one else witness the failure of it. If his theory was wrong, Centre Street and the public should never know; if it was right, his subordinates should receive all the credit for it. 

A strange, genuinely modest and acutely sensitive character, this new Police Commissioner of the modern 

Baghdad, who had often masqueraded like Haroun 

Al Raschid of the old. 

Bland experienced a thrill, a sudden return of lost youth, when at length Cameron and he set out for the Abercorn Arms. He walked with more than the elasticity lent by rubber heels. Fifteen years had turned back, and once again he was on South Field opening a hole in the line while Cameron skilfully boxed an opposing end. Not so difficult now to believe that once these two had been stars on the gridiron. Cameron's stoop had gone with his spectacles, and he walked like a panther. "At half-past twelve precisely," said the Police 

Commissioner, breaking a long silence, "a lady will ring up Mr. Hamilton Jones." "How do you know?" "Because I have arranged it." "Why? And who is she?”

"It's of no importance. The point is that any female, lady or not, will do for Mr. Jones. He's a true squire of dames. This unknown admirer will keep him on a string, otherwise the wire, for at least fifteen minutes. She guarantees to. That should be quite long enough for us to wait the chance of getting in without being seen." "But he'll see us when we pass the alcove. Have you forgotten that the switchboard is so placed that he can see into the hall when he's at it?" "No, I haven't forgotten, my friend. But why should we pass the alcove? Devore's flat-" "But I thought you said it was Meier's!" "Tut, tut, Jerry. Why go up the stairs and court observation when we don't have to? I've a key to fit Devore's flat. Are you too fat to climb a fire escape? Well, but you'll have to be careful; if you missed your footing it will sound like an earthquake. 

And think what a mess you'd make !"

They kept in the shadow of the buildings across the street and took up their position in a doorway opposite the Abercorn Arms. The long cold spell had abated, but it was still cool enough for a light overcoat, and they had the collars of these turned up, the brims of their slouch hats well down. "Funny if we got pinched for a couple of secondstorey workers," commented the District Attorney as a patrolman passed on the opposite pavement. 

They could see directly through the glass doors into the marble hall of the apartment house, see Hamilton Jones reading a paper and lounging about. 

Then a taxi whirled up and deposited two fares, Dr. Golding and Miss Camblin, who went in together.

MIDNIGHT ADVENTURE 

161 "Hello," said Bland, "he must have had another night off. Seem to be pretty thick. How about sneaking in now while the hall-boy's taking them up ?”

But a moment later the squat figure of Professor Vornoff turned the corner and hurried up the steps. "How long will Golding stay?" asked Bland. “We may meet him." "Got to chance it now," said Cameron, glancing at his watch. "Come along," and they walked obliquely across the street.

It proved to be simplicity itself, and thus they demonstrated how easily anyone might have entered on the night of Devore's death, all the easier if the door was on the latch. They had a clear view of the hall, and it took but a few seconds to slip through the swing doors and wait a breathless moment while the 

Police Commissioner used the key he had had made.

From the alcove they could hear the cultivated voice of Jones carrying on with his unknown admirer. "Remember," whispered Cameron as he snapped on his torch and turned its beam on the floor, "that if there's no one overhead, the janitor's below." "I'm as lightfooted as a fairy," boasted the District 

Attorney, and went sprawling over a chair.

Amid that silence the noise sounded prodigious and, no doubt, greater than it actually was. Yet 

Cameron, carefully replacing the chair, took no chances. Smothering Bland's exclamation, and bidding him walk lightly, he steered him into the bedroom. 

Then he opened the door of the clothes closet, removed the key and locked himself and Bland inside.

Here in the dark the District Attorney began to perspire freely, his thoughts turning unwillingly but

insistently to black mambas. Then something brushed against his neck and he jumped.

He was about to begin a whispered protest against such continued unnecessary caution when there came the sound of voices, and the invisible keyhole turned into a yellow eye. The janitor, hall boy and superintendent had entered and switched on the light in the bedroom. "It was out in the street," Jones was saying in a sulky voice as the trio drew abreast of the closet. "It wasn't," retorted the janitor. "I heard it right over my head." "But who could get in here?" demanded Macford. "And who'd try to steal furniture ?" "I heard 'em," said the janitor stubbornly. "I ain't deaf. They could get in them front windows if they hadn't a skeleton." "Well, we'll see," replied the superintendent in a sceptical voice. "Have a look under that bed and round here while I go into the kitchenette." 

Silence ensued. Then: "It was out in street," said the hall-boy, and yawned. "Yeah, if it wasn't you," said the janitor. "Me? What would I be doing in here?" "I guess you could answer that better than me, angel face." "Don't you get fresh with me. And I don't want any of your insinuosities either." "Here, quit that quarrelling, you two!" came 

Macford's distant voice. 

Then somebody tried to open the closet door.

CHAPTER XVI WAITING FOR A MURDERER RIDICULOUS situation! The New York Commissioner of Police and the District Attorney cooped up in a closet, not daring to move and hardly daring to breathe, afraid of imminent discovery as any hunted criminals. "The boss locked it," came the hall-boy's ironic voice. And no doubt he thought he spoke the truth. "It was out in the street." 

The janitor stopped trying to force the door, and 

Macford returned from the back premises. If Feigel happened to ask him about locking the closet. . .

But he did not. The trio departed, the superintendent evidently in none too pleasant a humour at being roused by a false alarm. "I'm smothering," wheezed Bland. "This is worse than a Turkish bath. Let's go." But Cameron would not open the door until quite some time had passed. 

The District Attorney had evidently learned his lesson, and the accident which so nearly had meant disaster was due to anxiety and haste rather than native clumsiness. For, like many fat men, Bland really could be surprisingly light of foot and touch when so minded. 

Cameron himself could move like a shadow and, after merging a minute with the surrounding darkness, 163

he reappeared with his spotlight and a Malacca cane. 

It was the walking-stick with which Keppel, the precinct detective, had armed himself, and it spoke volumes for the Police Commissioner's memory and geographical sense that he could find it unerringly as he had. ?? 

Bland forebore to ask any questions, afraid that even a whisper might reach the vigilant janitor, but sight of the cane did not tend to relieve a mind that was now centring on creeping horrors. "Human agency acting in conjunction with inhuman Cameron's phrase recurred to him vividly and he thought of that terrifying short story The Speckled Band. Was the answer to these two crime puzzles something similar? It was all very well to discuss such things academically in a well-lighted room, but here in the dark where a man had died a terrible death .. "Come along," whispered Cameron, and led the way to the kitchenette. 

With infinite caution they raised the window and looked out into the court. A characteristic smell came up to them, that of air which never sees sunlight, and the ghosts of countless dinners. A splash of ochre on the whitewashed walls below assured them that the janitor had not yet gone to bed. Here and there, farther up the shaft, a muffed bedroom or bathroom window showed like a wrinkled yellow face in the darkness. If anyone should happen to open a window and look out . . . But that must be risked. And, thank goodness, it was not summer.

165 trembled under his weight. They entered the kitchenette of Meier's flat and paused by the window, wondering if by chance they had been seen or heard. But all was quiet. No one below them now, so they need not be so meticulously careful about walking, but they must remember that Professor Vornoff was overhead.

Cameron closed the window as silently as he had opened it, and then the beam from his torch began to creep along the floor. It rested on the great crate of oranges and bananas. The fruit must surely rot, thought Bland, if it was left there much longer. The stuff should be opened and given to the poor instead of waiting for the tedious unwinding of the legal red tape. What a big crate it was! Meier had certainly believed in the slogan of "Eat more fruit”. "We may as well wait here for a time," said the

Cameron had switched off the torch and they sat in the faint light that came from the window. Bland was not listening to the modulated voice; he was thinking of all the questions that clamoured for an answer, all the suspects in the case. 

Who was the person that Cameron expected to visit the flat? 

Why should it be visited, and especially in the dead of night? Of course the scene of his crime has a compelling attraction for a murderer-it is not a fiction of the novelists-and naturally he will select a time when the risk of detection is least. But, in that case, why choose Meier's flat instead of Devore's? Was 

Red Rum Myer man or woman? 

Was it a person at all?

The thought even crossed Bland's mind that he might be the victim of a hoax, that this was one of 

Cameron's tricks, to keep him sitting here in the dark while the real drama took place elsewhere. That strange man, when the Gallic streak came to the fore, was liable to do anything. No doubt, for all his

WAITING FOR A MURDERER 

167 quiet, casual talk, he was now enjoying Bland's mystification and uneasiness. "Did you bring those sandwiches?" asked the Police 

Commissioner. "Good!" And he munched away with apparent relish while, for once, food had no lure for the District Attorney. Instead, he brought out his cigar-case, but even this solace was denied him. "Might just as well stick a notice outside the door saying we've arrived safely, Jerry," said Cameron.

Yes, of course, the smell would linger and betray them. Bland had forgotten that palpable truth. His wits were woolgathering. "Come," said Cameron at length, glancing at the illuminated dial of his watch and then carefully removing all trace of their meal. "Half-past one; time to be moving. The front room's our objective." 

It was quite evident that he had decided beforehand where they should spend the rest of their vigil, though why this particular spot should be selected above all others, Bland could only conjecture. To the right of the door stood an ornate screen, one of the rather garish articles Meier had fancied, and behind this was a double chair of the variety known as dos-a-dos. 

Cameron motioned Bland to the seat facing in the direction of the window, while he himself occupied the one facing the screen. "Now remember," he whispered, "that, no matter what you hear, you're not to move until I switch on the light. Is that clear?"

Bland, in a flash of unwonted intuition, then saw at least one use for the Malacca cane. 

The large white button of the light switch showed clearly on the wall near the door, looking like an astonished eye in the faint street and starlight that came through the

windows. Cameron had only to reach round the screen, stretch out an arm and press the ferrule of the cane against the button. It could be done without changing his position radically, and therefore without sound.

The minutes passed, dragging interminably. The peculiar intermittent whine of the elevator, the metallic click of its door, had ceased long since. The last tenant had returned and the Abercorn Arms had gone to bed. Hamilton Jones should be on his way home to that spitfire in Harlem. Yes, the girl who had tried to "razor" him.

Cameron seemed asleep, hat drawn over eyes, the cane across his knees. He sat on his spine, silent, immovable. But Bland could not sit still, could not keep hands or feet at rest. He was in a ferment, his excitement mounting with every passing minute. He began to sweat, unbuttoned furtively his overcoat and removed his hat. The cumulative tension was becoming unendurable, and yet, so strange is human nature, that not for worlds would he now have had Cameron reveal the identity of the expected visitor. No, he must discover it for himself, see with his own eyes. 

How would the murderer enter? 

The fire escape? The dumbwaiter? The door?

When at length the long-expected did happen, Bland was totally unprepared. His straining ears had heard nothing, but Cameron's slack shoulders suddenly straightened and tensed and he nudged his companion sharply. 

Ah, the door! No sound of a footfall, nothing but a faint click as the lock turned. Next a long, thin splinter of light on the opposite wall, the light from the hall that burned all night. It widened, vanished. The

WAITING FOR A MURDERER 

169 door had closed and the murderer was in the room. 

He was standing there in the dark, listening, waiting, on the other side of the screen. And still Cameron did not move. 

A disc of white light on the opposite wall, bobbing up and down, as the intruder switched on a torch. 

It ran down the wall to the floor, swept carelessly along it, suddenly steadied and pointed like an accusing or warning finger. Pointed at what? 

Bland's heart gave a great leap and he felt as though stabbed suddenly in the vitals with a poniard of ice.

What was that on the floor, that blackish mound but a yard distant from where he sat? The thin pencil of light, flashing past the screen, revealed it pitilessly, this thing waiting there in the dark to be revealed. 

This peril, this menace of which he had been sublimely unconscious-how had it got there?

CHAPTER XVII BLAND'S BLUNDER 

THE blackish mound was the District Attorney's hat.

He had placed it carefully by the chair and then, during his fidgeting and quite unconsciously, he had kicked it clear of the screen. A peril and menace indeed. 

Why should a hat be lying on the floor of an unoccupied apartment? The question, and its obvious answer, would suggest itself even to the most obtuse, while the ruthless criminal with whom they were dealing had given every proof of possessing the most acute intelligence, caution and cunning. 

What a fool! What a blundering, congenital idiot! 

No wonder Cameron had hesitated about taking him on this adventure. First to fall over a chair, and nowwell, he had ruined everything, ruined whatever plan Cameron had so carefully prepared. He was only fit to sit in an office and deal academically with crime and criminals.

These thoughts which seemed to take so long in passing through Bland's numb mind, in reality occupied but a few seconds. And in those seconds the intruder had grasped the full significance of what the light had revealed. He switched off the torch and silently opened the door. 

Cameron, sitting with his back to Bland, had been 170

BLAND'S BLUNDER 

171 unable to see what was happening. Ignorant of the tell-tale hat and the accident of its revelation, he awaited with confidence events to follow the course he expected. His first intimation that those events would not take place, and that something had gone radically wrong, was when he saw by the light from the hall that the door was opening again. 

The Police Commissioner sprang from his chair and literally hurled himself through the screen. There came the sound of savage conflict. Bland, galvanized to action, tried to reach the light switch but fell over the screen. Something caught him a blow on the chin and he grappled a pair of legs, brought their owner down and hammered him until resistance had ceased. 

In other circumstances the whole affair would have been ludicrous, burlesque bordering on tragedy, for when at length the District Attorney succeeded in turning on the light he found, as might have been expected, that the only person who had been knocked out was Cameron. The murderer had escaped. 

But he could not have gone far, thought Bland, and sprang into the hall to find it in complete darkness. 

The fleeing criminal had taken time to turn out the light so that no pursuer could catch even a fleeting glimpse of him. Which way had he gone, up or down?

He might escape over the adjoining roofs. 

Bland listened, but hearing nothing, groped his way to the head of the stairs where a faint light from the lower hall stained the right-angled wall. Not a sound. 

The murderer must be wearing rubber shoes. Not a sound from any of the tenants either. Of course the flat below was empty, Vornoff would be asleep, and the brief battle had been fought in silence. How strange and mysterious this human bookcase seemed

in the dead of night! A problem indeed. What secret history was written in the pages of the many human volumes?

Bland ran down to the entrance hall, his rubberheeled tread soundless for all his avoirdupois. But one light was burning here now, and the inside door was shut. A tenant who arrived after the elevator had stopped running and the hall-boy gone, would be obliged to use his key. If that key had been forgotten or lost, he must ring up the janitor or superintendent.

Yes, the inner door was shut, but that was no proof that it had not been opened within the past few minutes. 

The murderer would be quite clever enough to close it carefully after him and thus give the impression that he was still on the premises, or had left by another exit. But if he had actually gained the street, no matter how, then pursuit was useless.

A nice situation, thought Bland, and entirely his fault. The murderer would never pay a second visit to Meier's flat, Cameron's trap never be sprung. No, that cock would never fight again. But if the quarry had slipped out of the trap, his escape was only temporary, for Cameron knew his identity. That was the great point.

Bland, burning with humiliation and the desire to retrieve the situation created by his well-meant blundering, took heart at this thought. It did not spell disaster; it meant simply that Cameron must be done with secrecy and this passion for doing things his own peculiar way. It should be a lesson to him. A blow from a fist does not kill a man, not a man used to giving and taking the hardest of knocks. Why, Cameron had received worse treatment on the gridiron many a time! He was "out", but, of course, he would come round all right.

175 Bland obediently trotted into the kitchenette. It was for Golding to order, him to obey. In this situation the young man was qualified to issue mandates, and he felt futile as one does in the face of exact knowledge.
