CHAPTER XVIII  

THERE was a Headquarters' detective on the steps of the Abercorn Arms when Bland's taxi whirled up an hour later. Macford, now fully dressed, was standing in the entrance hall with Professor Vornoff, who, in carpet slippers and a few odd garments, looked like a tramp.

These two, it would appear, were the only volumes in the human bookcase who had been disturbed by the midnight proceedings, for the janitor, who had been so vigilant, apparently slept like the dead once he was in bed. Of course there had been nothing to disturb him or the rest of the tenants, and it was only Macford and Vornoff who had been within earshot of such little noise as had been made. The Professor, as he explained subsequently, had been awakened finally by the talking going on in Meier's supposedly vacant flat, consequent on the arrival of the Headquarters' men, as Macford had been aroused earlier by the voices of Bland and Keppel. "Well?" queried the superintendent anxiously. "What's the news? Not badly hurt, I hope ?" "Bad enough." "Did he say anything?" asked Vornoff. "Was he able to give a clue to the author of this mysterious outrage ?" 180

"No," said the District Attorney. "Have the police come ?" "Yes, Mr. Corrigan's here too," said Macford. "I didn't know you'd gone to the hospital, and, when I went up to Meier's flat, that precinct detective almost threatened to shoot me. He's too handy with a gun." "And me!" exclaimed Vornoff, shaking his great, round, polished head. "When I go to find out what has happened, he opens the door and presents the pistol at my bosom-so!" And he pointed a thick finger at himself. "I say, 'Let me come in'. And he says, 'Get out !'"' "He had his orders," said Bland. reason." "There's a "Doubtless," agreed the Professor. "But I think I move out of this house, yes. I think I feel more composed in Central Africa or Fiji. First I am robbed, next there are these mysterious deaths, then the 

Police Commissioner himself is assaulted murderously, and finally I have the pistol presented at me. It is too much." "I feel like moving out myself," said Macford sombrely, his black eyes restless and worried. "If I could get a good buyer for my interest in the place he could have my share, and welcome. Mr. Corrigan's been asking me some questions. I'm getting fed up with these goings on, and that's a fact. It's not my fault if these things keep happening. Everybody seems to blame me." "I don't blame you, no," said Vornoff, patting him on the shoulder. "Why should Mr. Police Commissioner

Cameron find himself in Meier's flat, and at such an hour?" he added, his greenish eyes on Bland.

"Explain to me, please, how it all happened. I am consumed with a curiosity. But we may go up to the flat now, eh?" "No," said Bland as he turned to the stairs.

Woe betide the man who kills or maims a member of the Force, and fortunate is he who renders one a service. No injury or favour is ever forgotten by the men in blue, and all combine to square accounts. The news that someone had dared to lay violent hands on the Commissioner himself had been a bombshell that blew Corrigan and others from their beds. O'Reilly and Delaven, Varcoe and Maddox-they were all here, prowling futilely about, as they had done on two former occasions, while they waited for Bland to return with the first authentic details.

It was now three o'clock in the morning, and Bland, who had been on the stretch for twenty hours, was beginning to feel the mental and physical strain. Not the least drain on his vitality was the new worry and concern caused by Cameron's condition. "He has a fifty-fifty chance-no more," he replied in answer to the eager inquiries. "No, he isn't able to speak an intelligible word, and God alone knows if he ever can. You realize, I suppose, the significance of that? I mean as regards this business.” "Yes, I guess the circumstances explain themselves," nodded Corrigan. "The Commissioner wouldn't have been here if he hadn't had something up his sleeve we knew nothing about. And maybe this thing couldn't have happened if we had known. But I'm not criticizing him, understand," he added, a belligerent look on his hard, scalded face as he turned to the others. "There's nobody like him, there never has been or will be, and he's got a right to act any way he

thinks best. He's the whole works, and he's entitled to work any way he pleases. He's proved it." "I entirely agree," said the District Attorney. "You understand then he'd reason to believe, though I don't know how, that the person who murdered Devore and Meier would pay a visit to this flat to-night. 

That's all he told me, and it's no longer possible to keep it secret. I mean everybody can guess that he set a trap for the murderer and got caught in it himself. 

He got caught in it simply and solely through my blundering. But I'll tell you exactly what happened.”

The wrecked screen had spoken already for itself, and Bland now showed where Cameron and he had been sitting, explaining all that had happened since they had entered Devore's flat. "So you see," he said, "the plan would have succeeded only for me. It wasn't Cameron's fault that it didn't. In fact, it had succeeded, and then I wrecked it. It's not the Commissioner's fault, but mine. And even at the last he'd have won out—I'm sure of itif I hadn't pulled him down and made it impossible." "Anything's liable to happen in the dark," said the loyal young Assistant District Attorney, eyes on his chief's drawn face. "And you were in the dark in more ways than one." "And anybody can make mistakes," said Corrigan. "The man who doesn't make a mistake, never makes anything else. You didn't catch a glimpse of the person who entered the room?" "No, not a glimpse. Cameron, you see, tackled him before the door had been opened more than a few inches. There wasn't any light to speak of, and I got hold of the wrong pair of legs. That kick in the face

made me so mad that I hardly knew what I was doing. 

It mayn't have been a man, for all I know." "No woman could hit a blow like that," asserted 

Corrigan. "Not many, anyway." "Much would depend on the weapon," said the

That's the real seat of the trouble. No, I wouldn't rule out a woman. It's possible." 

O'Reilly asked if Bland thought Cameron had been able to see who his assailant was, and the District 

Attorney's answer was a decided negative. Cameron could have been able to see no better than himself.

That's just the point. He would only know the murderer's identity by the fact of him coming to 

Meier's flat. Do you see what I mean? He wouldn't know the identity of that person until he turned on the light. I've been thinking it over, and I'm sure of that." "Why?" demanded Corrigan. O'Reilly, his bird-like, intelligent eyes bright with concentrated thought. "There's something in this flat that the murderer's after, and it would be only by seeing him take it that the Commissioner could be sure of his guilt. For that reason he wouldn't turn on the light and scare him off until he knew he'd got it."

"You're right, O'Reilly !" exclaimed Bland. "That's how I've figured it out. That's why I gave Keppel the orders I did. That's why the Commissioner has had a picked man here day and night since Meier died, and that's why there'll be a picked man here day and night until Cameron's able to speak. For if he doesn't actually know the identity of the murderer, he may have an idea, and he knows at least what it is in this flat that the murderer wants. A clue, that's all we need." "What a situation!" murmured young Varcoe almost unconsciously. "A tremendous crime puzzle, the only innocent person who holds a clue to the answer unable to speak, and that person the Commissioner of Police himself! I don't remember anything like it, either in fiction or fact. It's great!"

Entirely ignoring the tragic element, he spoke as though it were some splendid academic entertainment staged for their benefit, but the older men could understand and condone the youthful outburst. They also were vastly intrigued, gripped by the novel situation. They could remember nothing like it. 

The District Attorney went silently to the door and looked into the hall. Satisfied that there were no eavesdroppers, he closed the door again and took out his cigar-case. They began to smoke, all but Delaven, who, in an apologetic and furtive manner, produced some tobacco which he always referred to as "red hair", and slipped a quid against his gum. The atmosphere became blue and informal; seniority and authority had gone by the board. They were ready to pool their mental equipment, and one man's suggestions were as good as another's. It was a free-for-all crossword puzzle, this mystery of the human bookcase.

"You see" began the District Attorney, and told about the adventure with Dr. Golding. "Of course, if he had the weapon, he could have got rid of it before I searched him." "Yes, hid it somewhere and then walked out with it later on," nodded Corrigan. "The Commissioner didn't hit his head against any table; there'd be blood on it if he had. He was walloped." "With the usual blunt instrument, according to the house surgeon," said Bland. "But a tyre iron, say, could do it if the flat side were used. Or a wrench, spanner, or hammer.” "The janitor has tools like that," contributed Maddox. "Yes, but it mayn't be a tenant at all. A duplicate key could be made for this flat, just as Cameron had one made for Devore's," said Bland. "Easy enough to take an impression." "Yes, that's what Macford said," nodded Corrigan, "when I put it to him. He pointed out, too, that the duplicate's hanging in his office. Anybody might slip in and take an impression of it. He says he thinks now that somebody was in Devore's flat to-nightdon't know it was you and the Commissioner, see? 

He thinks you got in here with a skeleton." "But the motive, that's the point," said Varcoe. "There must be some connection between the two murders and the visit of the murderer here. it he came for ?" 

There was silence, each man thinking hard. 

What is "Could it be the weapon?" suggested O'Reilly, clapping a hand on his angular knee. When excited, a hint of his Donegal ancestry crept out in his speech and gestures. "I'll be meaning, you mind, the one

Devore and Meier were killed with. What if there's really one like the Commissioner spoke of at the start, of the beginning of all this business? Supposing it was hidden here--" "Why should it be?" broke in the phlegmatic

Delaven like a douche of ice water. "There'd be no sense in hiding it here." "There would not," agreed Corrigan. "Why here any more than Devore's flat? Why should he leave it behind at all?" "To prevent it being found on him if he was searched," retorted O'Reilly. "He might expect to be searched, like maybe he was the night Devore was killed. Sure he might have hidden it, too, in Devore's flat for the time being. And, don't you see, his own flat might be searched, so he daren't hide it there." "But why leave it here all this time ?" said Varcoe. "Why only come to-night for it?” "Sure hasn't the District Attorney already told you why? Could he have come any sooner, with a detective on duty here day and night? Wasn't he after taking the first chance he thought he'd got?" "Ingenious, O'Reilly," said Bland, "and logical up to a certain point. That's the devil of this businessthat all logic seems to have definite limits. It goes just so far, and then it fails us. I've thought of that explanation of yours, but it won't wash. For, you see, here's the snag: If there was plenty of time for the murderer to escape, not only after Meier's death, but Devore's, there was plenty of time to put the weapon in a safe hiding-place. These crimes were carefully planned and he would have arranged for that. He wouldn't leave the weapon on the premises, no matter what else he might leave, for he wanted it

to appear that a snake was responsible. For the weapon to be discovered would be to give the show away." "That's as true as God made little apples," approved 

Corrigan. "All he had to do was stick the thing in his pocket. He knew the place would be searched and that it was bound to be found-and it would have been found if it was there. Didn't we turn the place upside down? His first thought would be to remove the weapon. There's no getting away from that." "Emphatically no," said Bland. "If such a weapon was really used he wouldn't keep it on his person, and he mightn't hide it in his home, but there are plenty of places where it could be hidden. There's absolutely no logical reason why he should run the far greater danger, court inevitable discovery, by leaving it behind." "That's true enough," mused O'Reilly disconsolately as he rubbed a long chin. "Faith, the closer you look into this black business, the less you see. Well, then, if it's not the weapon, what in the name of glory is it ?" "Perhaps something above eye level," said Bland, and smiled as once Cameron had smiled at the look of inquiry and astonishment depicted on the faces of his audience. Yes, there was a certain enjoyment in mystifying people, not fools, but intelligent men.

Even though he was giving but a very poor imitation, and he himself was thoroughly mystified, he understood how Cameron had felt. "Come into the next room-all of you," he said soberly. "Perhaps one of you will be able to see what I can't."

CHAPTER XIX  

"Now," said the District Attorney, when they were all assembled in the bedroom, "I've an idea that the clue to the murderer, perhaps the clue to the whole mystery, lies, not merely in this flat, but in this room. 

I think it has narrowed down to that-a single room. 

Cameron said once that there's a pivotal point in every case, something on which the whole thing turns. 

I disagreed at the time, but I don't now. The pivotal point of this case, to my mind, is here before you.". "Where?" demanded Corrigan, thrusting out his grizzled red head. "Show me something I haven't seen before." "I only wish I could," said Bland with a helpless gesture. "But six heads should be better than one, and it may depend on how we use them whether we'll ever be able to bring this murderer to justice. 

But, first of all, I must put you in possession of all I know, and it will be for you to draw your own conclusions. I must tell you all that the Commissioner said, though I don't know why he said it, and what led up to our coming here to-night-or rather last night."

Was it only twelve hours or so since Paul Garwinn died? That visit to St. Luke's seemed to Bland, as 189

he now marshalled mentally the sequence of recent events, to have happened days ago. "I think it started with that," he said. "There was something that Cameron wanted to ask Garwinn. 

Then, when we were waiting for the elevator" 

And he told of the woman in the corridor. "There's no use asking me what she looked like; I'm as blind as a bat without my glasses, and she was at the far end. But Cameron may have known her, or, if he didn't, she may have reminded him of somebody I say 'may', for he might have been looking at something else." "What else was there?" asked O'Reilly. "Nothing. That's just it. There was nothing but the moving elevator cable" And Bland explained how it had reminded him of a snake. "But," said O'Reilly, "was the woman a nurse or a visitor? Sure you must have been able to see what she was dressed like ?" 

She was a "Yes, I could see she wasn't a nurse. visitor, standing there waiting for somebody." "We mustn't forget," said Varcoe, "in all this talk about a weapon that a snake may have been used. 

That theory hasn't been disproved. Do you think the elevator cable suggested to the Commissioner what it did to you?" "That's what I don't know. But at all events, whatever it was, the idea that had been germinating in his mind got a sudden and decided impetus. And it must have been connected in some way with the Abercorn Arms, this human bookcase, for he decided to come here at once."

The District Attorney then told of the ride down-town and Cameron's remarks about a comet and a comma.

"The tail of a comet !" exclaimed Varcoe, rubbing his hands. "What on earth did he mean ?" "Perhaps nothing," replied Bland. "You all know his moods, and he may have been trying to mystify me purposely. I think he was to a certain extent. 

For, of course, the tail of a comet's harmless, being composed simply of gas. It's the head that counts. 

And yet I'm sure that some pertinent truth underlay all he said." "There's no more sense in a comma than a comet,” asserted Corrigan, chewing savagely on his cigar. "Well, at least it introduced the subject of Miss Camblin and Meier's will. Perhaps that's all it was meant to do," continued the District Attorney. "He knew I hadn't heard about it and, when he's in that mood, he likes to spring surprises on me. That's what makes everything the more difficult-that I don't know how much was fooling and how much was not. Yet I've got to tell you all he said, and in proper sequence. And I repeat that I'm sure he was hot on the scent of the murderer, saw the solution of the whole mystery, and that he wanted me to see it for myself. 

He's always trying to educate my poor brain up to his standard-an impossible job," said Bland with a shake of the head.

He next repeated what Cameron had said concerning Devore and Meier; how one had been struck in the neck and the other the leg, although, from their respective heights, one might have thought it should be the other way about. "Does that suggest anything to you?" he asked, looking from one earnest and perplexed face to another. 

The five good men and true pondered long and hard, but could produce nothing helpful.

"Sure, it's an odd fact in a way when you come to think of it now, and I hadn't thought much about it before," said O'Reilly at length. "But what the real significance of it may be, I don't know for the life of me. True enough that it would have been easier to reach Meier's neck, the vital part, than Devore's." "All I can see," offered Varcoe, "is that it tends to support the snake theory. As Professor Vornoff pointed out, a snake must strike where it can.” "Yes," said Corrigan, "but if the murderer wanted it to look like the job of a snake, he wouldn't strike twice in the same place. No, not a clever bird like the one we're after. That would be asking a bit too much of coincidence." "Quite true," nodded Bland. "And, as the Commissioner pointed out, the second murder may have happened in the dark. In fact, there seems to be every evidence that it did. So the murderer may have had no choice. Like a snake, he may have had to strike where he could, or aimed at one place and hit another." 

Failing any further suggestions, he then went on to relate the interview with Mrs. Royal. "Of course this matter of Meier's will may produce some surprising developments," he said; "but we needn't speculate now on that. It's quite possible, however, that Cameron knew or suspected something concerning it that we don't. You must bear that possibility in mind when considering all he said. I believe that the first inkling of the truth came to him when he heard about the will, though he said it happened the previous night. Anyway, he failed to see

Miss Camblin, and his main interest seemed to lie in the fact that her aunt had revealed another side of her character-the real Mrs. Royal, as he phrased it.”

"So she's all ready to fight Mrs. Meier ?" commented 

Corrigan. "A hard blade under all the soft stuff, eh? 

Well, I kind of thought so, the little I saw of her." "But perhaps the Commissioner's real interest from the first was neither Mrs. Royal nor her niece, but 

Meier's flat," continued the District Attorney. "His objective was this room, and, after looking round it awhile, he went straight to that window and stood staring out for a long time without saying a word." "Was he looking at the drainpipe?" asked Maddox, perhaps the least intelligent of the company. "I don't know what he was looking at," said Bland shortly, "and he can see things without looking, things nobody else sees."

He described how Cameron had sat down on the bed, and what he had said concerning Devore and Meier. "A scientific truth," repeated Bland. "Meier, he said, was a man of oranges and bananas, while Devore was a man of mathematics and books. But it was 

Meier, not Devore, who knew the scientific truth." "Yes, odd enough if it's so," said O'Reilly. "But how did he know it was so ?" "Because he saw it!" exclaimed Bland. "That's what I've been leading up to, and that's why I've brought you into this room. He told me to put on my glasses and look, and now I tell you to put on your mental glasses and look. He said he saw it the first night he entered this room, and he as good as said it was above eye level."

The five men raised their eyes, as though worked by a common mechanism, and stared intently at the walls and ceiling as Bland himself had done, and as he now proceeded to do again.

"There's nothing to see," exclaimed Corrigan at length. "That's precisely the point," said Bland, unconsciously and triumphantly employing Cameron's own words. "It's not there, and yet it is there."

They looked at him as though suddenly doubtful of his sanity. "Yes," continued Bland, "it's a paradox, but it must be true. The Commissioner's own words were, 'I saw it, I see it, but it isn't there. He also said emphatically that, except for the fact that Meier's clothes had been removed, everything was exactly the same as on the night he first entered the room. Nothing had been added to, and nothing taken away." "How could he see what isn't here?" demanded 

Corrigan, as once Bland himself had demanded. "There's no sense in it. He must have been pulling your leg." "He wasn't," retorted the District Attorney stubbornly. "Yes, it's nonsense, but I believe there's sense in it, if only we could find it. It may have nothing to do with the weapon, but there's something in this room so important to the whole case that, if we knew what it was, I believe it would lead to a solution of the mystery. I'm sure of it." "Then," said Corrigan grimly as he began to peel off his coat, "if it's here we'll find it. There's nothing 

I haven't seen a dozen times before, but we'll have another look. Above eye level, you say?"

They set to work with a will, the whole six of them. 

They took down the pictures and examined them back and front; they scrutinized the steampipe in the corner that led up to Vornoff's flat and down to Devore's; they stood on chairs and pored over the

window curtains; opened the window and flashed a torch on the adjacent drainpipe. As their zeal and exasperation increased with failure, they ignored the above-eye-level restriction and examined everything between floor and ceiling. The chandelier came in for a scrutiny all its own. So also the telephone, and they tapped the walls for any hidden compartment. All to no purpose. At the end of half an hour six weary, warm and irritated men sat down and looked at one another helplessly. "Well, the Commissioner was right-it's not here," said Corrigan grimly as he mopped his neck and lit a fresh cigar. "We'd have saved ourselves these physical jerks if we'd believed that at the start.” "No, we couldn't have missed it the first time we searched the place," said Delaven. "He must have been fooling you, Mr. Bland." 

Bland, hot, with heavy head in hands, stared at the carpet as though hoping to find a clue in its intricate pattern. "Either you're all as blind as I am, or I'm not the owl Cameron said I was," he remarked wearily. "Look here, do you think it could have anything to do with the only things that aren't here now-Meier's clothes?" asked O'Reilly. "No, I asked Cameron that. They weren't above eye level. But he said that Meier's clothes had an interest all their own." "Where are they?" asked Varcoe with renewed alertness. "In that closet," said Corrigan, pointing with his cigar. "You know they were put away with his other things until Mrs. Meier turns up-if she ever does. 

We've been all through them, of course."

Nevertheless the garments worn by Herman Meier on the night of his death were brought out and examined piece by piece. But whatever significance they may have had for the Commissioner of Police now failed to reveal itself to these earnest disciples. "This thing's getting a joke, and a bad one at that," remarked Corrigan disgustedly. "We might as well be playing ping-pong." 

And then Maddox, sitting in a corner brooding, had his big idea. All the more surprising because no one expected it from that quarter. Maddox's large mottled face and protuberant eyes did not suggest themselves as the home of great ideas, nor had he ever been unduly notorious in that direction. "Wait, I got a big idea!" he exclaimed, holding up an impressive hand. "A man of oranges and bananas, that's what the Commissioner called Meier, didn't he? Well, why did he call him that?" "Because he was that," said Bland shortly. "Yes, but why didn't he call him a fruiterer? Look here, I think there was a hidden meaning in those words. Why oranges and bananas? Well, there's a big crate of them in the kitchen. See what I mean?

Yes, it seems to be nailed tight, but it's never been opened-by us." 

Bland recollected suddenly how Cameron's torch had flashed on the crate after they had entered the window of the kitchenette. Was that accident or design? Although it had been handled freely, and showed no sign of having been tampered with, the fact remained that of all the objects in the flat, it was the only one that had not been submitted to an examination. Its stamped and labelled contents had been taken for granted, because one could

But see part of those contents through the slats. what might there be in the centre where no one could see? "Of course snakes come in crates of fruit, even when you don't want 'em to," said Corrigan. "Is that your big idea? But what's that got to do with something above eye level in this room ?" "I don't know-yet," replied Maddox. "But, as 

Mr. Bland says, what the Commissioner saw in this room, or said he saw, may have nothing to do with the weapon. If a poisonous snake's kept in that crate, it would explain a lot of things, like why the weapon should be in this flat and not in Devore's." "That part of it's true enough," exclaimed O'Reilly. "It would explain why the weapon had to be left behind. Supposing now, do you see" "I see a lot of things," broke in the District Attorney excitedly. "Yes, it would explain much, if not everything. I even said to Cameron how if, when Devore was struck, a snake had been hanging from the portieres . . . and he said he hadn't forgotten about 

Macford thinking the cord a snake. That shows he thought the incident significant. But come on, we'll soon find out!"

The opening of the crate was not an operation that any of them cared particularly to perform. There was no mad rush to see who would get there first and claim the privilege. If Maddox was right, that innocentlooking crate was as dangerous as a Mills bomb from which they purposed removing the pin. 

They armed themselves with various impromptu weapons, for revolvers would only prove a source of danger to themselves, and Bland got the Malacca cane that Cameron had brought from Devore's. He was

now convinced that his original idea about it was right. "You open it," said Corrigan to Maddox. "It's your big idea, and you're entitled to be the one. Go to it."

But it was O'Reilly and Delaven, armed with an ice-pick and tin-opener, who began to prise off the slats while the others stood round, ready to take a whack at the first living thing that showed itself. 

A tense and exciting five minutes as the upper slats were removed, the fruit lifted out. Ludicrous also, for the only living thing in the crate was an insignificant spider. "Well, there's your big idea, Maddox," said Corrigan, waving a hand at the spider as it scuttled over the floor. "The mountain laboured and brought forth-that. 

I knew the crate hadn't been opened since it left the shippers. Anybody could see that." "It's what I said," remarked Bland wearily. "All logic has its limits in this case, and every explanation seems to peter out. We're in a blind alley. But now 

I'll tell you the last thing the Commissioner said to me in this room," he added as they returned to the bedroom, "and it's the last bit of help I can offer you. 

He said the murderer was Red Rum Myer." "What !" exclaimed Corrigan. "Then he does know his identity, and that proves it's a man." "It does not, neither one nor the other," retorted the District Attorney. "I said to him, 'Tell me one thing Have you discovered anything about Red 

Rum Myer?' 'I have', he replied. 'Who is it?' I asked. 'The murderer', he said. Now those are the exact words. If I had said, 'Who is he?' or 'Who is she?' I used that pronoun, having no idea what a

difference it would make. And so, you see, 'it' may be either masculine or feminine.” "Or neuter," added O'Reilly. "I've been wondering myself sometimes if it's a person at all." "What do you mean?" asked Varcoe. "It's bound to be a person-somebody. What else could it be?

There are those two warning messages, if nothing else. 

'The Ides of March'. 'Devore is dead, but Red Rum 

Myer remains'. When you think of all the questions that need an answer-Lord, it's a whale of a mystery !" "And supposing there's another killing?" suggested 

Corrigan as he paced the floor. "That's the thing that has me lying awake o' nights. I've been half expecting it, not knowing the Commissioner was all ready to close down on the show. We don't know why 

Devore and Meier were bumped off, and so we don't know who may be the next." "Why should there be a next?" said Varcoe. "Why shouldn't there?" demanded Corrigan. "Just as much sense in having three as two. We haven't got the weapon, and even possession of a weapon is temptation enough to use it." "Yes, armaments make wars," nodded Bland. "If the murderer's got away with it twice, why not again?" added Corrigan. "If the Commissioner isn't able to talk, how can we put a stop to this one-man crime wave?-if it is a man. That's what I want to know." "All we can do," said Bland, "is to have a man on duty here day and night. Even if we can't find it, I know there's something here, if it's not the weapon, whose possession means so much to the murderer, that he'll still hang around in the hope of getting it. That's all we can do for the present."

"Yes, I suppose that's right," nodded Corrigan. "But if there's anything here like you say, it must be below sea level instead of above eye level. I guess the Commissioner was having one of his French jokes -what they call a blague-with you."

CHAPTER XX  

JEROME BLAND had no sleep for another twenty hours, for the spring sun was gilding the ragged eastern skyline when at length he left the Abercorn Arms and returned to the hospital.

During his first visit, he had arranged for an official stenographer to be on duty in case Cameron should be able to speak, but he now found that there was nothing to report. Cameron had developed a temperature, but otherwise his condition remained unchanged. He muttered and tossed about, but not one intelligible word did he say. 

But, if no better, the patient was no worse; the odds for survival had not swung against him. Brain fever and delirium might be expected, and perhaps a long sojourn in the Valley of the Shadow, but a sound constitution should pull him through. And, of course, all that modern medicine could do would be done. 

Bland left with better heart, for a shave, tub, some breakfast, and then down to the office for the day's work that must be done though the heavens fall. 

In the anxious days that followed, he commuted between the Criminal Courts building and the hospital, visiting the latter during the lunch hour and again when he had pulled down his desk for the day. First thing in the morning and the last at night; that was 201

his programme. And still Cameron, battling with the waves that rolled in from the dark, unknown, shoreless sea, said no word of any kind. He was swimming in deep waters and, reserved by character and training, he seemed bent on living this crucial experience in silence. Mutter and mutter he did; but speak, no. 

Not one intelligible word. 

Meanwhile the District Attorney's office and Centre

Street bent themselves anew, and with savage purpose, to the task of solving the mystery of the human bookcase, as it had come to be called. A personal element had entered into the case; not merely the murderer of Devore and Meier did they want to lay by the heels, but the assailant of Kirker Cameron, the person responsible for that cowardly and murderous blow. 

If that person were caught, he might expect rough handling, not merely from his official captors, but the crowds that waited for daily bulletins concerning the condition of the Commissioner of Police. Cameron, whose full work as the guardian of society was not suspected even now by the public, had entered the limelight which he abhorred. No doubt if he died he should become a national hero. Death brings universal recognition to more than creative artists like Henry Devore, although Cameron was a creative artist ín his own way. 

There had been no new developments concerning

Devore, although Centre Street was still pondering the purple patches in his past, hoping to uncover something connecting him with Meier. But the only connection remained alcoholic.

The bootlegger Belshaw was still having his past investigated also, while Meier's life in the Bahamas was

under a microscope. Even in the tropics, the wholesale fruiterer, as Corrigan had said, seemed to have specialized somewhat in peaches. Everything tended to portray him as the kind of man who, if given to building air castles, would have seen them closed by the air police. 

Miss Gower called again upon the District Attorney, demanding that "something be done", and painting anew in glowing colours Devore's saintly character.

But in spite of her high lights the original picture of the successful novelist remained-that of a philanderer and bohemian. No evidence was forthcoming to correct it.

The glistening and implacable woman had grown noticeably thinner, her eyes rather too bright. Bland, in the light of Cameron's comments, was very patient with her. No doubt she was grieving for Devore, and this secret sorrow was producing a desire for vengeance on his murderer that threatened to become an obsession.

Bland advised her to go to the shore for a rest, and was told indirectly that he might go pernianently to a place where there is supposed to be neither water nor rest. "It's you who should have a change, you and the police," she declared. "You should make room for people who can do something. You've done absolutely nothing. You haven't stirred a finger against that sleek, brown-eyed cat. I tell you she knows perfectly well who murdered Mr. Devore, if she didn't do it herself." "Yes? And the motive ?" "Because she couldn't hoodwink him like she could 

Mr. Meier. Everybody knows what she got out of him. Just what I expected. But Mr. Devore would

have none of her wiles. He saw through her, and he wasn't that kind. His purity protected him." "Really, you know," protested Bland weakly, "you mustn't say such things." "I say what I think, and what other people think but are afraid to say. Mr. Macford-anybody in the

Abercorn Arms could tell you the truth if they weren't such cowards." "What truth?" "The obvious truth that Miss Camblin, and that Amazon who poses as her aunt, live by fleecing fools. 

Gold diggers, that's all they are. I shouldn't be at all surprised if they realized that Mr. Meier was more helpful dead than alive. It's only another step from theft and blackmail to murder. Didn't the person who murdered Mr. Devore murder Mr. Meier? Is there any doubt of that?" "There may be. But, as regards Meier, why kill the goose that lays the golden egg?" "Because he'd lay a bigger one dead. Meier was going to marry Miss Camblin-or thought he wasafter he got a divorce from his wife for desertion." "How do you know?" "I know." "In that case, why murder him?" "I've told you why. And husbands are an inconvenience; they prevent other would-be husbands making a will in one's favour. Why marry him when she could get the money-more than he'd give her as his wife-without it ?" "But why was he going to marry her?" "Because he could get her no other way. Oh, she was sly enough to see to that. I know her kind. 

And then, as his future wife, she got him in the

meantime to make a will in her favour. That's all she was after."

Of course this theory was by no means new to the 

District Attorney's office. Bland had considered the matter of Meier's will from every angle, was still considering it. But the expected surprising developments had failed to materialize thus far, though it was true that Mrs. Royal's lawyer offered something of a problem in himself.

Investigation now showed that Adrian Gloz had started his legal career as an "ambulance chaser", and that for years his practice had been of a questionable kind. Ready to take any case, whatever its merits, on a contingent basis, his early days had been spent almost wholly among crime and criminals. He was a "fall money guy", the lawyer whose fee comes out of the nest egg which the provident crook provides against the day of his inevitable arrest.

Astute and wary as a veteran fox, which he somewhat resembled, Adrian's only slip had been when he was nearly disbarred for subornation of perjury. 

Eventually he branched out into civil practice and became, to all appearance, successful and highly respectable. Yet he remained, and would remain, a lawyer of a certain type; and it was questionable if his new clients, for all their social status, were fundamentally different from his original ones. In civil practice, as in criminal practice, he took cases that more fastidious members of his profession declined. 

Which is not to say that Mr. Gloz or Mrs. Royal and her niece were guilty of any reprehensible conduct in the matter of Meier's will. Apparently there was no sinister conspiracy connected with that document, nor did Meier's lawyer, member of a most reputable firm,

suggest any. Bland had had an interview with both him and Gloz.

True, Meier's regular legal advisers had been ignorant that a new will was made, but the wholesale fruiterer had kept more than that from them. They knew nothing of his profitable sideline, the financing of such men as Belshaw. But the will in Miss Camblin's favour was properly drawn and attested, and, as 

Mrs. Royal had said, a man may leave his money where he likes, so long as his widow gets her legal share. "Of course I knew nothing about this matter," said Gloz, "until Mrs. Royal called me up and told me she had been informed by Mr. Green, Meier's lawyer. 

I needn't say it was as great a surprise to me as to her and her niece. I was aware that Mr. Meier thought highly of Miss Camblin, but as for making her the principal legatee 

He shook his bald, pearshaped head. 

"Have you been representing her long? You knew

Mrs. Royal and her niece before you became their neighbour at the Abercorn Arms?” "Oh yes," nodded Gloz. "You see I helped to settle the estate of the late Mr. Royal, whom I knew personally. A very fine man indeed. That's how

I came to be their legal adviser, not that they've ever had any law work to speak of." "Surely you're too modest, Mr. Gloz," said Bland, who had been doing some delving into court records. "Weren't you the successful plaintiff's attorney in 

Camblin v. Goodson some years ago?" "Oh, yes, I'd quite forgotten," said Gloz, waving an airy hand. "A trifling matter.”

This trifling matter had cost a certain Mr. Goodson,

a Boston publisher, some twenty-five thousand dollars, a sum calculated to heal the lacerated affections of 

Miss Camblin. In other words, a breach of promise action that was settled before it ever reached the jury. 

There were exhibits, in the form of letters, which the elderly publisher, however free with other people's writings, was modest about giving to the world. "Trifling," explained Mr. Gloz, noting the District 

Attorney's look, "in a legal sense, because there was no case to fight. Mr. Goodson thought he could bluff us, but very discreetly wilted at the last moment.

Trifling in that sense, but it wrecked that poor young girl's life. She loved Mr. Goodson devotedly, and I often marvel that the unfortunate experience didn't warp and sour such a naturally trusting and sunny disposition."

Mr. Gloz, it will be seen, had acquired more than a new clientele in his evolution from an ambulance chaser. Apart from his new accent and vocabulary, he now affected gold nose-glasses with a broad silk ribbon, and white spats. "A designing, experienced man of the world,” he said, "and an innocent young fledgling fresh from the home nest. That was the case. Mere money can't compensate for such injury, but these men who prey on innocent young womanhood must be reached somehow, and a blow at the pocket is what they feel most. As I say, it wrecked Miss Camblin's life, and I daresay it's the reason she has never married."

An interesting sidelight, Camblin v. Goodson, on little Madeline. Bland wondered if Cameron knew about it. He could understand why the publisher had found discretion the better part of valour. Quite apart from compromising letters, that "fledgling from

the home nest" stuff, which Miss Camblin so eloquently supported with her baby stare and confiding little ways, would have been certain to wring the heartstrings of an intelligent American jury. Aided by a pleader like Adrian Gloz, the result was a foregone conclusion.

It explained also in part those Liberty Bonds which had increased so gratifyingly since Mr. Royal's death. 

The income and inheritance tax machinery being what it is, estates and incomes are no longer quite the mystery they once were to all but their possessors. 

Data collecting in the District Attorney's office showed that Mr. Royal, a Brooklyn baker, had left a very modest estate. No doubt due to successful speculation, aided perhaps by the advice of Mr. Gloz, Mrs. 

Royal and her niece had flourished since that family bereavement. But who and what Miss Camblin's parents were, the precise date of her orphanage and transference to the motherly wing of Mrs. Royal, remained a matter of conjecture. She was not known in the baker's old neighbourhood.

There was no evidence, however, to support Miss 

Gower's statement concerning the ultimate marriage of Meier and Miss Camblin, and nothing to show that any undue influence had been exerted in the making of the new will. That will, found by Meier's lawyer in a recently discovered safety deposit box, apparently never had been seen by any interested outside party. 

Not the least of the many maddening questions that beset Bland's harried mind concerned this alleged fund of information possessed by Miss Gower. Was she only guessing, or did she know? Did jealousy and hatred of Miss Camblin lend her prescience, or did she even actually know far more than she would say? 

That she had spied to a certain extent on Devore

seemed beyond question, but did she still spy on other tenants of the Abercorn Arms? Had she a method of so doing unknown to the police? Thus far they had been able to find out little about her prior to the Devore connection. Apparently she was one of the city's countless flung stones. 

Then there was Dr. Golding. Mrs. Royal and Miss

Camblin had corroborated his statement that he had just left their apartment when met by Bland; but was this corroboration worth the time spent on getting it ? No question that Golding had become very intimate with Madeline Camblin, that he had taken the place once shared by Devore and Meier. Did he really think her the fine girl he said, and was she?

Was she simply the victim of jealous gossip and circumstances in general, perhaps even the victim of a mercenary and designing aunt ?

Next there was Professor Vornoff, Feigel, the hall-boy, Macford, the Miss Hamilton whose nose had been put out of joint; and, as if all this was not enough, there was Mrs. Meier.

CHAPTER XXI  

It was strange in a way how, from a cipher in the case, Mrs. Meier had grown gradually into the unknown quantity "X", and become quite a major problem in herself. Where was she? Why had she not appeared?

Meier's lawyer was even advertising now, while Centre Street was making every effort to trace her. "It's funny," ruminated Corrigan. "Maybe she's still so mad that she won't touch a penny of the estate, and don't want to have anything to do with her husband, alive or dead. If she was ever in California it must have been under another name. They say they don't know her there." "Yes, it's strange," agreed Bland. "She must know that her husband's dead, even if she doesn't know about the will. Meier was a rich man, and her legal share of the estate will amount to a good bit. There aren't many who'd want to give it up." "Well, she has money of her own. She wouldn't take any separation allowance." "Yes, so Meier's lawyer says. But even if she doesn't want her share, that doesn't explain why she hasn't come forward like any regular person." "Maybe she's abroad," said Corrigan. "Or maybe she has a good reason. She may have hooked up with somebody else." 210

"Bigamy ?" "Not necessarily, though it happens lots of times. 

These separations are worse than divorces. But maybe she's living as Miss So-and-So and don't want some young admirer to know she's a married woman. 

That happens too." "From all I can gather she was devoted to Meier," dissented Bland. "I don't think she'd have given him a divorce. It shows the kind of character she is, the way she packed up and got out, refusing to touch his money. I suppose she could stand his infidelities just so long. Mrs. Royal claims that she never knew her. That may be the truth, but I believe that at least Miss Camblin knew Meier before either of them lived at the Abercorn Arms.” 

Corrigan nodded, and Bland added: "I wish we could get hold of a photo of Mrs. Meier. Nobody seems to have known anything about her, not even Mr. Green, and I can find no record of her marriage." "Maybe they weren't married, and maybe she wasn't even a common-law wife. Perhaps that's why she hasn't shown up. Or maybe we'll find a record in the Bahamas or some place like that.” "Why?" "Well, they say at the Belvedere, where they lived last before separating, that she looked like she'd some nigger blood in her. The 'Octoroon', that's what they called her. I don't know that Meier had things all his own way either. They say she'd some temper when she got properly started. Neighbours heard her bawling him out more than once." "I'm sure he never got any more than he earned,” said Bland. "The more I dig into that man's lifeand we haven't dug very far-the less I like what turns

up. Devore was the plaster saint that Miss Gower thinks him compared to Herman Meier. A brutal and thoroughly vicious man."

But Mrs. Meier was not the only person whose vanishing act added still another problem to the many that beset the District Attorney's office and 

Police Headquarters. They were as anxious to get in touch with another woman who seemed to have vanished mysteriously. This was the woman who had done the cleaning at the Abercorn Arms at the time Professor Vornoff claimed to have been robbed of the black mamba venom.

Like Mrs. Meier, this woman had emerged gradually, a shadowy and inconsequential figure, from the background to the foreground of the picture. Yet, in spite of this promotion, she remained almost as amorphous. But to Bland's mind, if not Corrigan's, she had become so important that he questioned 

Donald Macford closely about her. "I've no reason to think her name wasn't Mrs.

Harrap, or that she wasn't all she claimed to be," said the superintendent. "Was she a superior sort of person?" "Well, she seemed to have seen better days, if that's what you mean. But the work isn't what it used to be, and we get all sorts nowadays." "Did you look up her references ?" "No, because she hadn't any. This was her first job. She said her husband had died and she had to take any work she could get. She was all alone in the world, and she'd a couple of children to support. I'd had trouble with the service company that did the cleaning, and I was willing to try her out. She certainly proved honest and efficient."

"No complaint about her? "No, and she looked after more flats than Vornoff's. 

Why should she want to steal a thing like that snake poison ?" "I'm not suggesting she did. But it's a possible answer that should have received more attention before this, although it mayn't have escaped the Police Commissioner. This woman only took up her duties here after Professor Vornoff returned from abroad?" "Yes, after the newspaper talk about him experimenting with snake poison, if that's what you mean." "Exactly what I mean," nodded Bland. "And, I understand, she only kept the position a month? 

What reason did she give for leaving ?” "The same that most of them give-a better job. 

No, she didn't say where or what." "What did she look like ?" "Well," said Macford, pondering, "she was about thirty something. Good figure. Black hair, brown eyes, dark complexion. I thought more than once she might have been quite a good looker if she'd taken some trouble with herself, if you know what I mean. 

She must have been pretty when she was young; but she'd sort of let herself go to seed. I suppose she'd met so much hard luck that she didn't care any more about her looks. She looked like she'd been through a lot of worrying trouble, and that's why I was glad to give her a chance at the job." "She only reported for work after the tenants had gone? And she left before they returned ?" "Yes, that's right. She didn't come till after ten, and she was all washed up about four. That's all the work I had for her, there being a limit to the

bachelor flats. And that's why she left as soon as she landed an all-day job."

The superintendent added that if Mrs. Harrap could not be located at her old address, and no one knew where she had gone, it was likely that she had moved out of town. This, of course, was probable. Also she might have married again, and so there would be no mystery connected with her disappearance. "How's the Commissioner to-day?” added Macford. "Is it true, like the papers say, that even if he does pull through, he mayn't remember anything about how he got hurt ?”’ "Yes, true enough. Not only that; he mayn't remember anything that happened before the attack— I mean the events that led up to his being in Meier's flat." "Well, but you know.” "I do not," said Bland. "Commissioner Cameron didn't tell me or anyone. Whatever he expected to happen I was to see with my own eyes. But why he expected Meier's murderer to visit the flat-if it was the murderer-I don't know any more than you."

Now in this, as it may be surmised, Bland was endeavouring to turn the liability of ignorance into an asset. If not merely the truth became generally known, but the fiction concerning Cameron's possible permanent loss of memory, then the murderer might be lulled into a false sense of security. Safe in the knowledge that his identity was unknown, and that 

Cameron remembered nothing, he might even make eventually another attempt to gain possession of whatever lure had brought him initially to Meier's flat. "And yet," Bland said in discussing the matter with

Corrigan, "we daren't remove those detectives for a minute, because the murderer, if he's a tenant, would whip in and get what he's after." "While he won't come so long as he knows the flat's guarded?" "Yet we've got to choose the lesser evil," said 

Bland. "He mayn't be a tenant, and, if he isn't, he won't know it's guarded."

A most curious and involved situation, fitting equation of the whole complex problem. Was the murderer a tenant, somebody known to them, and was the use of the masculine pronoun justified? If not a tenant, had he, profiting by his narrow escape, vanished beyond the ken of justice for ever? "But if he's a tenant," said Bland, "of course he daren't try to escape. That would only draw attention to him, suggest his guilt." "Yes, but Vornoff and Mrs. Royal and others have talked about moving out. So has Macford, if he can get a buyer. If they do, we can't stop them." "No, we can only have them watched, wherever they go. But if we get this into the papers about the 

Commissioner's probable permanent loss of memory, and if we tell the truth about our own ignorance, then the murderer should have an extra inducement for staying. Meanwhile Cameron may be able to give us the hint we've been looking for." "Well, if he's ever going to speak another word, even in fever, it's time he was starting," said Corrigan gloomily. 

Cameron had started talking, as Bland found on reaching the hospital, after his interview with Macford concerning Mrs. Harrap. The peak of delirium was being climbed and, for the first time, the official

stenographer's notebook was something more than a blank. Bland seized it eagerly, then frowned in puzzled wonder as he read the following transcription :

Van. Masterpiece. Window. Case. Anna. 

These five words were written over and over again, in various sequences, with here and there the name of 

Devore and Meier. Bland now heard some of them, and others, with his own ears as he sat by the bed, and Cameron, with febrile, sightless eyes and tossing arms, began to talk. "Masterpiece!" he exclaimed, and added a few wholly unintelligible words. "The 

Ides of March. Anna" Then suddenly he began to laugh. It was terrible to hear that laugh, to watch the futile struggle of the once brilliant and crystalclear mind as it tried to co-ordinate and win free of the murky clouds of delirium. "He said something after the word 'van'," said the stenographer, "but I couldn't make it out. 

It was either 'go' or 'off'." "Of course those words may mean nothing," said the visiting specialist, "but I think they do. I believe they relate to something, some phase of the case that has become predominant in his mind to the exclusion of everything else. But I'm glad he has begun to talk at last, even if it should be only nonsense.' 

Bland returned to Centre Street, his brain in a whirl.

He believed that, raving or not, Cameron had revealed some truth, if only he had the wit to penetrate it.

Though it might seem like so much nonsense, as his remarks in Meier's flat, sound sense must underlie it. "But who is Anna?" he demanded, discussing this new problem with Corrigan. "Is she the woman

Cameron saw in St. Luke's, and is that woman the murderer ?" "It's worse than the old teaser, 'How old is Ann ?' "' said Corrigan. "Yes, there must be a woman somewhere in the case, but I don't think she slugged the 

Commissioner, no matter what else she did. And what in thunder has a wagon got to do with it?" "Not a wagon; a van.” "Well, it comes to the same thing, don't it? We've had comets and commas, and the Lord knows what, and now we've a wagon and a window. Can you beat it ?" "The window must refer to the one in Meier's bedroom," said Bland. "As I told you, the Commissioner went straight to it and spent a long time looking out." "Well, and we spent a longer time-the whole six of us. 

And what did we find? If there's anything phony or funny about that window, I'll qualify for the booby hatch." "It mayn't be the window itself, but something outside; something whose significance hasn't struck us. But what could be there, and yet not there? 

What could he see, and yet not see?" "Nothing material," said Corrigan. "He could see, for instance, something in his own mind, like when we say 'I see it', when we see the answer to some riddle or other. He must have meant that." "I don't agree, Corrigan. I believe it's something material. Cameron, even when he's in that humour, plays fair. There was something he wished me to see for myself; not something in his mind, but something in that room, something above eye level. But what is it? I think we'll all qualify for the asylum if this

keeps up. I can't sleep o' nights, thinking about it all." 

The District Attorney, like Miss Gower, was losing flesh. His appetite had gone and insomnia had come.

Not only worry about Cameron, but all these maddening questions were robbing him of sleep. "Go where?" said Corrigan, waving his arms. "Where has the wagon to go to? Or are we to go to it? Maybe we'll find the comet and comma in the wagon." "Not a wagon; a van," insisted Bland. "There may be a great deal of difference. Van, for instance, can be part of a person's name, while wagon can't. 

And we don't know that the word which followed was 'go'. It may have been 'off'." "Look here : "That makes still worse sense." "Does it?" said Bland slowly. supposing that Cameron was trying to say Vornoff? 

Remember that the Professor has been in the tropics, not merely in Africa, but places like Trinidad and 

Martinique-and, no doubt, the Bahamas. Well, couldn't he have known Meier there ?" "Yes, of course, he's a likely answer. But I think the doc was right and we shouldn't pay attention to these ravings. They'll only ball us up worse—if that's possible." "The doctor thinks there's truth in them, and so do I," retorted Bland. "It may be that the Commissioner's still speaking in symbols, as he did that day to me. I mean a man's character doesn't change just because he's sick. Cameron's whole idea was to keep the secret to himself until he had proved his theory, meanwhile giving me a chance to see it for myself, and he may be sticking to that idea even

unconsciously. It may have become the dominant factor in his mind. I think it has." "Yes, I see what you mean." "And yet they mayn't be symbols," added Bland. "He may have been trying to say Vornoff. And by 'masterpiece' he means clearly a masterpiece of crime. 

He called it that to me, referring to the murder of Devore and Meier. Also his reference to a woman called Anna is plain enough. By the way, what is 

Mrs. Royal's first name?" "Pansy," said Corrigan. "It would be. Of course she looks physically capable of knocking out an elephant, let alone the Commissioner." "Miss Camblin's name is Madeline; Miss Gower's, Henrietta; and Mrs. Harrap's is supposed to be

Alice. There remains Mrs. Meier's, whose Christian name we don't know. Those five are the women involved in the case, though, of course, there may be any number of others. If only we could locate Mrs. 

Harrap or Mrs. Meier !" "You think they're the same party?" 1 Bland, who had considered himself the sole possessor of this idea, looked surprised and somewhat discomfited. We are all prone to think ourselves a little more clever than our neighbour. "Well, I shouldn't wonder if they are," he said. "Their descriptions tally, and it would explain a great deal." "There are quite a few well-built women in the world with black hair and brown eyes," said Corrigan drily. "Macford's dark himself, but he hasn't nigger blood. 

It wouldn't explain either why she should kill Devore." "It might if we knew everything," retorted Bland. "Or different people may be responsible for the two

murders. We can't ignore that possibility. Anyway, it's quite possible that Mrs. Meier killed her husband. 

You've spoken of her temper and the quarrels they had. Caring for him as she did, she might prefer to see him dead by her own hand rather than lose him to another woman." "In that case, according to his record, she'd have done for him long ago." "No. Premeditated murder has got to be worked up to, like premeditated anything. As I said before, she could stand his infidelities just so long. We all have a breaking-point. Besides, all Meier's other affairs were temporary. But supposing she knew that he meant to marry Miss Camblin if he could?

Supposing she knew it was a serious affair? Men of 

Meier's age get bitten like that. And she must have known, or she wouldn't have packed up and left.”

Translating all his recent thoughts on the matter into words, Bland went on to explain how Mrs. Meier could have read in the newspapers about Professor Vornoff and the black mamba venom. "Chance has helped her," he continued, "for Meier is now living at the same address as Vornoff-and you may be sure she knew where he moved to. She has been watching, waiting, and brooding; wanting to kill him, and deterred only by the thought of discovery. 

But now she sees a safe way. As Mrs. Harrap she changes her appearance, and anyway she's never at the Abercorn Arms when Meier's there. Very likely also that, though she knows Miss Camblin by sight, Miss Camblin doesn't know her. Wives always know their rivals, if their rivals don't know them." "You seem to know a lot about it for a bachelor," commented Corrigan. "Well then, what about the

weapon? Easy enough for her or anyone to get the poison, but how make the weapon?" "If such a weapon exists, somebody had to make it," retorted Bland. "It didn't grow. And what she couldn't do herself, she'd the money to get others to do. If we knew where she came from, her ancestry and early environment, it might lead us to the answer.

Anyway I'm pretty certain that Mrs. Harrap is Mrs. 

Meier, and that the Commissioner knew or guessed it. 

When we find one, we'll find the other." "Well, maybe," sighed Corrigan. "And maybe we'll find the meaning of a comma and a wagon and all the rest of it. I guess it's the only way we will find it, sort of accidental like, if the Commissioner don't come across with the answer to this supercrossword puzzle that's keeping more than you lying awake o' nights. Meanwhile I'm waiting for something else to drop the third murder." "There won't be a third," said Bland confidently. "Even if there was any reason for a third, the criminal daren't attempt it. His whole game now is to lie low. 

No, there won't be another one."

But, even as he said this, there came news of another one.

CHAPTER XXII  

THE scene of this other murder, however, was not the Abercorn Arms, nor did anyone know when or where it had been committed. That it had been discovered at all, was due solely to that chance which seems to stalk abroad in the name of justice, operating sooner or later, and lending truth to the old adage that murder will out.

The scene of the discovery, if not the actual crime, was a storage warehouse on the Upper West Side. It seemed that among their clients was a gentleman who had gone abroad the previous year. This person, explaining that his stay in Europe would be indefinite, and that he had sublet his flat, stored a few articles which he did not wish the subtenant to use. 

Among them was a large and strong packing-case which he said contained precious books. Books are notoriously heavy, however light their reading matter may be, and so the weight did not provoke remark.

All went well, and should have continued to do so indefinitely, but for the aforementioned intrusion of chance. As time passed, business, which had been none too good for the storage company, improved to such an extent that floor space became increasingly difficult to find. This necessitated more economy in the arranging of articles, and during the most recent 222

of these operations a large iron trunk fell accidentally on the alleged box of books.

For all its stoutness, and designed to withstand successfully any ordinary rough treatment, the box could not cope with such an assault, and, though by no means demolished, a breach was made in it large enough to disclose the curious fact that these books were so precious as to be enclosed in a sort of leaden casket. This in itself might not have prompted investigation, but the leaden shell had also been punctured, and these books proceeded to emit a faint but unpleasant odour, quite unlike anything known in their line. 

Now neither Bland nor Corrigan was particularly interested in this harrowing tale; in fact, they were disgusted and irritated. It meant simply another murder mystery to solve, and they had more than enough to do. It had nothing whatever to do with the great Abercorn Arms mystery in which they were absorbed, and they could not give it any personal attention. So they thought, until apprised of the fact that the gentleman who had stored the precious books called himself Parker Dinsmore. "Yes, Parker Dinsmore-Henry Devore," repeated

Detective Delaven, who had amplified the initial news with this astounding revelation. "Of course he stored those things before he moved to the Abercorn Arms. 

You'll remember Macford saying he moved in a year ago come May. Well, it was in March of last year that he stored them." "And the victim's a woman ?” "Yes. She's over at the Morgue now." "I always thought he was something more than a philanderer," said Bland grimly. "That yarn of his,

The Murder in the Morgue, seems to have a new significance. Perhaps his victim is the blonde whom 

Macford saw that morning, and maybe she was his secretary before Miss Gower came.' "They don't know her, of course?" asked Corrigan. "Well, identification will be a hard job if she's been dead all this time. Nothing to identify her by ?" "Not a thing. But this is the funny part," said 

Delaven. "They say she looks like she was coffined only a short time ago. You see, that packing-case was hermetically sealed, and she might have stayed there till the cows come home if it hadn't been for that accident." "I see," nodded Bland. "Still, I didn't think that, even if sealed, there would be such remarkable preservation as you say. How was she killed?" "They don't know. There isn't a mark on her, nothing to show how she died. It mayn't even have been murder." "She wouldn't be there if it wasn't," retorted the 

District Attorney. "Well, this puts a new angle on

Devore, and maybe the mystery of the human bookcase." 

Corrigan and Bland boarded a police car and sped up to the Morgue, where they met the Chief Medical Examiner. "Arsenic," said Dr. Brockelbank. "I'm sure of it, even without a postmortem. I'm certain an analysis of the organs will prove it. It would account for the remarkable state of preservation which the method of interment couldn't wholly explain. Arsenic, of course, has that effect, though the murderer mayn't have known it." "Very likely not," agreed Bland. "Even if he

thought the body would never be found, he wouldn't knowingly use something that makes identification easier instead of harder." "I guess he'd use whatever came handiest," said 

Corrigan. "Arsenic's dead easy to get. That's why it's always been so popular as an angel-maker. But let's have a look at her."

The state of preservation was indeed remarkable, considering that the victim must have been dead for at least a year. She was not the blonde, however, that the District Attorney had suggested, but a pretty and very dark brunette. Her age was somewhere between thirty and forty. As Delaven had intimated, not a garment or article of any kind had been found in the packing-case, and everything by which she might be identified, such as jewellery, was missing. Naked as the day she came into the world, yet the same idea seemed to strike Bland and Corrigan at the same time. "I'm wondering," mused the latter, as they drew the sheet over the object on the slab, "if we've found 

Mrs. Herman Meier." "Not if I'm right and Mrs. Harrap is Mrs. Meier.

Obviously she couldn't have been working at the Abercorn Arms a few months ago, and dead a year ago. 

In all probability it's not Mrs. Meier. She fits the description we have of her, and it would explain why she hasn't turned up, so we jump to that conclusion." "It would explain more than that," said Corrigan. "I guess it would explain why Meier should kill Devore, even if it don't explain who killed Meier." "Well, we needn't start speculating along that line until she's been identified. Mr. Green knew Mrs. 

Meier well by sight, and the thing to do is have him

come down here as soon as he can, while we go up and see the storage people "

It was all very well to speak of the futility of speculation while the victim remained unidentified, but neither Corrigan nor Bland himself could refrain from doing so while on their way uptown. If the woman was indeed Mrs Meier, then the impenetrable mystery of the human bookcase might be starting to clear up at last. "It would show," said Corrigan, "that Devore must have known Meier before either of them moved to the 

Abercorn Arms. Maybe Mrs. Meier left her husband on account of Devore and not because of his carryings on." "She might have taken up with Devore out of pique, after Meier had shown he didn't want her. I think that the more likely explanation." "Well, anyway, Devore tires of her, as he tired of all his sweeties, and he finds he can't get rid of her like he did the others. She was all right when he was poor; she'd money and he could use it. But now his books are paying big and he can get lots of prettier and younger dames. She don't see it that way; she won't give him a free hand, and so he gives her a free pass to a better world. It's the old, old story, and he didn't write those slick crime stories for nothing." "Yes, he'd have it carefully planned down to the last detail," nodded Bland. "It wasn't done, of course, at his boarding-house in the Bronx. Perhaps at her home, wherever she lived, after leaving Meier. Or they were living together and he had established another identity under the name of Parker Dinsmore." "Yes, that name wasn't known then like it is now.

And he'd have to give a genuine address to the storage

company. If he hadn't been murdered he'd have got that packing-case out of storage. He hadn't figured on the name Parker Dinsmore becoming so well known in the meantime. He must have been in a jam, afraid to leave it there indefinitely, and yet afraid to remove it. That's why he let things slide for a year." "But all that doesn't explain why Meier should kill 

Devore," said Bland. "That hypothesis is only tenable if we suppose that Meier loved his wife and didn't love Miss Camblin." "Well, we've really no evidence that he preferred Miss Camblin. There's nothing but talk. Mrs. Meier's packing up and getting out could be explained just as well by her loving Devore as by Meier loving somebody else." "Yes, but Meier's will isn't talk," said Bland. "What reason had he, then, for making Miss Camblin the principal legatee? I can't see any sense in the theory of him killing Devore unless, as I say, he still loved his wife. Why kill a man for relieving him of a burden? 

And, if he knew Devore had murdered Mrs. Meier, why not, in any case, inform the police ?" "He didn't know it, and nobody else either. That's clear," said Corrigan. "He didn't know where she'd gone to, and he killed Devore simply for taking her away from him." "Who, then, killed Meier, and why?" "Meier may have killed himself, accidentally or on purpose, with the same weapon he used on Devore. 

I've just thought of that." "You might have saved yourself the trouble.

Leaving out everything else that goes to show it was murder, if Meier killed himself, why didn't we find the weapon? Even if he'd hidden it, for some reason,

we'd have found it. It isn't in the flat. We know that much. Whatever it is the murderer may be after, it isn't the weapon." "Well, anyway, we know that Devore killed somebody," said Corrigan, scratching his head. "Whether it's Mrs. Meier or not, he killed some woman-and that explains what the Commissioner meant by a wagon." "You mean a van." "Yes, a moving-van. The body went to the storage company in a moving-van. That's what the Commissioner was trying to tell us. 'Van, go, case'-go to the storage company and find the case that was sent in the van. That's what was sticking in his mind. 

It's as plain as day now."

But it became less plain when they interviewed the manager of the storage warehouse. The packing-case had not come in a moving-van, nor even an express wagon, but an ordinary automobile. "I remember it perfectly well, as if it happened yesterday," said the manager; "for I was here in the office when Mr. Dinsmore drove up. It was a Saturday morning, March fifteenth." 

March fifteenth! On the same date, a year later,

Devore had been murdered. Was there any connection? Was this the reference to the Ides of March? "There were no preliminary arrangements, you see," said the manager. "It was Mr. Dinsmore's first and last visit. I never saw him again. The packing-case, and a few other articles, were in the tonneau of the automobile." "Was he alone ?” "Yes. He explained about his going abroad, said that the European representative of his firm had died

and he had to take his place till they found another. 

Everything had been done in a rush, and now, after letting his flat, he was storing his precious books and some personal belongings." "Yes, a case of books alone might look funny, so he had to have some other articles. What address did he give ?" "Poste Restante, London, England-the same as our General Delivery, I understand. You see, he paid for a year's storage in advance, cash down, and there was no need to give any New York address. And he didn't know yet where he'd be located abroad. He said he'd write if he wasn't back at the end of the year, but he was sure he would be." "You'd no idea at the time he was Parker Dinsmore, the novelist ?" "Not the slightest. I'm not a reading man and that name meant nothing to me. I understand it didn't mean much to anybody a year ago, that his success has been real sudden." "Yes, a sort of freak of chance. But you'd heard or read, of course, about his death at the Abercorn 

Arms ?" "I never connected that with him," said the manager. "It's the name Henry Devore, not Parker Dinsmore, that's been figuring in the papers. And it wasn't as if Mr. Dinsmore had been making quarterly payments, as if he'd written to keep himself alive in my mind.

We've been very busy, and I'll say frankly that I forgot all about him. It wasn't until to-day, when the accident happened, that I remembered his year was up and he was overdue on his next payment. "And I'm not sure even now," concluded the manager, "that Devore and our client were the same

man. 

Although Parker Dinsmore isn't a common name, it's quite possible for more than one person to have it. You see, from what I understand, Devore was a tall, thin dark, good-looking man, while the 

Dinsmore I knew certainly wasn't." 

Bland and Corrigan, who had been asking questions in turn, stared at each other blankly. This was a development they had never expected. "What did he look like ?" they demanded in unison. "Why, he was short and fat, and his hair-what he had left of it-was blond, going grey. He was a gross sort of man in a way. He looked more like what he said he was, a certain type of successful business man, than a writer." "Why, that's Herman Meier !" exclaimed Corrigan.

CHAPTER XXIII THE GREAT MYSTERY REMAINS UNSOLVED 

THERE could be no question that the owner of the packing-case was the wholesale fruiterer, precisely as there could be no question that the contents of the case was his wife. Meier's lawyer, Mr. Green, iden- 

So did others; these including tenants of the Belvedere Apartments, where the couple had last lived together. tified them both.

Here at last, then, logic seemed to proceed to a definite end instead of losing itself in a blind alley, as it had done in everything else pertaining to the mystery of the human bookcase. For whereas it was not entirely logical why Devore should have committed the crime, Meier himself furnished a wholly logical answer. "I never believed that Meier and Devore knew each other before they moved to the Abercorn Arms," said Bland. "This supports the evidence that the woman didn't want to lose her husband, but that he was determined to get rid of her. And he got rid of her the only way he could. It explains those quarrels, the use of a packing-case—a lot of things. I told you the man was a vicious brute." "Yes, and you also told me you always knew Devore was more than a philanderer," said Corrigan. "You didn't put this job past him any more than I did. 231

He seemed the only answer, but now we know the right one. And I guess we'll find that Meier did the job at the Belvedere after long and careful planning." "Yes, Doctor Brockelbank says that white arsenic is tasteless. Meier could have given her a fatal liquid dose in anything. He must have planned it to coincide with the time when it was known they were moving." "Yes, and I'll bet he prepared the case down at his business premises after hours. Then he brings it home to pack his precious books in, or maybe it comes as a case of fruit." "Then he gives out that his wife has left, or perhaps he doesn't say anything at the time," nodded Bland. "The neighbours may think she's at their new home. 

The packing-case doesn't attract any more attention than the other personal belongings that he gets the janitor or hall-boy to help load into the hired automobile. A cold-blooded, ruthless scoundrel. He must have watched while his wife retched her life away. He must have stifled her cries when she found he had no intention of calling a doctor." "Whoever did for him, did right," said Corrigan. "It saved the State a job. But why did he take the name Parker Dinsmore? Wouldn't that go to show he knew Devore before he went to the Abercorn Arms? 

Maybe he wanted, in case of discovery, to throw the job on to him.” "I don't think so," dissented the District Attorney. "According to all the available evidence, he never knew that Devore was Parker Dinsmore. I believe

Macford was right, and that nobody at the Abercorn Arms knew it. I think the likely explanation is that 

Meier took this alias and thought it original. The

THE GREAT MYSTERY REMAINS UNSOLVED 

233 subconscious mind can play queer tricks. He wasn't an imaginative man, and finding an original name is much harder than it may seem.' "You mean he may have seen the name Parker 

Dinsmore on one of Devore's books?" "Yes, and forgotten about it. Then, searching for a false name that would seem important, Parker Dinsmore pops into his head and he thinks it original. You must remember that at the time Devore was only beginning to become famous."

If the District Attorney's suggested explanation were true, then it presented another unique feature in a case already replete with the singular and astonishing.

Yet such unconscious plagiarism has been known in literature and elsewhere before this. Indeed, so common is it, that the law recognizes no copyright in titles. Not so singular that Meier should hit on the name Parker Dinsmore and think it original, as that the owner of the name should actually be living in the same house with him. "Well," summed up Corrigan, "if Mrs. Meier had nothing to do with the quarrel between her husband and Devore; if they weren't more than neighbours, and Meier took the name like you say, then we don't know any more than we ever did. If it clears up the mystery of Mrs. Meier's disappearance, it clears up nothing else." 

Obviously not. It did not answer the question of who killed Devore and Meier, and tried to kill the

Commissioner of Police. The great mystery, on which it seemed as though some light were to be thrown, remained as dark as ever. "It even knocks out what the Commissioner said about a van," complained Corrigan. "But maybe he
