took it for granted that the storage company moved 

Meier's things from the Belvedere." "But how could he know, in the first instance, that

Nobody else did; not even tenants of the Belvedere, who, if anyone, might have some reason for suspicion." "Well, you know the Commissioner," retorted 

Corrigan with pride. "He isn't like anybody else. He knew, all right, but don't ask me how." "I don't believe he knows anything about it," asserted Bland. "I don't believe that his reference to a van has anything to do with Mrs. Meier's murder. 

If he had even suspected the truth, his first action would have been to take some steps to prove it. That would be the natural and logical thing to do, and he's nothing if not logical, even in his most Gallic moments." "Well, maybe he did prove it, and kept it up his sleeve with the rest of what he knows." "The only way to prove it would be by going to the storage company, and we know that didn't happen. 

No, I'm sure that all this about Meier will be as big a surprise to Cameron as it was to us." "Can't be any bigger, that's certain." "I'm sure it's a development he never considered," continued Bland, "and that it has nothing to do with what he was considering. It's an extraneous matter, useful only as a footnote to Herman Meier's character. 

And it proves also, I think, that he cared for Miss Camblin. But I don't believe it will affect or alter in any degree whatever solution Cameron has in mind. 

Finally, it's useful in showing us that the woman at the hospital wasn't Mrs. Meier. She's one suspect ruled out of the case, and that's something."

THE GREAT MYSTERY REMAINS UNSOLVED 

235 "Well, maybe you're right," conceded Corrigan. "Like I said, we seem to be back just where we started from, and we'll never know the right answer till the 

Commissioner talks some sense. But, if he don't mean a moving-van, I'd like to know what kind of a wagon he does mean. Yes, and a few other things." "Perhaps we'll know to-night," said the District 

Attorney hopefully, as he prepared to leave for the hospital.

Good news, the best possible, awaited him. The peak of delirium was safely passed, the fever abated, and Cameron had taken a sudden and decided turn for the better. For the first time in many anxious days he had dropped into profound, untroubled sleep. "He's turned the corner and he'll be all right now," beamed the house physician. "Coming along handover-fist. When he slides out of this twenty-hour nap, he'll be wanting something more than barley water. Oh, his mind will be all right; no fear that the tale we've been giving to the papers will come true. Men like that, when they do die, never die at the top. If you want to live long, keep your mind active."

But only one new word had been added to the meagre collection of the official stenographer, who now abandoned his thankless task. For to-morrow 

Cameron should be able at last to speak for himself, talk rationally and coherently. Meanwhile, true to his character, he had taken the solution of the problem with him into health-giving oblivion. The new word he had uttered, not once but several times, was "picture", and it told Bland no more than did the other five mentioned. 

The District Attorney went home at length, filled

with a profound sense of gratitude to the Almighty, and that feeling of ease and power, that levitation of spirits, which follows the lifting of a heavy load of worry. Kirker Cameron was on the highroad to recovery, and that was all that really mattered. And to-morrow, at the latest, this cursed mystery should be solved. 

Yes, cursed. It had kept him from sleeping, from eating; it had driven him almost insane. If only he could solve it, have the murderer under lock and key before to-morrow! He wanted so hard to do that, craved it more than he had craved anything in his life. To pluck victory from defeat! Through him the murderer had escaped. If only he could retrieve that blunder! "And the answer's there," he thought as, for the first time in days, he sat down to dinner with something of his old appetite. "It's in that room of Meier's. 

It's among all those things that Cameron said. I'm sure of it. It's staring me in the face somewhere. 

Why haven't I the intelligence to see it?"

Dinner over, he went into the study and brought out the paper on which he had written all those maddening questions. To these he proceeded to add the words that Cameron had uttered in delirium. Surely now, his mind relieved of its principal worry, he might see the answer. His brain should work with unusual clarity; he should receive an inspiration, some clue that would lead to a solution of the riddle. By George, if hard work and concentration meant anything, he'd succeed! He'd tear the vitals out of this cursed mystery somehow. 

He began with high heart and a cigar. Yes, the thing could be done if tackled calmly and scientifically

THE GREAT MYSTERY REMAINS UNSOLVED 

237 with one's whole mind. That had been the trouble hitherto, not only with him, but with Corrigan and the others. Too many things to think about; too many theories. But this was different; now he had internal peace and he could work at it scientifically, as though it were a bridge or crossword puzzle. Now, why was the light burning in the kitchenette and nowhere else? What difference did it make where 

Devore, but not Meier, was killed?

An hour passed, and the pile of cigar ashes in the tray had grown higher, while Bland had lost something of his scientific calm. His face was rather flushed, his eyes bright. A sheet of paper was covered with various questions and answers, none of which satisfied. Hang it all, what could a comma have to do with it? Why was Devore struck in the neck, Meier the leg? What importance were Meier's clothes? 

Another hour gone, the room blue with smoke, ashes scattered over the desk, and Bland's face congested. He had removed coat and collar. What scientific fact had Meier known? Who the devil was 

Anna? What had Cameron seen that wasn't there? 

Above eye level. Above eye level. Damnation ! 

The clock struck one. Bland, his eyes frenzied, his face flaming, was now pacing the floor like a caged lunatic. But, by Jupiter, he wasn't beaten! No, sir, he'd tear the vitals from this mystery before daylight. But studying that paper was worse than useless. The real answer lay in Meier's flat. Something above eye level. By George, he'd go and tear that bedroom to pieces! No matter if it had been ransacked before. The answer must be there. He would search for it again, calmly and scientifically.

CHAPTER XXIV  

It was nearing two o'clock when Bland reached the Abercorn Arms, for he had walked from his home, the better to achieve that calm and scientific frame of mind essential to this final assault on the problem. It was a mild spring night, or morning, and a fresh wind from the Hudson combined with the exercise to relieve the state of mental congestion, the spiritual frenzy, into which he had succeeded in working himself. 

The human bookcase was in darkness, save for the single light that burned in the hall, and the front room of Meier's flat. Hamilton Jones must have gone to his Harlem home, and everybody else to bed except the Headquarters man keeping his lonely vigil.

Bland wondered if, picked men though they were, the Headquarters detectives actually stayed awake all night. There was every temptation not to do so, for none could be any the wiser. It was a monotonous assignment, and it must seem to them futile and unnecessary. Night after night now one of them had been on guard and nothing had happened, not the vestige of an alarm. Hogan was the man on duty to-night. Well, this was his last vigil, thought Bland, for even if the secret of the room should not be discovered, Cameron would reveal it on the morrow. 

How was he to get in? The front door was locked, 238

of course, and he must ring up Feigel or Macford. But that would mean explanations and more lies. They would be sure to ask for the latest report on Cameron Feigel had shown a really touching solicitude, perhaps because his sojourn "in the shade" had not been revealed by Centre Street-and Bland had no intention yet of making the good news known to the public -and the murderer. 

Bland did not balk at necessary lies, but he hated unnecessary trouble. Much better if he could get in without arousing the janitor or superintendent. And perhaps it would be as well if he could surprise Hogan, see how he was performing his monotonous duty. For all those lighted windows he might be sound asleep.

The mere thought that these watchdogs might thus neglect their duty, and that the murderer could accomplish his purpose, was alarming.

Now the obvious method of clandestine entrance was the window of Devore's flat. Easily reached from the steps, as Macford had said; one had only to lean over and stretch out an arm while holding on to the pillar flanking the doorway. And there was a ledge beneath the window, broad enough at least for a toehold. 

Perhaps the joyous spring night had something to do with Bland's decision; it set the blood racing, as it set the sap mounting in the trees. It called to high adventure and romance, and he could have the former if not the latter. Many a time, on just such a night in the long ago, had he shinned down the drainpipe by his bedroom window and gone adventuring with 

Cameron and the local juvenile gang. 

Perhaps also Bland wished to prove that, for all his fat and lost youth, he was still capable of such a

feat. Decidedly there was risk in it, not merely of being seen and mistaken for a burglar, but of breaking his neck. For beneath the narrow ledge yawned the deep, dark well leading to the janitor's quarters. Yet such risk, in his present mood, served as an additional lure. At least it would be a tale to tell Cameron, a practical demonstration that he was not the futile fool which their last adventure together pictured him. 

He craved removal of that unflattering picture from his own mental gallery. And so perhaps, in the last analysis, wounded vanity was the real instigator.

At all events, satisfied that the street was deserted, he now leaned over the yawning well and grasped the stone edge framing the nearer window of Devore's flat. Then, thus supported, he stepped along the narrow ledge, his back to the street, his toes alone finding a foothold, and his right hand pressed against the wall.

A dangerous but short passage. He could now support part of his weight on the window-sill. The window, of course, was keyed, but the blade of a penknife soon remedied that, and he was in the flat. 

The District Attorney for New York County had perpetrated this little bit of housebreaking quite as capably as might Feigel himself, or any professional whom he had prosecuted. He had not forgotten the days of his youth.

Mindful of his recent experience in this same room, he felt his way carefully toward the kitchenette. For, not contemplating such a mode of entrance, he had not brought a torch. He was thoroughly familiar with the plan of the flat, and he had the borrowed light from the street to aid him. That, however, was a poor substitute at best and, the farther he went from

the front windows, the darker it became, for there was no light in the airshaft. 

Not very pleasant, this groping one's way on premises where a man had met a mysterious and terrible death. Unpleasant enough even when accompanied by such a person as Kirker Cameron. But soon he would be upstairs in the light with Hogan.

Meanwhile his subconscious mind continued to grapple with the problem that he was making such a last desperate effort to solve. By his reference to a picture, did Cameron mean one of the pictures in 

Meier's bedroom? But they had been examined meticulously. However, he would examine them again. Was Vornoff the culprit? Had Cameron been trying to tell them that? 

In the kitchenette, without the slightest warning, he received one of the greatest shocks of his life. Feeling his way with groping hand he touched something soft but resilient, something cold and round and long. It was draped over a chair by the table as though waiting for him. 

He started back, heart racing and the cold sweat of fear upon him. Devore had been found in this room. Contrary to Cameron's belief, had the death blow been delivered here-and by this ?” 

For a few moments Bland hovered on the brink of utter panic; then memory of the sense of touch, if not sight, assured him that the object could be nothing but a piece of rubber tubing. It took courage to verify this explanation of reason; he dare not strike a match, and he must stretch forth a hand in the dark and grope anew for the object hanging over the back of the chair. But Bland did so. And, though never boasting of the adventure, he liked to think afterwards

that, alone there in the dark, with none to see how he acted, he had not played the coward. We are all a bit braver, when it comes to the pinch, than we imagine. 

Yes, a length of rubber tubing. What was it doing there? Emphatically it had not been there before, or he could not have failed to see it. His eyesight was not so bad as that. Even if he had failed, Cameron and the rest should have seen it. It would have provoked some inquiry. How had it got there? Who had been in Devore's flat? Could it have anything to do with the murders, the scientific fact that Meier knew?

Guessing thus wildly at its meaning, Bland stuffed the rubber tubing in his pocket and turned to the window, which he opened gently. He climbed to the second floor and entered the kitchenette of Meier's flat. 

This room, like the bedroom, was in darkness save for the borrowed light that came from the front room.

Evidently Hogan had not heard him, and he stepped softly to the door from which one might have a vista of the other two rooms. Precisely as he had expected!

There lay the Headquarters detective sprawled in an easychair, dead to the world. 

Yes, dead to the world. His was the sleep of death. 

Noon of the following day. Bland and Corrigan in the room of the private ward, and Cameron propped over a bed-tray finishing a substantial meal. Though pale and gaunt, he looked remarkably fit, considering the nature of his illness; while Bland and Corrigan, who had been up all night, looked exhausted from futile endeavour and worry. "Oh, I'm quite able to listen, and talk too," Cameron

was saying with an impatient gesture. "I'm damned sorry about poor Hogan. How was he killed? Carry on with the tale." "He was strangled," said Corrigan. "No fingerprints. They used a cord," said the 

District Attorney. "At first I thought it might have been the rubber tubing I found, but Feigel explained that. It seems he had been in Devore's flat to fix something, and he left it by mistake. It belongs to an old gas-iron." "I see. And how long had Hogan been dead ?" "Brockelbank says about half an hour. His body was quite warm." "There isn't a scrap of evidence against anybody," said Corrigan wearily. "Just like the other cases.

Everybody was asleep, so far as we can prove to the contrary. No use watching the place any longer. 

The murderer got whatever he was after." "I'm sorry, Kirk," said Bland miserably. "I thought 

I could repair that bloomer I made. Yes, the murderer has won out, thanks to me. He has got what he wanted; and now, even if you were sure of his identity, how could you ever prove his guilt? He's made good his escape." "Don't worry about that," said Cameron. "If he's got what he wanted, and we don't know what it is, why try to escape ?" "Then it is a man !" exclaimed Bland. "What's his name? Who is it you suspect?" "Perhaps we'll see, Jerry. Give me time to finish this square meal and I'll be right with you." "What do you mean? going to the Abercorn Arms. Yes, to-day, now. You needn't argue about it; I'm going if it's the last thing I do on this earth. Meanwhile, what's this I hear about Mrs. Meier being found in a storage warehouse? The doctor was telling me-proof that he thinks I'm quite able to talk business.”

Thus Bland had been right in at least one instance, the murder of Mrs. Meier coming as a complete surprise to Cameron. "No, I'd never considered that. Why should I?” he said, as the District Attorney and Corrigan told the story between them. "In fact, I never considered

Well, it has certainly a great deal to do with the solution of the case-in fact, everything." "Then it isn't a man?" said the bewildered Corrigan.

Bland produced the paper on which he had written all the other things Cameron had said, and he waved it accusingly at the silent man in the bed. "If you won't tell us even now whom you suspect, you might at least explain some of these," he exclaimed. "They've pretty nearly driven us crazy. What in thunder is it in Meier's bedroom? What did you see that isn't there ?" 

Cameron smiled as Corrigan described how the six of them had searched the room from floor to ceiling,

I'm willing to omit those words you said in delirium, but I challenge you to give a reasonable and legitimate answer to the others.

CHAPTER XXV  

It is to be imagined that the hospital authorities had something to say concerning Cameron's determination to go out the first day of his convalescence, but they might as well have expostulated with the west wind. It was a warm, sunny day, explained Cameron blandly, and an automobile ride would do him a world of good.

Perhaps the doctors realized that it would do him more harm if they tried to prevent him, for he was keyed-up and in a state of mental tension. This condition of mind was shared by Bland and Corrigan, who found themselves at last nearing a solution of the great crime puzzle. At last they would learn the answer to those maddening questions, and the identity of the person whom Cameron suspected. 

Perhaps Cameron's mental state had something to do with his remarkable powers of recuperation, and perhaps his strength was more that of the spirit than flesh, but at all events, when he left the rear entrance of the hospital, none would have thought that he had arisen so recently from a sick-bed. He declined the aid of his companions, while one might have imagined the walking-stick he carried to be more ornamental than useful. A slouch hat was pulled well down over his pale, gaunt face; while a silk muffler, worn 347

under his light overcoat, helped to conceal his features.

The morning papers had intimated merely a change for the better in Cameron's condition, and certainly no passerby who now chanced to see him would have thought for a moment that it was the Police Commissioner himself. Nor did anyone have much opportunity to see him; a police car, with Detective Delaven in attendance, was waiting, and they were soon on their way to the Abercorn Arms. 

Cameron sat for some time in silence, hands folded on the head of his cane, chin resting on hands. They could only see the top of his hat, and he seemed to have fallen asleep. Then he glanced up suddenly and said: "So poor Hogan was strangled-or rather garrotted? 

And there were no signs of a struggle?" "None," replied Bland. "If there'd been a fight it would have wakened Vornoff.” "The "So one would imagine," nodded Cameron. 

Professor couldn't have been asleep very long, unless he changed his usual habit and went to bed early. But 

Hogan was a powerful man, and he should have given a good account of himself." "He must have been taken by surprise," said 

Corrigan. "Like I told you, the murderer must have got in by the fire escape. Then he hides and waits for Hogan. Or maybe he makes a noise on purpose.

Hogan comes to see what it is, and the strangling cord is whipped over his head and round his neck before he knows what's happened." "In skilled hands a highly efficacious way of committing murder," nodded Cameron. "The Dacoits, those Thugs of India, have been partial to it from

the remotest times, while the Parisian Apache practises it extensively. But that postulates not only a certain efficiency on the part of the murderer, but a certain inefficiency on the part of Hogan. I don't like to think of one of my picked men not only failing to hear the murderer enter, but falling so ingenuously into such an ambush." "But you and I have proved," said Bland, "that one could enter like that and not be heard, unless somebody happened to be in the kitchenette." "Quite so. It's the ambush, Jerry, that's sticking in my craw. Hogan was a seasoned hand." "Well, but supposing he'd fallen asleep?" suggested the District Attorney. "He sits down in that comfortable chair and has forty winks, perhaps without meaning to. Of course he'd leave the light on, so that it wouldn't be thought he was asleep. Easiness itself for the murderer to slip up and put the cord round his throat." "Yes, that explanation would eliminate the unflattering ambush," agreed Cameron. "But everything would depend on Hogan's attitude, how he was sitting. If he fell asleep with his head pressed against the back of the chair, how could you slip anything round his neck without waking him? Yes, possible to do it, perhaps, but all the odds are against it. To be wholly plausible we must imagine he fell asleep chin on breast, or with his head inclined over the side of the armchair." "Well, what's against that theory?" demanded 

Bland. "Don't people fall asleep as often in the one position as the other ?” "Yes, there's no standardizing the embrace of 

Morpheus, my friend. But what was Hogan strangled

with? You say a cord, but there are many kinds of cords." "Rope would be more like it," remarked Corrigan. "We can't make out what was used, but it was something far thicker or broader than cord." "That's why I thought of the rubber tubing," said 

Bland. "You see, it must have been something soft, if tough, for it didn't cut into the flesh. I mean there were no abrasions. Brockelbank said the tubing might have done it." "But you're satisfied it didn't?" "We've got to be," grunted Corrigan. "We can't hang anything on Feigel. Macford backs him up about doing some repairs in Devore's flat. If it seems a bit fishy why Feigel happened to leave that tubing behind him, why should he leave it after killing Hogan ? 

That would be still more fishy." "Yet if he could forget it in the one instance, he could forget it in the other," pointed out Bland. "But 

I don't see any reason why he should visit Devore's flat after leaving Meier's. Of course, in the first instance, he'd have to reach the fire escape through 

Devore's flat, but coming down he could use the fold-up section that he couldn't reach from the court." "He might have been scared of making a noise,” suggested Corrigan. "He might have come back through Devore's flat the way he went. I think that bit of tubing's a strong clue, but so far we've got nothing to show that Feigel wasn't asleep like he says." 

Had Hogan been killed by the same weapon, if not in the same manner, as the one that killed Devore and 

Meier ? Bland saw again in his mind's eye that discoloured band midway between Hogan's collar and

chin. Was the weapon capable of inflicting death in two different ways? Although he did not know very much about reptiles, the District Attorney was aware that a constrictor is not venomous. But might not a venomous snake be capable of thus strangling a victim?

He was about to put this question to Cameron when the car pulled up at the Abercorn Arms. "Now, Delaven," said Cameron, "the rest of us are going to Meier's flat, but you're to remain in the lower hall. You needn't pay any attention to whoever may come in, but your job is to see that nobody goes out. 

And whoever may come in must remain. Is that clear ?" "Yes, sir." "Nobody," repeated Cameron with emphasis. "Detain them on any pretext you like, or by force if necessary. Put the handcuffs on them at the first sign of trouble. Nobody should be going out at this hour, but have your gun handy. Remember that you may have to deal with a ruthless and desperate murderer." "If that's the case," said Corrigan, "hadn't we better have more than one man on the job? What's to stop a party leaving some other way?" "But we don't know that the murderer is on the premises," said Cameron, "and any outside reception committee would only serve as a warning. Delaven's a host in himself and, as I say, I don't think he'll have to hold up any innocent person."

For by now it was the dead hour of the day, between three and four o'clock, when the human tide has ceased to ebb and flow in apartment houses. Everybody in the Abercorn Arms, if they had pleasure or

business abroad, would have left; while any visitors would not be thinking of leaving for another hour. 

On hearing their steps, Hamilton Jones came sauntering out from the alcove. Recognizing the 

Police Commissioner, he looked the astonishment he must have felt. "W-why, sir, I'd no idea you were able to be out of bed!" he exclaimed in his curiously cultured voice. "I certainly am glad to see you're so much better, sir." "Thank you," said Cameron. "If you're wanting the superintendent, he isn't in, sir," added the hall-boy, still staring as though hardly able to credit his eyes. "We're going up to Meier's flat," said Cameron. "By the way, Jones, how are the affairs of the heart progressing?" he added, as they entered the elevator. "No more attempts by irate females to sever your jugular?" "What do you mean, sir?" "I mean that little affair with Miss Hannah of 

Harlem, when she pulled a razor on you, you know." 

This was Bland's first intimation that Cameron actually knew the name, Christian or otherwise, of the lady in question. He himself had considered the incident of no importance, but evidently nothing was too insignificant for Cameron or Centre Street to investigate. "Oh, that!" said Jones with a gesture of dismissal. "She was only fooling, sir. She really didn't mean anything." "I see. Nothing but jealousy, eh? Perhaps she mistook your attitude towards a certain tenant of this house ?" 

Jones gave all his attention to the elevator.

Cameron's last question, which he made no attempt to answer, seemed to have upset him, for he passed the second floor and had to return to it. And when his passengers had left he stood staring after them for some time, a surprised and uneasy expression stamped on his handsome features. 

Although Corrigan had said that no longer was there any use guarding Meier's flat, a detective was still on duty and he opened to their ring. "Go down to the entrance hall and give Delaven a hand if he needs it," ordered Cameron, and closed the door on the other's back. 

Bland and Corrigan expected some questions about

Hogan, where he had been sitting, and so on, but Cameron marched straight into the bedroom without saying another word. The room was as it had been, the window still open at the bottom, and he sat down on the bed facing it, as he had done on that memorable former occasion. "Well, here we are," he said at length, his face pale and tired looking, but his eyes bright and ardent. He fished in a pocket and brought out his pipe, which he sucked and gestured with but did not light. "Now let us go back to that day and the genesis of the riddle about what I saw, yet didn't see. I had remarked that Meier knew a scientific truth which Devore didn't, and you asked how I knew it. I replied that my eyes assured me it was so, and that I'd seen it the first time I entered this room, though I didn't draw the proper deduction. Is that right?" "Yes," nodded Bland, his mouth dry with excitement. "Your exact words were, 'I saw it, I see it, but it isn't there'. You said nothing had been changed or altered but Meier's clothes, and you gave me to

understand that this important something was above eye level." "Good," said Cameron. "We're agreed then on the way the riddle was stated. I'm not prepared, of course, to give a reasonable and legitimate answer to something I never said. But I certainly said all that we've mentioned. And you don't see the answer even now?" "No," said Bland, and Corrigan shook his head. "Amazing," murmured Cameron, and pointed with his pipe to the top of the window. "That's above eye level, isn't it? And it's a simple scientific fact, is it not, that for a room to be effectively aired, a window must be open at both bottom and top? The stale air must have a means of getting out while the fresh air comes in. 'I saw it, I see it, but it isn't there'. Precisely. I saw that the window wasn't down from the top, I still see that it isn't, and therefore the scientific truth isn't there. Is that plain, reasonable, and legitimate ?" 

Bland and Corrigan looked at each other. No wonder they had been unable to find anything! No wonder that Cameron had said nothing had been altered or changed but Meier's clothes! No wonder there had been nothing to see. "Yes, that's all right," conceded Corrigan at length. "I guess it's entirely reasonable and legitimate as far as it goes, but it don't go far enough.” "Hardly," said Bland with an air of acute disappointment and resentment. "As the window wasn't open at the top, what scientific truth did Meier know? That's the point." "That it wasn't open at the top on the night he

died, doesn't prove that it wasn't open on every other night," replied Cameron. "It don't prove anything else either," retorted 

Corrigan. "We might just as well suppose that 

Devore's was open on every other night too." "You might, but not I," said Cameron with a smile. "Why not?" "Because I know that Devore never opened his bedroom window at the top, while Meier did.” "Why, then, wasn't it open ?" demanded Bland. "Because Meier was felled by the fatal blow before he could open it," replied Cameron. "How do you know that?" demanded Corrigan. 

And Bland said: "What reason have you for thinking he made a practice of opening it at the top? What evidence is there?" "Take the fact of "Facts," replied Cameron.

Devore's height and Meier's height. Devore was tall-over six feet. He could press down the top half without standing on anything. But Meier was short, and, to exert the necessary pressure, he would need to increase his height. Moreover, you'll find the upper half of that window a bit hard to move. I did." 

Bland and Corrigan looked more bewildered than ever. Evidently the solution of the whole problem was within reach, yet it still eluded them. "But what evidence is there to prove that Meier had any idea of opening the window from the top ?" demanded Bland. "He was struck on the leg, wasn't he?" replied 

Cameron. "Good heavens, can't you two see it even now?" he added impatiently. "What would he stand on ? 

What does anybody stand on? Look, man, look!" And he pointed at the high-backed chair by

the window. "Don't you remark anything singular about it ?" "Well," said Corrigan as he scratched his head, "it's the same kind of chair like there is in Devore's flat. I noticed that long ago, of course.

I guess everybody did. But there's nothing singular about that." "No, nothing to provoke remark," agreed Cameron. "It's a conventional design, and we may expect the same kind of chairs in flats that are rented furnished. 

I daresay there may be others of the same pattern in 

Vornoff's or Gloz's flat. Yes, a conventional design, gentlemen, but useful. You'll find that if a person of

Devore's height sat in that chair the lion's head would come directly opposite his neck, while if a person of Meier's height stood on it--but you now see the answer?"

Curious how the seemingly insignificant can suddenly become of the most tremendous and sinister importance ! Bland remembered how, on his first visit with Cameron to Devore's flat, the high-backed chair topping the desk had been the first thing he saw. He remembered even thinking how fatuous the lion's yawn or snarl had seemed. And now, here in Meier's flat, he had been looking at this same chair, intermittently over a period of days, without having the faintest suspicion of what must be its deadly secret. Inhuman instrument acting in conjunction with human. Oh, decidedly. Not a snake, but a chair! "So O'Reilly's idea, which you told me, was right.

It was the concealed weapon which the murderer was after," added Cameron. "You see now why it had to be left behind for the time being. A chair can't beconveniently-placed in one's pocket."

"But how does the infernal thing work?" asked the amazed Corrigan. "I've seen many queer weapons in my time, but nothing like that. You mean the needle comes out of the lion's mouth ?" "Needles," corrected Cameron. "Curved precisely like a snake's fangs. Observe what a substantial article that chair is; note the breadth and depth of the frame. Plenty of room for everything; and not very much room is required. A simple but ingenious piece of mechanism, yet there's nothing new in crime. The 

Borgias, they say, had something similar. At any rate, you'll find traces of these death chairs or beds or divans in Oriental history. A modern improvement, that's all. An advance on the famous Carhart case." "Well, I'm damned!" exclaimed Corrigan as he approached the chair warily. "I can't see anything queer about it. It looks just like a regular chair. 

Why, I've even sat in the darned thing! Why didn't it work then ?” "Obviously because it wasn't cocked or set," replied 

Cameron. "And probably its reservoir of black mamba venom is exhausted. If not, and if there had been any opportunity to reset it, poor Hogan might have died a harder death than he did." "But look here !" exclaimed Corrigan, who was cautiously inserting a pencil between the gaping jaws of the lion. "This thing isn't hollow; it's solid wood clear through !" "Yes, solid as your head," nodded Cameron. "Of course the real weapon has been removed. That's a perfectly harmless replica. Don't you understand that there must be at least three chairs all looking precisely alike? There would be the innocent couple, one in Devore's flat and one here, and there would be

the death-chair which was exchanged with the other two. "Van Gogh," he added, "is famous for a certain picture, the subject of which is nothing but a chair. 

I think it's in the Tate Gallery in London, or was. 

That's what I meant by 'picture' and 'masterpiece'.

Evidently even in delirium I was trying to give you a hint. If you knew anything about the Dutch masterand I thought most everybody did-you'd think at once of this famous picture of the chair. Then you'd see why Meier was struck in the leg, and you'd guess about the window." "But I don't pore over old books and things like you," said Bland. "Yes, everything you've explained so far is reasonable and legitimate, but I'd no reason to suspect for a moment- 

A shot suddenly crashed out. It came echoing up the airshaft, and in that confined space it sounded like a small cannon. It seemed to come from the lower hall, from the alcove whose small window opened on the shaft. 

Corrigan turned and bolted for the door, the District

Attorney at his heels, while Cameron followed at a more leisurely pace.

In the entrance hall, opposite the alcove, Delaven and the other Headquarters man were bending over a grey-clad figure that lay face down in a curiously collapsed and crumpled heap. A dark and ever widening stain was creeping out from under this heap. "He pulled a gun on me!" exclaimed the astounded

Delaven. "I grabbed it, and it went off-plugged him clean through the heart. He must have gone crazy!" "We all have our breaking-point," said Cameron as he joined the group, and, leaning heavily on his cane,

contemplated sadly and without surprise or triumph the lifeless figure. "But perhaps he wasn't so crazy. 

If the State's death-chair is less painful than the one he conceived and made, it's equally sure." 

They carried the body of Donald Macford into his flat.

CHAPTER XXVI  

ERE the sudden tragedy happened, this fourth and final death in the Abercorn Arms, it was dawning on 

Bland and Corrigan that the superintendent must be the culprit for whom they had sought so long and fruitlessly. For who else had not merely the skill and materials for making such a weapon, but the opportunity of substituting it? 

Everything pointed with inexorable logic to Donald Macford. The bachelor flats were furnished by him; he was the only one who possessed a master-key, and with the hall-boy gone home and everybody in bed, he could transfer in the dead of night-the only possible time-the chair from his own flat. He was the logical thief of the black mamba venom, and of what use now were his watertight alibis when this instrument could commit murder automatically? 

Yes, the logical answer; so logical that Bland had dismissed him as being too obvious. And then, if further support were necessary, the alibis had convinced him of Macford's innocence. Also there was the apparent total lack of motive. Centre Street itself had shared the District Attorney's opinion; it was the Police Commissioner alone who had never wavered, Those alibis had not shaken him, nor the seeming lack of motive. He had held stubbornly to 260

his original idea, and he had proved his contention that the obvious is more often right than not. He

The strain and nervous tension which Macford had undergone can be imagined, and the murder of Hogan shows that he had reached the breaking-point. knew that Cameron must at least suspect something about the weapon, otherwise why lay that trap for him?

He must get that chair out of Meier's flat before Cameron recovered. He must destroy the only evidence against him, or, if Cameron suspected but did not actually know the nature of the weapon, he must remove its secret beyond reach of chance discovery. 

By placing a detective constantly on guard, Cameron had prevented him substituting a genuine chair, further proof if need be that the Police Commissioner was hot on the scent. And the detectives were still on duty.

Thus Macford, after many anxious days, was forced to the conclusion that he had only two alternatives; either he must leave the weapon where it was and risk the consequences, or he must commit another murder. 

For he was aware that, if discovered, he must kill; these detectives knew him, of course, and he must prevent them from revealing his identity. He was aware also that, with such men, discovery was inevitable. He knew, better than Bland, that they kept vigilant watch all night. They never turned out the light; he had seen it burning, their shadows on the blinds.

He knew that his only chance of success lay in trusting to the fact that the police, with the possible exception of Cameron, did not suspect him. Hogan would have no objection to having a friendly chat with him. And so, arming himself with some good

cigars and a large silk handkerchief, he waited until the hall-boy had gone and the tenants were in bed, then went boldly to Meier's flat. Hogan would chat, even if he would not let him in.

It will be seen, then, how entirely right Cameron had been in scouting the ambush theory. With Macford in mind, it was not difficult to imagine what had actually happened a proffered cigar, a word of condolence about the lonely and fruitless vigil, and then the silken band whipped round Hogan's unsuspecting neck.

Coming down to the final tragedy, it transpired that Macford entered the Abercorn Arms shortly after 

Cameron had gone up to Meier's flat with Bland and

Corrigan. Delaven was chatting in the hall with the other Headquarters detective, while Hamilton Jones was on the elevator. "He stopped and had a yarn with us," said Delaven. "He seemed all right, no more than naturally worried about the mystery of Hogan's death. And, of course, we didn't suspect him. We didn't say anything about you being upstairs—but the hall-boy did." 

Jones had appeared while the trio stood talking, and he made a very natural and inevitable remark about Cameron's astounding recovery. This must have come as a thunderbolt to Macford. Cameron able to be up and about! Cameron in Meier's flat! 

He must have seen in a flash how he had been fooled; seen, as though he were present, what was transpiring upstairs. Instead of being safe, he was in the greatest peril. 

Making some excuse, he started to leave by the front door, his only thought instant flight. If he had not lost his head, he should have known that he could escape through his flat and the antique store. But he was

verging on panic, and when Delaven prevented him leaving, his panic was complete. All his fears and suspicions were thus confirmed, and his frayed nerves went to pieces. Problematical whether he meant to shoot his way to freedom, or, realizing that the jaws of the trap had closed on him, he preferred the other way out. He had bought the pistol only that day. It was what he had gone out for-further proof that his nerve was breaking.

They found the death-chair in Macford's flat, or rather what was left of it. Macford had had no opportunity to destroy it utterly, or he had seen no immediate reason for doing so, and it was the knowledge that Cameron must find these remains and thus be able to reconstruct the weapon sufficiently to prove his guilt that prompted his last desperate act. "It's possible," said Cameron, "that the idea came to him from an actual chair of the kind. Of course I don't mean such an article as this, but some crude work of the Middle Ages. He dealt in antique furniture, and an actual chair may have come into his possession whose secret he alone happened to discover. Although a great deal of nonsense has been written about poisoning in the Middle Ages-arsenic was what was chiefly used -there's no doubt there were some such articles of the kind. And there's more than a legend behind the poisoned rings of the Borgias. "On the other hand, the famous Carhart case may have been his sole inspiration. You remember how, though claiming to recollect the case, he corrected

Doctor Golding, and said there were two punctures instead of one. I believe he made that mistake deliberately in order to demonstrate that his memory

wasn't so good as he claimed. A very clever and subtle character. "Yes, whether or not he actually came into possession of such a chair as I've mentioned, I think the Carhart case proved very useful to him. It showed him what to avoid as well as what to improve. I think it's obvious that he had this murder machine well under way before chance gave him the opportunity of using the black mamba venom. "Of course we've evidence," concluded Cameron, "that he was a highly skilled mechanic, an adept, not only with wood, but steel. And we know he had the tools, the workshop, the seclusion necessary for the secret manufacture of such an article. Let me see now if I can explain to your satisfaction how the diabolical contrivance worked." 

Unlike Cameron, neither Bland nor the others were of a particularly scientific or mechanical turn of mind, yet with such evidence as they were able to find, Cameron reconstructed the original mechanism so clearly before their mind's eye that they had no difficulty in grasping its simplicity and ingeniousness. "You see," said Cameron, "the automatic action was precisely that of a striking snake, that is, so far as the propulsion and injection of the venom is concerned. 

This little rubber bulb acted like the poison sacs. But, unlike a snake's, the fangs didn't lie along the upper jaw when not in action; you might have put a finger in that lion's mouth and felt nothing. All you could have noticed was that it had a remarkably deep throat. 

But who would think of probing it?" "Not me, certainly," said Corrigan, ungrammatically and emphatically. "I wasn't looking for a piece of furniture."

"Exactly," said Cameron. "You thought that if there was such a weapon as I suggested, it must be something like the Carhart one, something small and easily concealed." "I thought it must be a snake," confessed Bland. "You mean, then, that the needles shot out from the back of the lion's throat ?” "And returned automatically," nodded Cameron. "Those curved needles were in themselves part of the spring, you might say. Something like a piece of steel, that, released from tension, flies out and back again. The general direction of the blow, owing not merely to the arc of trajectory but the curve of the needles, was downward. The artificial poison sac was compressed synchronously and automatically when the spring was released. And the trigger which set the whole thing in motion was one of those buttons, to the right of the lion's head, that studded the back. To set or cock the chair you had to remove part of the upper back. Necessary also, of course, when refilling the poison reservoir. "But I think that little rubber bulb held a solution of all the dried venom stolen from Vornoff, and that's why we could find no trace of it in Macford's flat. 

And I think it was all expended on those two victims, for otherwise we might have had the opportunity of adding this unique exhibit to our Black Museum specimens." "You mean," said Bland, "that Macford wouldn't have wrecked the thing?" "I think not, even though he saw the danger of keeping it intact. And I'm quite sure that if he hadn't believed I was still a very sick man, confined to the hospital, he would have managed to complete the work

of destruction. We shouldn't have found a shred of evidence. But he thought he had plenty of time. "An artist hates to destroy his work, especially a masterpiece; Donald Macford was an artist in his way, just as that chair is as much a masterpiece in its way as Van Gogh's. I think that what reconciled him to its destruction was the fact that its days of usefulness were over. In other words, the black mamba venom was done, he couldn't get any more, and he daren't use any other poison if he was to maintain this admirable snake theory he had built up. "You remember that first night in Devore's? I told you I hadn't forgotten about the cord of the portiere's. Now, whereas you and I, Jerry, passed those curtains without incident, Macford goes out of his way to have the cord touch his neck so that he can adroitly support the snake theory. An illuminating incident, one of many." "You think," said Corrigan, "that if there'd been any more poison left in the infernal thing he'd have kept it to use on someone else?" "Quite possible. Even if the reservoir wasn't empty, he couldn't use it on Hogan, or anybody. The chair was harmless, of course, so long as he hadn't a chance to set it. But once in his possession again he would have been tempted to use it a third time if there had been any more poison. And I think his next victim would have been Doctor Golding." "Golding!" "Yes. That the doctor has survived is due possibly to the fact that, unlike Devore and Meier, there was no easy and infallible way of getting him to use the chair. 

I say possiby, because you must remember that it's

only recently that Golding has become so intimate with Miss Camblin.” "Do you mean" began Bland and Corrigan, as one man. "Yes, that was the the great-hearted one. No, she wasn't party to the murders, and in law she isn't even an accessory after the fact. But does that make her less culpable? 

Who is it that inspires crime? Do men commit murder for good women? Search the pages of history." motive. Our little Madeline, "But we haven't a thing on her !" exclaimed Corrigan. "Nor Mrs. Royal, either. There's only that old breach of promise case" "I know," interrupted Cameron. "In the eyes of the world they are good women, and perhaps even in their own eyes. But not in mine. There are virtually two kinds of women in the world—those who give all and expect little, and those who give little and expect all. 

Even the woman of the streets tries to give value for payment, but Miss Camblin? No, she's virtuous, respectable. "No, we've nothing on them, as you say. And they would sue us for libel, and what not, if we even hinted that Miss Camblin, in the last analysis, was responsible for these murders. Yes, and they'd win. I don't intend to offer them still another way of adding to their questionable income. We can prove nothing, and we dare say nothing. But I say now that Miss 

Gower was right, the janitor was right, the hall-boyeverybody who said or hinted that Miss Camblin was at the bottom of it all. And," finished Cameron, "I myself was right." "But there's absolutely nothing to show that Macford was in love with her !" protested Bland.

"Love?" echoed Cameron. "What has love to do with crimes of passion? Macford coveted her precisely as Devore and Meier and Golding-yes and even the hall-boy. Sex, sex! The girl knows her onions, as they say, and with the help of her aunt has made a very good and safe thing out of it. Oh, she can't help being friendly and sympathetic; she can't understand why men like her so much. But she can't even resist experimenting with the good-looking hall-boy; everything presentable in pants must be brought under the spell of Circe for the sake of gain or her vanity. 

And it is all so astounding, poor innocent ! "Proof?" he continued. "Haven't we got Macford's own words that he gave her a good time? Consider his age, his antecedents, his veiled threat of what he would do to the hall-boy, if Jones was guilty of what he suspected-in other words, daring to raise his eyes to Miss Camblin. "Yes, Macford was madly in love with her, if you call it love. Of course he pretended it was only friendship, and that his association with her decreased voluntarily. 

But the real truth is that Devore and Meier had become his successful rivals. Cast your mind back to the night of Devore's death-the picture of Macford leaning over the foot of the bed. Did you note that look, Jerry? It's the keynote of the whole thing. 

Do you remember what he said?-'And he was such a handsome man'. There spoke the victor, triumphant, gloating over a hated rival's disfigurement and death." "That idea never occurred to me. I merely thought he was sorry," said the bewildered Bland. "I wasn't looking at him at the time. This is staggering about 

Macford, but I can't say that the light you throw on 

Miss Camblin is altogether a new one.”

"I guess not," nodded Corrigan. "The more you think about it, the more likely it seems. Still, it's a theory that can't be proved?" "It's been proved already by the murder of Devore and Meier," retorted Cameron. "What other motive could there be? None. And the psychology of the thing is perfect. If Macford had possessed Miss

Camblin, and she had left him for these rivals, his first action would have been to revenge himself on her. But he didn't possess her; none of them did. But each thought he would, so cleverly did she string them along Golding was a negligible factor at the time, and with Devore and Meier out of the way, Macford thought success was assured. He refused to see that she'd simply thrown him over for bigger game, and that she was essentially a gold-digger who meant to take all and give nothing. "I believe Macford invented those four women who were supposed to have visited Devore; otherwise we'd have got some trace of them. Naturally it was his game, when he learned Devore was Parker Dinsmore and that he'd a reputation for philandering, to make him out worse than he was and throw suspicion on some unknown woman. "It's possible that Meier was blackmailed by Mrs. 

Royal and her niece. It's possible that one or both suspected, if they didn't actually know, the real explanation of Mrs. Meier's disappearance. I believe they knew 'Meier before he or they came to the Abercorn 

Arms. I think that may be the real reason, or the deciding factor, why he made a will in Miss Camblin's favour. Who knows all that they got out of him and Devore and Macford? "Yes, virtuous, respectable women," concluded

Cameron with a bitter smile. "There are far more in the world like them than people imagine. Too clever to come within reach of the law. Yes, there are many 

Madeline Camblins. I see in to-day's paper that one I knew has been caught at last in Brussels for 'fraud by marriage'. During a period of twenty-five years that damsel 'married' more than fifty men and became ‘engaged' to over seven hundred. Incredible, isn't it?

She lived on the fat of the land. Well, I think we'll find, if we can ever trace the subterranean history of Mrs. Royal and her niece, that they have also managed to live on the fat of the land, by much the same means And no doubt the legal acumen of Mr. Gloz has had something to do with their success. "To sum up, Feigel said that Miss Camblin's advent was like a Tabby coming into a home of Toms; if vulgar, that's a true explanation of the mystery of the human bookcase. You were looking for something complex, like the gentlemen of the Sûreté in that case I mentioned. Certain features, apart from the unique, weapon used, made it apparently an insoluble complexity. And what was it? Actually nothing but a very simple and sordid affair with a motive as old as mankind. Nothing but a crime passionel. No, there's nothing new in crime." "But how did you know all this?" demanded Corrigan. "What has this explanation got to do with the Ides of 

March ?" "Yes, and a comma?" said Bland. "And Meier's clothes, the woman you saw in the hospital, the one called Anna, and Red Rum Myer? There are some things you've explained, reasonably and legitimately, but not all. My list of questions is still formidable." "I've done all the explaining I intend to do for the

present, and I don't think I've done so badly for my first day's convalescence," said Cameron wearily. "If you meet me in Devore's flat to-morrow, I'll answer every question on your list, and explain how Devore met his death. Until then I refuse to discuss the subject further."

CHAPTER XXVII CAMERON EXPLAINS 

"Now," said Cameron, the following day, when they were in Devore's flat, "let us take the first question on the list: Why was the light burning in the kitchenette and nowhere else? Surely the answer to that is now evident ?" "You mean," said Bland, "that Macford carried

Devore's remains into the kitchenette, because if they were found here it might lead to the chair being suspected ?" "He had time to "Exactly," nodded Cameron. remove the body, but not the chair. Even if there had been time, he daren't risk it. If a tenant came in and saw him carrying the chair-you see? He could only attempt it in the middle of the night. "Now that answers another question on your list- Why was it important where Devore happened to be when he was struck, and of no importance regarding 

Meier? Devore died in or near that chair." And

Cameron pointed to the harmless replica that stood in its accustomed place by the desk. "Obvious now," he continued, "that Devore must have been sitting in the chair, and therefore the light would have been lit here. As you say, Macford wanted to remove all possible suspicion from the chair; wanted it to appear that Devore had been killed somewhere else, and so he removed him as far from it as 272

CAMERON EXPLAINS 

273 possible-in fact, the whole length of the flat. He wished it to appear that Devore was in the kitchenette, and so he turned on the light there and turned it out here. "Now that's where he made his first vital mistake.

It didn't occur to him that Devore's first action on entering this room would be to switch on the light, and that he wouldn't turn it out when, as an analysis of the evidence shows, he had no intention of remaining in the kitchenette for any length of time. He merely stayed there long enough to take the wine-bottles out of the box." "You were the only one who saw that," said Corrigan. "It means then, of course, that Macford came in here before the janitor?" "Yes, between the time he left the antique shop and the time Feigel called him. Quite true that there wasn't time for him to have killed Devore with his own hands, but there was time for him to do what he wanted to do. And he wanted to do four things. "Now, you'll remember," pursued Cameron, "the question I raised about that door being on the latch. 

Macford wished it to appear that Garwinn's expected arrival caused Devore to put it on the latch. But it was the superintendent himself who did it. You remember him first denying he had done so, after the arrival of Doctor Golding and Keppel, and then saying he must have put it on the latch unconsciously.

But I'll return to this matter of the door later. It's very important."

Cameron then went on to explain how the absence of the janitor and the hall-boy, rather than being a coincidence, had been part of Macford's plan. "He had decided to attempt the murder that night, and he had timed everything carefully," said Cameron.

"He knew Devore's habits, and the chair had been substituted and set. He knew that the janitor might hear a cry, or the sound of a fall, and so he sends him up to fix a light which he himself has broken. He has seen to it also that the faucet in the bathroom needs fixing. I doubt that Devore spoke to him about it; if he did, it was coincidence. Macford had planned to send the hall-boy up with the message, not only that Jones should be out of the way, but that Feigel should discover the crime when he did. It was essential to his plan that the discovery should happen as soon as possible, so that he could prove he was in the antique shop when it happened. Now is everything clear and reasonable so far?" "Quite," said Bland, while Corrigan nodded. "He knew, of course, it was Devore's habit to sit in that chair, and it was simply a question of how long it would take him to lean back and press the button that released the spring." "Yes. And that button being where it was, he could only press it if he leaned back." "Supposing he'd happened to press it with his hand before he sat down?" suggested Corrigan. "Well, that was a risk Macford had to take," replied 

Cameron. "Something like that might have happened, but all the odds were that it wouldn't. Devore's natural action would be to sit down and read the evening paper, or correct typescript, as was his custom. 

He'd no occasion to pass his hand over the back of the chair, though he might have done so. "Now I believe that Macford's reason for coming in was more than the removal of the body. In order to prevent Devore calling for help, if he was able, I'm sure he'd put the telephone out of order."

CAMERON EXPLAINS 

275 "So that's why you asked Keppel if he'd 'phoned from the hall or here ?" asked Bland. "Yes. Naturally, if we'd found that the 'phone wasn't working, we'd have asked certain awkward questions, and so Macford fixed it before leaving.

He was an expert mechanic, and, moreover, he'd at least five minutes. "That, then, was his second reason for coming in, and

I believe there was a third. I believe he wanted to see Devore suffering, dying. I think there can be no question that at the time he hated him more than Meier." "Why?" "Because Devore, with his good looks and personality, was the chief rival. He was an attractive man to women, apart from his money. It wasn't until after Devore's death that Meier became formidable, just as Golding became prominent after Meier's death. 

The irony of it is that Macford, instead of clearing a path for himself, cleared it for these others. But let us now leave Devore for the time being and consider the case of Herman Meier."

As Cameron had said, and now reiterated, Meier had only become formidable through Devore's death. 

It seemed reasonably certain that, at the start, Macford had contemplated only the one murder. Then, with

Devore out of the way, he found that Miss Camblin was becoming more and more taken up with Meier, instead of with himself. The wholesale fruiterer, if he had no good looks or charming personality, had more money than Devore. "It's no wonder," said Cameron, "that, seeing the great success of his first crime, Macford was tempted to try a second. But in this instance he must chance

Meier being found near the chair; must chance him. even calling for help, for he couldn't enter the flat. 

It was no longer a case of stepping across the hall, but of going up to the second floor. He must rely on the fact that the weapon wasn't suspected, while he furnished even a better alibi than in the other case. 

Now he could be actually miles away from the premises." "But," said Bland, "how about the great difference in height between Devore and Meier? What would hit Devore in the neck would go over Meier's head. 

Didn't that occur to Macford ?" "No, the truth occurred to him," said Cameron with a smile. "He was a closer observer than you, Jerry. And the truth is that, while Meier was almost a foot shorter than Devore, when they were standing, they were practically the same height when seated. 

Most people are. Devore's extra height came from long legs." "I see," said the District Attorney in a chastened voice. "So far as Macford could possibly know," pursued 

Cameron, "Meier would sit in that chair and meet his death precisely like Devore. Macford could have no means of knowing that it was Meier's nightly custom to do what he did. That was a piece of luck, like 

Meier drinking more when he came home; that he couldn't have reckoned on. All he knew was that 

Meier used the chair, that he came home at a certain hour, and that in all probability he would have had several drinks. "You understand then what happened? Perhaps

Meier actually sat in the chair while undressing, but he didn't lean back. Then he turns out the light and goes to open the window, his customary last action

CAMERON EXPLAINS 

277 before getting into bed. He opens the lower half, then stands on the chair to push down the upper half. 

Not only is the window somewhat hard to move, but he's drunk. In his fumbling efforts to lower it, his thighs press against that button, the needles flash out, bury themselves, recoil. It's dark, he's drunk, and the only explanation he can think of is that he's been bitten by the same snake that killed Devore." "Well, he wasn't so far wrong at that," commented 

Corrigan after a silence. "But what did you see about his clothes that we couldn't ?” "I saw, you all saw, the night we first entered the room, that some of his clothes were hanging over the back of the chair. Therefore they concealed the lion's head, and therefore I didn't realize the significance of that chair. I didn't see that it was exactly the same as the one in Devore's flat. But even if I had, I shouldn't have realized the truth at that time." "But look here," said Bland. "If the clothes were over the back-and I know some of them were-how could Meier have been struck? They'd have blanketed the needles." "Precisely the question I asked myself later," said 

Cameron. "And so I questioned Feigel. It was he who put them there. They were lying on the floor, where, quite evidently, Meier had knocked them after being struck. It's evident also that originally all the clothes were on the chair, that is, on the seat. Feigel's action, when the light was lit, was quite natural and instinctive. He thought it of so little importance that he said nothing about it. No doubt he'd forgotten the incident until I questioned him.” "Well, who was the woman you saw in the hospital ?”’ asked Bland.

"I haven't the slightest idea," replied Cameron. "Let us go back to that day, the day Paul Garwinn died. What did I see while we were waiting for the elevator? I saw a woman, a window, and a chair grouped together at the end of the corridor. I told you in so many words that it didn't matter who the woman was, nor did it. All that mattered was the grouping, the association of ideas. In a flash it came to me how Meier could have been struck in the leg, Devore the neck. And so I came here, not to see

Miss Camblin, but to examine more closely that chair in Meier's bedroom." "But you didn't examine it while I was there!" protested Bland. "Oh, yes, I did-first with my eyes alone," smiled 

Cameron. "Meier's clothes had been removed, and 

I saw enough to satisfy me that my theory was right. 

And, when you were looking somewhere else, I slipped a pencil in that lion's mouth and found, as I expected, that there was no back to the throat. It proved that that part of the chair was hollow." "But what about that warning message found in 

Meier's flat?" asked Corrigan. "Why was that message printed, while the one we found in Devore's flat was written ?" countered 

Cameron. "Because writing, even if disguised, may be identified. Macford knew that, and he was taking no chances." "You mean he put the paper there for us to find ?”' "Of course.

He hid it when he substituted the chair, hid it where Meier wouldn't find it, but we must. As you said to Varcoe, it was bound to be found sooner or later when we searched the flat. Of course he put the message there, so we'd think naturally

CAMERON EXPLAINS 

279 that Meier's death had also something to do with bootlegging." "But if he didn't write the other one," said Bland, "who did ?" "Devore, of course." 

"But why? What have the Ides of March got to do with it?" "Everything," replied Cameron. "As I suggested long ago, that's the pivotal point of the whole case." "How? And look here, you said that Red Rum 

Myer was the murderer.” "I still say so. 

Have you brought the original message, as I told you to? Well, let me see it, and 

I'll explain."

CHAPTER XXVIII  

THE District Attorney, exchanging a look of baffled wonder with Corrigan, produced the original paper found on Devore's desk, bearing the pencilled message: 

Warning. Red Rum Myer, Ides of March. 

"Now," said Cameron, "we've noted already two peculiarities about this message: firstly, that it's like, yet unlike, Devore's writing; and, secondly, that it's written in pencil. If Devore wrote it, why should he use pencil, when he always used ink? And why should it be different from his ordinary hand?” "I've thought that if he wasn't himself, if he was drunk-" began Bland, and suddenly struck a hand on the desk. "I've got it! You mean supposing he wrote it after he'd been poisoned ?" "Good!" approved Cameron. "And would a man, who knew his minutes were numbered, select pen and ink if a pencil was equally handy? But there's another peculiarity about that message; you don't seem to have noticed it, but, I assure you, it gave me a lot of thought. Why should there be a comma instead of a period after the name Myer?" "I never noticed that !" exclaimed Bland. “Or, if

I did, I didn't think anything about it. So that's what you meant by your talk of a comma ?" "That's what I meant," nodded Cameron. "Why 280

should it be a comma instead of a period? Was it a slip of the pencil? Was it meant for a period, or was there a logical reason why a comma should be used? 

That was my first clue. I told you, Jerry, that an idea came to me in the night. You preferred to believe that 

I only got it when I heard about Meier's will. But I told you the truth. I sat up all the previous night puzzling over this question of the comma, and I finally solved the secret of this message." "But you said it wasn't in code !" exclaimed Bland. "Nor is it, otherwise more experts than I would have succeeded in decoding it. But it's something they or 

I weren't looking for-an anagram." "A what?" asked Corrigan. "Anagram," repeated Cameron. "You see now who 

Anna is? That stenographer, of course, spelt it with an extra 'n', because he thought it was a woman's name. And he didn't catch the rest of it. That's the hint I was evidently trying to give-that the Ides of 

March was an anagram." "But what is an anagram ?" demanded Corrigan. "According to the dictionary it's the letters of a word or phrase so transposed as to make a different word or phrase," explained the District Attorney, as he stared with new eyes at the paper. "And they're darned hard things to solve, even if you know what to look for." "But not hard to make," said Cameron. "Nor hard to solve by an expert. That's the point that concerns us. Anybody can make an anagram, if everybody can't solve one." "What's the message?" demanded the excited 

Bland. "I can't make anything out of it." "You've had no practice," said Cameron, "and it

would take you even longer than it took me. Now let us begin with the first word 'Warning'," he added as he took out a pencil. "We'll cross out G and put it down here for the first letter. Next we'll take A 

Then comes R, then W- "Why, it spells Garwinn !" cried Corrigan. "Yes, a message to Paul Garwinn," said Cameron. "And why? Because he's Devore's closest friend, and 

Devore's expecting a visit from him. And what is this message? What is 'Red Rum Myer' but the anagram of 'my murderer'? And the Ides of Marchbut here's a translation of the whole thing." 

Cameron, swiftly crossing out one letter after another and putting them down in a new formation, wrote the following: 

Garwinn. My murderer, he is Macford. "Well, I'm damned!" exclaimed Corrigan. “I'm certainly damned !” "You see now the significance of that comma?" said Cameron. "And you understand now what I wanted to ask Paul Garwinn? I'm certain that Devore and he were interested in more than chess, that they were thoroughly familiar with the making and solving of anagrams. Why, an anagram figures in one of 

Devore's books! Perhaps Garwinn helped in the making of it. That both were of a mathematical turn of mind is beyond question. "Now you see why that incident in the hospital led me to think of the chair. I've shown why I'd some reason to suspect Macford from the start, and these suspicions being verified, the question naturally arose -How could Macford have killed Devore and yet been

in the shop? Obviously by proxy. 

The weapon must be something that acted independently of him. 

And so, wondering what this weapon could possibly be, the whole thing flashed on me when I saw the woman standing by the chair at the window. Nothing miraculous, you see; merely the subconscious mind and the logical association of ideas." "Well," said Bland at length, still staring at the paper as though fascinated, "this is the biggest kick in a case that's kept me jumping from the start.

I thought there could be no more surprises left in the bag. What gets me is the fact that Devore conceived and wrote that thing, not only in such circumstances, but in such a short time." "It couldn't be done," said Corrigan emphatically. 

Cameron smiled. "Throughout the pages of human history, Dan, you'll find that things which couldn't be done, have been done. There was no such word as 'couldn't' in 

Henry Devore's vocabulary. He believed in the old saying, that the man who thinks he can is always right." "Still it seems impossible," protested Bland. "You've never given Devore credit for being the man he was," said Cameron earnestly. "He vindicated his character, and my opinion of it, by the manner of his death. He had not only brains, but courage and determination. You called his literary success a freak of chance. Well, undoubtedly chance had something to do with it, as it has something to do with everything. But that doesn't wipe out the fact that for years he fought a dogged fight against odds and finally won out. That has nothing to do with his philandering, and we've seen what a harmless enough weakness that

was. On the whole, Miss Gower had a true conception of the man. A bohemian, a philanderer, yes, but also a fighter. Don't forget that. "Now you may say that this message is susceptible of many a different translation than the one I made. 

I don't deny that. You can transpose those letters in many different ways; but would it make a phrase, or phrases, of equal sense, using all the letters? And would it furnish an equally logical reason for its being written ?" "I don't doubt that Devore wrote it," said Bland, "and that you've made the right, and perhaps only possible, transposition. I don't think there's any getting away from that. I'm only amazed that he could, and did, do it." "Yes, and yet not so amazing when you consider all the circumstances and the man," replied Cameron. "A quick-thinking, mathematical brain, remember. 

An intuitive, receptive mind. And what did I note the first time I saw him? I noted that combative chin and determined mouth." "But why did he trouble to make an anagram out of it?" asked the District Attorney. "Why didn't he write a plain message to Garwinn? Wouldn't that be the natural thing to do?" "No, most unnatural in the circumstances. He saw that it must be a cryptic message or nothing. What would be the use of leaving a message that his murderer could read and destroy? He knew that Macford would be the first to enter the flat." "How did he know ?" asked Bland. And Corrigan said: "Why didn't he go out into the hall for help? 

Wouldn't that be the first thing anybody would do?

If he could write that message, or try to 'phone,

he could have walked across the room to the door." "But the door was locked," said Cameron quietly. "That answers the question of how Devore knew that Macford would be the first to come in. He knew it must have been locked by the superintendent, the only person with a duplicate key, and that Macford would have to unlock it before the crime was discovered. 

It was not only locked from the outside, but the key had been left so that Devore couldn't use his." "But how could it be locked when Feigel found it on the latch?" demanded Corrigan.

Cameron raised his eyes to the ceiling in mute protest. "Haven't I said that Macford put it on the latch?

Don't you imagine he knew that Devore would try to leave the room, just as he knew he would try to telephone? He locked the door after Jones had gone to the elevator, and Devore had gone into the kitchenette with the box. "I told you I'd return to this matter of the door, that it was very important. That was the fourth reason Macford had for coming in-to put it on the latch. He had two reasons for doing so he wanted 

Feigel to open the door without coming to him, and it widened the field of suspicion indefinitely. It proved how easily anyone might have entered the flat. We've proof that the door must have been locked, for otherwise Devore would have managed to leave the room. "Now let us see what must have happened. Unlike

Meier's murder, this one took place in the light. 

Devore not only knew it was the chair, but that nobody could make and substitute such a weapon but Macford. That's an essential point to remember.

He would see that at once, precisely as we saw it.

Also you may be sure that he knew Macford was his rival; for all we know they may have quarrelled about Miss Camblin, just as Devore and Meier quarrelled about her. Moreover, there's the locked door. So

Devore knew how he had been given his death wound, why, and by whom. "What does he do? We don't know at what precise moment he was struck, but he must have had from ten to twenty minutes in which to think, in which to force his hand to write, if finally he can't force his legs to walk. Does he cry out? If so, there's none to hear. Does he go to the door? If so, he finds it locked. Does he try to use the 'phone? If so, he can't get any answer. "He is trapped, and his naturally quick mind works even more quickly in the face of imminent and inevitable death. What is his dominant thought? 

What would your dominant thought and wish be if you knew you were doomed? Why, that the murderer should be known. If he must die, Macford mustn't escape. The author of this foul deed must be brought to justice. "Now that's the situation that faced Henry Devore," continued Cameron solemnly. "He realized that not only must he leave a message, but one that Macford could see no reason to destroy. In fact, if it was properly worded, Macford might see to its preservation. Paul Garwinn would arrive inside an hour, and 

Garwinn was familiar with cryptic writing. "Here, then, we have a man, an expert at making anagrams, and we may be sure that both Garwinn and he were familiar with the anagram of Garwinn's name.

Devore wouldn't have to think about turning it into the word 'Warning'. Then, facing him on the desk,

there is that quotation from Julius Cæsar. Ides of 

March! Why, the phrase in itself spells Macford's name, and the words 'he is'! What more obvious than that 'my murderer' should follow? "And so Devore pulls himself together for his last creative effort, his last and his greatest. What better way to attract Garwinn's attention than the anagram of his own name? What better way of ensuring that

Macford won't destroy the message than by making it appear as though it were connected with a story he's writing, and that Macford will use as a false clue for the police to sniff? The irony of it is that Macford, in writing that other message, repeated the accusation against himself. "Now," concluded Cameron, "reviewing all the circumstances, I don't think you can say any longer that the thing was impossible or even improbable. 

At any rate, it was actually done, for that's the only possible explanation of the matter. Macford not only read his own death warrant, but perpetuated it, while thinking all the time what a piece of luck it was that 

Devore should have happened to write it. And if 

Paul Garwinn had come here, as he intended, instead of going to the hospital and then to his grave, we should have had a solution long ago. There's no question of that. "Now have I given a reasonable and legitimate answer to everything? I think the only thing you may take exception to is the fact that I said Red 

Rum Myer was the murderer instead of my murderer. 

But are there any more questions you'd like to ask?" "Yes," said Bland slowly as he folded up the paper, "I'd like to ask where you get your brand of brains. 

I take my hat off to Henry Devore and you. I'll say

he was a great character, nor are you a police commissioner or criminologist. You're a genius, old man." "Didn't I always say there was nobody like him ?” demanded Corrigan.
